## Milestone 6: Authentication

**Goal:** The complete auth lifecycle exactly as the frontend mock defines it (API §1).

- [x] **M6.1** — `POST /auth/register`: signup validation mirroring `features/auth/schemas/signup.ts`; 409 `email_taken`; consent timestamps; verification email (single-use token, TTL 24 h) via mailhog.
  - ✅ Verify: register → 201 `{ nextStep: "verify-email" }`; duplicate → 409; mailhog shows the email.
  - **Verified — 22 e2e cases against a booted application and a real database.** Register → **201** `{"data":{"nextStep":"verify-email","email":"…"}}`; duplicate → **409 `email_taken`** with `detail`; a differently-cased address is the same account (citext, DB §A4). **The email is proven delivered, not constructed:** the harness overrides `MAILER` with a real SMTP client pointed at an in-process SMTP server, so the assertions are about a message that crossed a socket — mailhog needs a Docker daemon this environment does not have (M1's gate note), and what the item wants checked is that a message reaches a relay at all. The link lands on the frontend route (`/verify-email?token=…`), the token is stored **hashed** and expires in 24 h, and both consent columns are stamped. **Uniqueness is guarded twice and needs to be:** the pre-check answers the ordinary case with the contract's 409, and the unique index is what makes the answer correct when two signups for one address arrive together — a concurrency case that is tested, and that surfaced a real trap. Prisma 7 talks to Postgres through a driver adapter (ADR-021), and the adapter reports P2002 as `meta.driverAdapterError.cause.constraint.fields`, not the `meta.target` every example shows; a catch written the documented way never matches, and the 409 becomes a 500 under exactly the concurrent conditions it was written for. The reader lives in `shared/database` and reads both shapes. **No `doctors` row is created** — signup collects a licence number and neither specialty nor department, both NOT NULL — so the licence number goes to `users.license_number` and M9 creates the professional profile. `confirmPassword` is accepted and ignored (API §1.1); both consent flags are `z.literal(true)`, since `terms_accepted_at` is NOT NULL and `false` is the one way an account with no recorded consent could exist. Self-registration as an administrator is what the frontend's form offers, so it is what the endpoint accepts — RA-025.1 carries the invitation question to M8.2.
- [x] **M6.2** — `POST /auth/login`: Argon2id check, `email_unverified` block, lockout vs `loginAttemptLimit`, `login_events` row per attempt, session row + access JWT (≤15 min: sub/role/permissions/sid) + refresh cookie (`httpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict`; rememberMe 30 d / 12 h), `singleSessionPerUser` honored; response = `{ user: SessionUser, accessToken, expiresIn }`.
  - ✅ Verify: API tests cover every branch of the mock service (success, invalid_credentials, email_unverified, lockout→429). **Each failure carries the `code` the frontend branches on** (`frontend/src/features/auth/mock/types.ts` lists all eight).
  - **Verified — 29 e2e cases, one `describe` per branch of `mock-auth-service.login()`.** Success returns the exact `SessionUser` shape (`id` is the `usr_…` public id, `name` the display name, `permissions` the seeded doctor bundle — `patients.view`, `prescriptions.create`, `ai.use`, and none of the administration capabilities), `expiresIn` 900, and a cookie carrying `HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict; Path=/api/v1/auth` with `Max-Age` 2592000 or 43200 per `rememberMe`. The JWT carries `sub` (uuid), `pid` (public id), `role`, `permissions` and `sid`, with `exp - iat` exactly 900. **The order of the checks is the contract, and two placements are tested because they would be easy to get wrong:** lockout is checked *before* the password, so a locked account costs a 429 rather than an Argon2id verification (asserted by the counter not climbing while locked); `email_unverified` is checked *after* it, so a wrong password against an unverified account is `invalid_credentials` and not a way to discover which addresses have unverified accounts. An unknown address is verified against a **decoy hash** before being refused, and the two 401 bodies are asserted byte-identical. Lockout: five failures → **429** with `Retry-After`, the correct password refused while it holds, the lock expiring, and `loginAttemptLimit: 2` honoured when lowered **through `PLATFORM_POLICY_SOURCE`** — the seam M24 will fill, exercised the way an administrator will use it rather than by restarting with a different environment. `singleSessionPerUser: true` revokes the others through the same seam. Every attempt writes `login_events`, including one against an address with no account (`user_id` null, `attempted_email` recorded, failure code per attempt: four `invalid_credentials` then `rate_limited`). The 2FA branch returns `{ requiresOtp: true, otpToken }` with **no cookie and no session row** (API §1.2 — "no session yet"). **The global guard inverts the default:** every route requires a token unless it is `@Public()`, so a route added by a later milestone is protected by construction; health says so, and is asserted still reachable. **A latent kernel defect surfaced here and is fixed:** `withSoftDelete` filtered `$allModels` unconditionally, but only 43 of the 107 models carry `deletedAt` — adding `deletedAt: null` to a `where` for one of the other 64 is an invalid query, not a stricter one, and every successful login 500ed. The extension now narrows per model from the client's own field metadata; three cases in the M4 spec pin it, including the regression.
- [x] **M6.3** — `POST /auth/refresh` (rotate; reuse detection revokes family; sliding expiry) + `POST /auth/logout` (revoke + clear cookie).
  - ✅ Verify: reuse of a rotated token → 401 and the whole family revoked (test). Cookie `Path` matches the M0.6-1 ADR and is actually sent by the browser.
  - **Verified — 22 e2e cases, and one schema addition that this item made necessary.** **Rotation alone cannot detect reuse:** once `refresh_token_hash` is overwritten a replayed token matches no row and is indistinguishable from a random string, so the detection M5.2's own model comment promises was not expressible in the schema as transcribed. One nullable column closes it — `previous_token_hash`, keeping a single generation back (migration `20260826190000_m6_3_session_rotation`; **ADR-026** records the design and the trade). The canonical theft is tested end to end: the attacker refreshes with the stolen token, the real client presents the one it still holds, that presentation is recognised as a replay, the session is revoked, and **the attacker's new token stops working too**. A token that merely expired does *not* trigger it — revoking on expiry would make every returning user look like an incident. The row stays the family, so a replay on one device leaves the user's other sessions alive (tested); ADR-026 explains why the alternative costs ~2,880 rows per remembered user per month on a table nothing prunes. Rotation is conditional on the presented hash and decided by the row count, so two concurrent refreshes cannot both rotate. **The cookie's scoping is verified by what a client returns, not by what the server offers:** the real `Set-Cookie` header is fed to `cookiejar` — the library superagent itself uses — which confirms it goes back to `/api/v1/auth/refresh` and `/logout`, is **not** attached to `/api/v1/patients`, `/api/v1/health` or `/`, and is withheld from an insecure connection. That is ADR-011's actual failure mode: a mis-scoped cookie is set successfully and then never sent again. Supertest's own agent cannot answer this over plain HTTP, which is why the jar is driven directly. Logout answers **204**, revokes only the session the token names, and clears the cookie with the attributes it was set with (a `clearCookie` missing `path` leaves the user holding a refresh token). API §1.5's **mobile variant** is implemented: `X-Client-Type: mobile` reads the token from the body, but only as a fallback — the cookie wins whenever one is present, so a header an attacker sets cannot talk a browser into a body token. **Two lookups moved from `findUnique` to `findFirst`, which is a fix rather than a style change:** the soft-delete extension cannot filter `findUnique`, so a deactivated account (M8.2 deactivates by soft delete) could still sign in and still refresh.
- [x] **M6.4** — 2FA: TOTP enrolment (`/me/2fa/setup|enable|disable` — QR, encrypted secret, recovery codes) + login step-up (`requiresOtp` + `otpToken` → `/auth/otp/verify`, 6-digit, bounded attempts, `/auth/otp/resend` rate-limited).
  - ✅ Verify: full 2FA login round-trip with an authenticator lib; wrong code → 401 `otp_invalid`; abuse → 429.
  - **Verified — 22 e2e cases, the round trip driven by `otpauth` acting as the phone.** The library is fed the secret `/me/2fa/setup` handed out, which is exactly what a scanned QR gives an authenticator; a hand-rolled expected code would only prove the implementation agrees with itself. `setup` returns API §2.9's `{ otpauthUrl, secret, recoveryCodes }` — the URL carries `issuer=Medora`, the account's address as the label, and `algorithm=SHA1&digits=6&period=30` stated rather than defaulted, because changing any of them silently invalidates every enrolled device. **Enrolment is two calls and must be:** `setup` stores the secret, `enable` flips the flag and only after a code proves the round trip — doing both at once is how a mis-scanned QR locks someone out of their own account, with the flag on and the phone holding a different secret. Tested: after `setup` the flag is still false and password-only sign-in still works. The secret is **encrypted, not hashed** (the one place in M6 where that is true — the server must re-derive the same six digits), AES-256-GCM into the `bytea` column DB §10 named for it; the stored ciphertext is asserted not to contain the base32 secret in any encoding. `disable` takes the **password**, not a code — someone holding the phone but not the password is exactly who must not remove the factor — and revokes every session, clearing the secret and recovery codes so a later `enable` cannot silently reactivate a factor the user believes they removed. The step-up creates its session through the **same** `establishSession` the password-only path uses, so the two are indistinguishable; `rememberMe` is carried in the challenge token, since API §1.3's body is `{ otpToken, code }` and the flag is never sent again (tested by asserting `sessions.remember_me`). The challenge is single-use — the same token and a still-valid code are refused on the second attempt — and an **access token presented as a challenge token is rejected**, which without the `typ` check would skip the second factor entirely, both being signed with the same key. Wrong code → **401 `otp_invalid`** with the attempt counted; a lowered `loginAttemptLimit: 3` gives `[401, 401, 429, 401]`. Resend is 429 inside the frontend's own 30-second countdown, extends the window once it has passed, and **does not reset the attempt count** — otherwise it would be an unlimited-guesses button. With an authenticator there is nothing to send again; ADR-025 records what the endpoint does instead and why. Both the user-facing activity feed (DB §A12) and the audit ledger get a row: one is what the account owner sees, the other what an administrator investigates. Recovery codes are generated, shown once and stored encrypted; **no endpoint consumes one** — the contract has no route that accepts one — carried as RA-025.2 to M8.
  - **Recovery codes redeemable, hashed, and re-enrollment hardened (2026-11-06; owner decisions R1–R5, Q7 / R4; closes the gap RA-025.2 / RA-035.2 record — the ADR text itself is not edited here).** `POST /auth/otp/recovery` completes a pending step-up with one recovery code: the challenge, the code and a transactional audit row (`auth.recovery_code_used`) are spent together under a row lock, so concurrent redemptions yield one session and an unwritable audit row refuses the redemption (503). The account holder is mailed on every use; the remaining count is served only by the new `GET /me/2fa`. Codes are now stored as HMAC-SHA-256 digests (key derived from `DATA_ENCRYPTION_KEY` by HKDF) inside the existing `recovery_codes_enc` envelope — no migration; rows in the old format are read as before and rewritten in the new one when the codes next change. `POST /me/2fa/setup` now refuses an account that already has a factor (409): before this a session alone could replace a live factor. Replacing one is `POST /me/2fa/reenroll` (current password + current code) then `/reenroll/confirm` (a code from the new secret), after which the factor and codes are replaced atomically. `test/auth-recovery-code.e2e-spec.ts`, `recovery-code.service.spec.ts`. **Not built:** the client affordances (web and patient app), and the separately approved recovery process for a user whose current factor is unavailable (owner clarification C-1).
- [x] **M6.5** — Password flows: forgot (always 202, anti-enumeration), reset (single-use token, policy, revokes sessions), change (`currentPassword` + policy + differs-from-current).
  - ✅ Verify: forgot for unknown email returns identical 202; reset with used token → 422 `token_invalid`.
  - **Verified — inside the 30 cases shared with M6.6.** `forgot` answers **202 `{"accepted":true}`** for a real address, an unknown one, a deactivated account and one inside the cooldown — the responses are asserted **byte-identical**, and the cooldown is enforced by *not sending*, never by answering differently, because a 429 there would leak precisely what the 202 exists to hide. Reset: 204, the new password works and the old one does not; a **used** token → **422 `token_invalid`**, as do an expired one, an unknown one, and a *verification* token presented as a reset token — both kinds live in `verification_tokens` and only `kind` separates them, so without that check a link that merely proves an address would be a password reset. A policy failure does **not** spend the token (validation runs in the pipe, before the service sees it), so a typo does not force another email. Reset revokes **every** session and clears any lockout; `change` revokes every session **but the current one** — the asymmetry is deliberate and tested, since someone resets because they lost control of the password while someone changing it is present and signed in. "Differs from current" is checked against the stored hash rather than by comparing two strings. The reset link lives **one hour** against verification's 24 (ADR-025 §3: one proves an address, the other is an account takeover in one click). `POST /me/password` is mounted here rather than in M8 because the roadmap assigns the rules to this step even though API §2.3 lists the route under Users & Account.
- [x] **M6.6** — Email verification: `POST /auth/verify-email` + `/auth/verification/resend` (rate-limited).
  - ✅ Verify: verify → 204 and login unblocked.
  - **Verified — the item's own check, run as a sequence:** the account signs in with **403 `email_unverified`**, verifies with **204**, and signs in with **200**. `email_verified_at` is stamped; the same link a second time is **422 `token_invalid`** and the account **stays verified** — `VerifyEmailPage` fires from an effect on mount, so a refresh after a successful verification lands there, and the honest answer is that the token is spent. A reset token presented as a verification token is refused, the mirror of M6.5's case. Resend answers **202** identically for an unknown address, an already-verified one (saying "that address is already verified" would say the account exists) and one inside the cooldown, sending nothing in each; once the cooldown has passed it issues a **second, independently valid** link, and the first still works — DB §A10 inserts a new row rather than mutating the old, which is what a user expects when the first mail arrives late and they have already pressed resend. **A test-harness defect surfaced here and is worth recording for how it hid:** nodemailer encodes text as quoted-printable, whose soft line breaks wrap at 76 characters — mid-link, because the URL plus a 43-character token is longer than that. M6.1's "stores only the hash" assertion had been passing on a *truncated* token, since a truncated token does not match the stored hash either. The first test to actually *use* a token out of an email is the one that caught it; the sink now decodes the body the way a mail client does.

**🔒 Exit gate M6:** the frontend with `VITE_ENABLE_MOCKS=false` completes login → silent refresh on boot → logout against this API (auth seam swapped, zero component changes). *Blocked on M39.1–M39.4 for the error-code and 401 paths — schedule accordingly.*
  - **Gate status 2026-08-26: the API half PASSES; the frontend half is blocked, as the gate itself predicted.** `typecheck` 0 errors · `lint` 0 errors · **505 tests green** across 32 files (M5's 327 plus 178 new) · `build` clean · `db:check` no drift · `migrate deploy` + seed clean into a **freshly created empty database**.
  - **Why the frontend half cannot be run, precisely.** `VITE_ENABLE_MOCKS` exists (`frontend/src/configs/env.ts`) and is validated at boot, but **nothing reads it**: `app/providers/AuthProvider.tsx` imports `features/auth/mock/mock-auth-service` directly, at line 13, and there is no HTTP implementation to swap in. `src/api/errors.ts` also has no `code` field on its `ErrorBody`, so the eight auth codes this milestone emits are not yet readable by the client that will branch on them. Both are M39.1–M39.4, both are frontend work, and the gate names them. Swapping the seam here would be building M39 inside M6.
  - **What was proven instead, and why it is worth more than a claim.** `test/auth-frontend-contract.e2e-spec.ts` holds the exact conversation the frontend's own `ApiClient` will hold — requests built the way `src/api/client.ts` builds them (`Accept: application/json`, a `X-Correlation-Id` per request, `Authorization: Bearer` when a token is held, the refresh cookie alongside), responses read the way `src/api/errors.ts` reads them. Both files are **transcribed rather than imported**: the backend does not depend on the frontend package, and a test that imported the real client would prove the two agree only for as long as the import resolves. The sequence runs: sign in → **a fresh client with only the cookie** (a reload loses the in-memory token) → refresh, which returns the user the shell renders with → logout → the next boot refresh is a 401, which `normalizeHttpError` maps to `kind: 'auth'` and the client turns into a hard log-out. Also asserted: every success is a single-key `{ data }` envelope; the user object has **exactly** the five `SessionUser` fields and no sixth; the correlation id comes back on the error path; and the status→kind mapping for all five auth failures, each carrying the `code` the frontend will branch on once M39.1 adds the field. The 401 → refresh → replay interceptor is exercised as the client implements it: a stale access token is refused, the cookie still refreshes, and the replayed request succeeds.
  - **What remains for M40.2** is to point the seam at this API and re-run the same sequence in a browser. Every response shape it depends on is pinned by a test here, so a failure there will be a wiring failure rather than a contract one.
  - **Two ADRs.** **ADR-025** (the mail kernel — the fifth — and the four auth values no upstream source states, each labelled a backend default rather than a transcription; plus the lockout's accepted enumeration disclosure). **ADR-026** (`previous_token_hash`: rotation alone cannot detect reuse, and the row stays the family).
  - **One schema change on top of M5**, recorded rather than folded in silently: the migration named under M6.3. Table count unchanged at 106.

## Milestone 7: Authorization

**Goal:** RBAC enforced everywhere.

- [x] **M7.1** — `@Roles()` + `@Can(<permission>)` guards; permission resolution = role bundle + user overrides, Redis-cached, invalidated on change.
  - ✅ Verify: guard unit tests; cache invalidation test.
  - **Verified (2026-10-16) — the Redis cache, the last unticked part.** `PERMISSION_CACHE` is `RedisPermissionCache` (`shared/authz/redis-permission-cache.ts`), on the existing `REDIS_URL` under the `medora:authz:permissions:` prefix, same 5-minute TTL. `redis-permission-cache.spec.ts` (9 cases, real Redis) proves an invalidation on one instance is seen by another (`forget`, `forgetMany`, prefix-scoped `clear` that leaves BullMQ's keys), a server-enforced TTL, corrupt entries read as a miss, and an unreachable Redis failing in <1 s instead of queueing (offline queue off, 250 ms command timeout) so the resolver falls back to Postgres. `RolesService` now invalidates through `PermissionResolver` so a Redis fault after a committed save is logged, not a 500. Full backend gate: 128 files, 2,522 tests pass. Closes AF-25 for the cache; the Multi-Hospital A3 employment-end invalidation still needs Phase 22.
  - **Built and live except the Redis cache, which is the only reason this box is unticked.** `AuthorizationGuard` is registered as a global `APP_GUARD` in `auth.module.ts`, immediately after M6's `AccessTokenGuard`, and **every route in the application now carries a rule** — `@Public()`, `@Authenticated()`, `@Roles(...)` or `@Can(...)`. A route naming none is refused 403, which is the opposite of the usual default and is the point: a forgotten decorator is the likeliest authorization mistake anyone will make here, and denying turns it into a 403 in development instead of a disclosure in production. `test/authz-matrix.e2e-spec.ts` walks Nest's own route table and fails naming any unannotated handler — verified by removing one annotation and watching it report `AuthController.logout`. **The registration site is load-bearing, not tidy:** Nest instantiates global guards in module-import order, and `SharedKernelModule` is imported before the domain modules, so registering this guard in the kernel put it *ahead* of authentication — where it found no principal and refused every authenticated request 401 despite a valid token. That was not theory; it failed twenty-seven M6 e2e cases before the registration moved next to `AccessTokenGuard`. **`@Authenticated()` exists because the contract needs it:** API §0.4's fourth state (`✓`, any authenticated user) has five real routes — `POST /auth/logout` and the four `/me/…` account routes — that act on the caller's own account and have no capability to name. An earlier revision of this kernel deliberately omitted the decorator; five routes disproved the general case. Resolution is `bundle ± overrides` with **locks re-applied afterwards**, and `PrismaRoleRepository` now reads the real `role_permissions` and `user_permissions` rows rather than the catalogue's constants. **What is missing is Redis.** `InMemoryPermissionCache` is per-process: with one instance it is correct, with several an invalidation on one leaves the others serving a stale effective set until the TTL expires — a revocation that does not take. The TTL is five minutes, deliberately inside API §1.2's fifteen-minute token life, which bounds the damage but does not remove it. The swap is not optional before multi-instance deployment (M31).
- [x] **M7.2** — `PatientScopeService`: doctors reach only patients assigned via patient_doctor_assignments; all patient-child access routes through it.
  - ⚠ **Superseded in part (Multi-Hospital, 2026-10-07).** The record below is historical and stays true of the code. The *rule* changes in Phase 22: default access becomes active hospital + role + permissions (DR-05), and assignment stays hospital-scoped workflow data that never grants cross-hospital access (DR-07). 404-not-403 and RLS-before-production are kept. `docs/multi-hospital/BACKEND_CHECKLIST.md` B1–B2.
  - ✅ Verify: doctor A requesting doctor B's patient → 403/404 with no data leakage (test).
  - **Verified — the service is real, backed by the real table, and the negative case is proven against Postgres.** `PrismaPatientAssignmentRepository` replaced the placeholder that denied everything, and it hides two joins the port's callers should not have to know about: `patient_doctor_assignments.doctor_id` references `doctors.id` rather than `users.id`, so the token's subject resolves through `doctors.user_id`; and `patient_id` is a uuid while every route quotes `patients.public_id`. Matching a `PT-…` handle against a uuid column would never match — **a scoping check that always denies looks like it works right up until it locks a clinic out** — so both are resolved in one query, and the uuid arm is only added when the input actually looks like a uuid, because Postgres answers a malformed uuid literal with a type error rather than an empty result and would turn "not assigned" into a 500. **Revocation is honoured:** `revoked_at` non-null does not grant access, tested by revoking a seeded row and restoring it, because a revoked assignment that still reached the chart would make removing a doctor from a patient's care meaningless. **Out of scope is 404, never 403**, and the refusal is asserted byte-identical to the one a genuinely absent patient produces — a 403 confirms existence, and walking `PT-1000`, `PT-1001`, … against a 403/404 split would disclose the whole roster without opening a single chart (ADR-022's consequences require exactly this test). Holding a capability does not create a care relationship: `canReach` never consults the permission set and has no parameter through which one could be passed, which is asserted structurally so no later edit can special-case an identity without changing the signature (reconciliation §5 rule 5). Nine e2e cases in `test/authz-repositories.e2e-spec.ts` drive both repositories against the seeded database, including the matrix read back out of `role_permissions` and compared with the catalogue. **The clause "all patient-child access routes through it" cannot yet be violated — there are no patient-child routes — so it transfers as a standing obligation to M9–M14**, where each new clinical route must call `assertCanReach` rather than query a PHI table directly.
- [x] **M7.3** — Masked admin projections (name masking + MRN last-4) + `POST /patients/{id}/reveal` returning full identity **atomically with the audit write**.
  - ✅ Verify: reveal without `patients.revealIdentity` → 403 and no audit row; with it → 200 + audit row exists. **Masking output matches `frontend/src/utils/phi-mask.ts`** (the frontend shipped maskers in ADM-10; the two must agree character-for-character).
  - **Verified (2026-10-23).** `test/patients-reveal.e2e-spec.ts`, 11 cases against Postgres: an administrator whose capability was revoked through the real §15.5 override surface → **403 and no row by that actor**; an administrator holding it → **200**, the clear identity, and exactly one `patient_identity.reveal` row filed `security`/`warning` whose `occurredAt` equals the response's `revealedAt`. Masking parity was already proven by `shared/phi/phi-mask.spec.ts`, which loads the frontend module and compares output directly.
  - **"Atomically" is asserted as the failure it forbids.** A second application whose audit insert is genuinely refused by Postgres (a non-uuid `session_id`, through the real writer on the real transaction) answers **503 `audit_unavailable`** with no name, MRN or phone in the body and no row. That needed a writer the platform did not have: the M28 dispatcher writes on its own connection and never throws (RA-M28.2), so a reveal built on it would return the identity with no row behind it. `TransactionalAuditWriter` (`shared/audit`) writes on the caller's transaction and throws; it is used only where the audited act is a **disclosure** — this reveal and the M20.7 content view — and the seventy-odd mutation call sites stay on the best-effort dispatcher by design.
  - **The capability is not a care relationship.** `patients.revealIdentity` is seeded on the doctor row too, so a doctor's reveal runs `assertCanReach` first: a stranger's chart is the same 404 as a missing one, with no row. An unknown or soft-deleted patient is 404 with no row — revealing nothing is not an event.
  - **The justification is required and matches ADM-10**: one of the five `PHI_JUSTIFICATION_CATEGORIES` and a reason of ≥ 12 characters after trimming (`PHI_REASON_MIN_LENGTH`); anything else is 422 with no row. **The ledger keeps the category, never the free-text reason** — ADM-10's own log makes the same call (`logs.ts`, `fromPhiAccess`), because the reason is the one field on this path that can contain the patient's name. ⚠️ If the owner wants the reason retained for investigations, it needs a store that is not the masked ledger; that is a decision, not a defect.
- [x] **M7.4** — Roles management API (API §15): matrix read, preview (`affectedUsers`), update with optimistic concurrency (409 both-change-sets), custom role from template, per-user overrides.
  - ✅ Verify: concurrent matrix edit test → 409 with merge payload. The frontend's matrix editor (ADM-09, `features/admin/sections/settings/PermissionMatrixPanel.tsx`) is a live consumer — its merge dialog must work against this API.
  - **Verified — and the concurrent edit is driven the way one actually happens**, not simulated: two saves quote the same revision, the second is refused, and the refusal comes from a compare-and-set *inside* the save transaction rather than from a read-then-write with a window in the middle. The 409 carries all three things a merge dialog needs — the caller's change set, how the saved grid differs from the one they are holding, and the grid to rebase on — and the second save is proved to have written **nothing**.
  - **`revision`, not `updatedAt`, is the concurrency token.** API §15.3 writes the body as `{ grid, updatedAt, confirm }`; a timestamp is the wrong token for a compare-and-set, because `updated_at` has millisecond resolution and two saves inside one millisecond compare equal — the second would silently overwrite the first, which is the exact failure the 409 exists to prevent. The frontend's own `PermissionMatrixState` already carries a monotonic `revision` beside the timestamp, so the body takes that and the response returns `updatedAt` for display.
  - **A locked cell is rejected, never repaired.** `lockViolations` runs against the body **before** normalisation, because `normalizeMatrix` re-applies every lock — so a check afterwards could never fail and the endpoint would accept a grid that moved a locked cell while quietly saving something else. Reconciliation §5 rule 2 says the request "is rejected, not merely hidden in the UI", and a repair-on-save is the server-side way of hiding it. The test asserts the revision does **not** move, which is what distinguishes a rejection from a silent repair. An orphaning grid is refused the same way, on `orphanedCapabilities`.
  - **Per-user overrides re-apply locks in the response**, so an administrator sees what the guard will see. Granting `roles.manage` to a doctor writes the row the administrator asked for and still resolves to nothing — asserted three ways: the response's `effective` set, the permission list in that user's next access token, and a 403 from the §15 surface itself.
  - **Every save invalidates `PERMISSION_CACHE`** — `clear()` for a whole-grid save, `forget(userId)` for one override. A save that did not would leave already-cached users on the old bundle until the TTL expired, which is the revocation-that-does-not-take that M7.1 flags as the reason the in-memory cache cannot ship.
  - **A role outside the matrix is left alone.** The row delete is scoped to the roles being written, so `nurse` — which has a bundle and no column (ADR-017 §6) — keeps it. A blanket delete would have revoked a role the editor does not even display, and the insert would not have put it back.
  - **One migration, `20260911090000_m7_4_permission_matrix_state`.** `platform_settings` was the obvious home for the revision and is the wrong one: `platform_settings_key_check` restricts that table to DB §L1's eleven slice keys on purpose, so that a typo cannot shadow a real slice, and widening it would have spent the property the constraint exists for. The new table is a singleton pinned by a CHECK — "the revision" must not be a query that can return two answers. `role_permissions` remains the authoritative grid; this table carries only its aggregate identity.

**🔒 Exit gate M7:** table-driven authz matrix test (route × role × permission) green for all existing routes.

> ### M7 status — authorization is **on**, and the row is still open
>
> The decision core was built ahead of M5 and M6 per **ADR-027**; both have since landed,
> and this milestone has now been reconciled with them and switched on.
>
> **What is live.** `AuthorizationGuard` runs globally after M6's `AccessTokenGuard`. Every
> route carries a rule, and a route naming none is refused. Both repositories are
> Prisma-backed against the real tables. `PermissionResolver`, the matrix algebra and its
> locks, `PatientScopeService` and the PHI maskers are all real and tested — 802 tests, of
> which the authorization suites drive a booted application against a seeded Postgres.
>
> **What is not, and why the boxes below stay unticked:**
>
> | M7 item | State | Blocked on |
> |---|---|---|
> | M7.1 guards, resolution, deny-by-default, live on every route | **built** | — |
> | M7.1 Redis cache (`PERMISSION_CACHE`) | **deferred** — `InMemoryPermissionCache` is per-process, so a revocation does not propagate between instances | a Redis client; **required before M31** |
> | M7.2 care scoping, real assignments table | **done** — box ticked | — |
> | M7.3 maskers + `maskPatientIdentity` projection | **built** | — |
> | M7.3 `POST /patients/{id}/reveal` | **deferred** | needs `patients` routes (M10) and a durable audit write (M28) for the atomicity that is the endpoint's whole point |
> | M7.4 matrix algebra, lock enforcement, `lockViolations` | **built** | — |
> | M7.4 the API §15 endpoints | **built** — all five routes, see the M7.4 note | — |
>
> **The exit gate.** `test/authz-matrix.e2e-spec.ts` drives every (route, role) pair over
> HTTP through the real guard, asserts the full 5 × 22 resolver grid against the seeded
> database, checks that guard and resolver agree, and — the part that will matter most as
> M8 adds endpoints — walks Nest's route table and fails naming any handler nobody
> annotated.
>
> **Guarding against drift.** Three files transcribe the 22 capabilities: the frontend
> union, this kernel's catalogue and the M5 seed. `permission-catalogue.spec.ts` binds the
> first two by loading the frontend source; `seed-parity.spec.ts` binds the third; and
> `authz-repositories.e2e-spec.ts` reads the matrix back out of Postgres and compares.
> `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs the backend job on changes to the three frontend files for
> that reason.
>
> ### ✅ Four collisions with M6, found on merging `main` — all resolved
>
> M5 and M6 landed on `main` while this core was being built, so both sides defined
> overlapping primitives. All four are now fixed; the table records what they were, because
> two of them were the kind that stay invisible until something wires the pieces together.
>
> | # | Collision | Resolution |
> |---|---|---|
> | 1 | **Two `@Public()` decorators** — M6's `auth:public`, read by the global `AccessTokenGuard`, and a second one here with key `medora:authz:public` | **M6's kept, mine deleted.** The failure was symmetric and silent: a route marked with this module's version would still have demanded a token, and — because the authorization guard denies what it does not recognise — a route marked with M6's version would have been *denied* by this one. `shared/authz` now re-exports `common/auth`'s |
> | 2 | **Two principals on the request** — M6's `AUTHENTICATED_USER` symbol and a second `medoraPrincipal` slot here | **M6's kept, mine deleted.** `request-principal.ts` is gone; `principalOf()` narrows M6's `AuthenticatedUser` to the `Principal` authorization needs. This is the exact failure that file's own header warned about — one guard writing a slot another does not read fails **open** |
> | 3 | **Six seeded roles, five matrix columns** | **Not a defect on main — a defect here.** `nurse` is deliberate and documented on both sides: ADR-017 §6 puts the wider staff vocabulary in `roles`, the ADM-09 matrix has no nurse column because no source states what a nurse may do, and the seed calls an empty bundle "the fail-closed reading". `receptionist` and `pharmacist` are alias spellings (DB §14.5-7), not extra roles. The bug was that `Principal.platformRole` was typed to the five-role union, so a nurse was unrepresentable and the resolver would have read `undefined` off the matrix. `Principal.roleCode` is now a string and `bundleFor()` resolves a column-less role to `[]` |
> | 4 | **Two permission resolvers** — M6's `auth/services/permission.service.ts` (Prisma, once per token issue) and this branch's `PermissionResolver` (ports, cached, **and re-applying locks**) | **Both kept; the lock step moved into M6's.** They answer different questions — M6's fills the token, M7's serves the per-request path M7.4 needs — but only one applied locks, so a single `user_permissions` row could put `roles.manage` in a doctor's token or strip it from the last administrator's. `PermissionService.resolve()` now takes the role code and applies locks before sorting |
>
> **A fifth gap, found while fixing these.** The 22 capabilities are written down three
> times — the frontend union, this catalogue, and `prisma/seed/permissions.ts` — and only
> the first two were bound together. The seed populates the rows M6's live resolver reads,
> and its own `assertBundlesReferenceKnownCodes` validates bundles against the seed's *own*
> list, which passes just as happily when both halves are wrong together.
> `shared/authz/seed-parity.spec.ts` now binds the third.
>
> **Whoever closes M7 must:** swap `PERMISSION_CACHE` for Redis and prove invalidation
> across two instances, then build the audited reveal. The guard, the resolver, the scoping
> and the maskers are done and need no further work to close the milestone.
>
> **Updated 2026-09-11.** The §15 endpoints are built and M7.4 is ticked — M8 landed, which
> was the only thing blocking them. Two items remain, and neither is blocked by code that
> does not exist:
>
> · **M7.1's Redis cache.** Still the one reason M7.1 stays unticked. `InMemoryPermissionCache`
>   is per-process, so an invalidation on one instance leaves the others serving a stale
>   effective set until the five-minute TTL expires. M7.4's save paths now call `clear()`
>   and `forget()` on every write, so the invalidation *call sites* are real and tested —
>   what is missing is a cache that propagates them. Required before M31.
> · **M7.3's `POST /patients/{id}/reveal`.** Still waiting on M10's `patients` routes and
>   M28's durable audit write, because the atomicity of the reveal and its audit row is the
>   endpoint's whole point. The maskers and `maskPatientIdentity` are built.

**Spec and plan:** [`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-26-m7-authorization-core-design.md`](../../docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-26-m7-authorization-core-design.md) · [`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-26-m7-authorization-core.md`](../../docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-26-m7-authorization-core.md)

## Milestone 8: User Management

**Goal:** Accounts, sessions, devices, activity (API §2).

- [x] **M8.1** — `GET /auth/session` (me).
  - ✅ Verify: returns the exact `SessionUser` shape.
  - **Verified — and the shape is asserted as an exact key set, not a superset.** `SessionUserService` re-reads the `users` row rather than echoing the token, because `displayName` and `email` are not in the token and the shell renders both; a renamed user therefore changes on the next boot instead of on the next sign-in. **Permissions still come from the token**, deliberately: API §1.11 fixes them for that token's life, and re-resolving them here would hand the client a capability list that disagrees with the one the guards are enforcing for the very same request — `useCan()` would render a button the API then refuses. `account-security.e2e-spec.ts` asserts the key set is exactly `{id, name, email, role, permissions}` (so a column added to `users` cannot reach the shell by default) **and** that it is byte-identical to the payload `POST /auth/login` returned, because two code paths now build this shape and a client trusting one must be able to trust the other. A suspended account answers 401, not 404 — the client's correct response is to sign out, and a 404 on your own session renders as a broken page with the user still apparently signed in.
- [x] **M8.2** — Admin platform-users endpoints: list/invite/patch (role, active) — soft deactivation only.
  - ✅ Verify: deactivated user's login → ~~401~~ **403 `account_inactive`**; row not deleted.
  - **Verified, with one correction to the verify step and one endpoint deliberately not built.** The refusal is **403 `account_inactive`**, not 401: ADR-035 §6 landed after this line was written and made a non-ACTIVE account a distinct, coded answer rather than a generic credential failure. The property the line was after is asserted — sign-in refused, `deleted_at` still null, `status = INACTIVE`. **`POST /admin/users` is not built and should not be**: it already exists as `POST /auth/admin-invitations` (ADR-035 §5), guarded by the same `roles.manage`, covering admin/reception/laboratory/pharmacy, minting no password and delivering the token to the invitee's inbox alone. A second provisioning path would either duplicate those guarantees or, far more likely, quietly not have them. **Deactivation is `status`, not a soft delete**, reversing an assumption `IdentityRepository`'s own comment recorded: `PlatformUser.active` is a boolean an administrator flips, so the state must be reversible and the row must stay visible in the list it was toggled from — and a soft-deleted row is filtered from `findByEmail`, which would answer a suspended clinician `invalid_credentials` and tell them their password is wrong. **Four refusals guard the patch**, each proved to leave the row unchanged rather than merely to return a 4xx: self-edit (403), promotion to `admin` (409 `two_factor_required` — ADR-035 §7 makes MFA structural, so this route would otherwise create an administrator `LoginService` refuses forever), demoting the last active administrator (409), and deactivating them (409, the same thing by another name). The last two are unit-tested rather than driven over HTTP: the guard counts *every* active administrator, which is right and is exactly what a parallel e2e suite cannot control. **Deactivation now ends live sessions**, which exposed a real hole while it was being built — see the M8.3 note.
- [x] **M8.3** — Active sessions: list (`ActiveSession` shape), revoke one, revoke-others.
  - ✅ Verify: revoked session's refresh → 401.
  - **Verified over HTTP with the real cookie, not by reading `revoked_at` back out** — the column being set is not the property that matters. **Revoking one session leaves the others alive** and `revoke-others` leaves the caller's own, which is asserted both ways: a "sign out everywhere else" that logs you out of the device you pressed it on is the failure this route exists to avoid. **Another account's session id answers 404 byte-identically to a nonsense one** — a 403 would confirm the id names a real session — and a non-uuid id is a 404 rather than the 500 Postgres would raise on a malformed uuid literal. **The list carries neither token hash nor `expires_at`**: the hashes cannot mint a session, but a value on the wire is a value in a log, and publishing the TTL tells a caller how long a stolen cookie stays useful.
  - **A real gap was found and closed here.** `RefreshService` checked only that `findById` returned a row — which soft delete hides — and never read `users.status`. So `LoginService` refused a suspended account while an account suspended *while already signed in* rotated its refresh token indefinitely and kept working forever. That is precisely the case suspension is pulled for, so the administrative lever was a no-op for its main use. The check is now explicit, and the session is revoked as well as refused so the family cannot be retried.
  - ~~**Known and bounded:** revocation takes effect on the next refresh, not instantly. The access token already issued stays valid for up to fifteen minutes (API §1.2) because it is a signed bearer token and nothing reads the session row per request.~~ **Superseded by PRE-01 (2026-11-14).** Owner decisions I4 and O-13 (FINAL) made per-request session validation policy, and `AccessTokenGuard` now asks `SessionService.isActive` on every protected request: a revoked or expired session, or an account that is no longer `ACTIVE` or is soft-deleted, is refused with 401 `unauthorized` from the next request on. The read is uncached PostgreSQL (I2; C-12 open). `POST /auth/logout` alone carries `@AllowEndedSession()` (read from the handler only), so logging out of an already-ended session stays 204 and still clears the cookie; it waives liveness, not identity — the token's session must still exist and belong to its subject. Evidence: `test/session-validation.e2e-spec.ts` (30 cases), `src/modules/auth/guards/access-token.guard.spec.ts` (12). Authorization and status: `docs/mfa-recovery/addenda/2026-11-14_OWNER_DECISION_ADDENDUM_PRE-01.md`. **Not built:** the "wrong session kind" refusal (spec §5.1), because recovery sessions do not exist yet.
- [x] **M8.4** — Trusted devices CRUD.
  - ✅ Verify: CRUD round-trip matches `TrustedDevice` shape.
  - **Verified, exact key set again, and two properties beyond the round trip.** **Removal is soft** — `removed_at`, asserted by reading the row back after a 204, because the schema calls the record of when a device stopped being trusted worth more than the row it saves. **Exactly one device is ever flagged current**: registering a second `current: true` clears the first *in the same transaction*, because "this device" pointing at two machines is a state the UI cannot render honestly and is reachable the moment a user registers a second laptop. All seven `DevicePlatform` members are accepted (the frontend's union names four), and an eighth is a 422 with a field error rather than a silent coercion.
- [x] **M8.5** — Login history endpoint (paginated `LoginEvent`).
  - ✅ Verify: failed M6 attempts appear with `result: "failed"`.
  - **Verified by making a real wrong-password sign-in, not by inserting a row.** The projection deliberately **drops `failure_code`**: the eight `AuthErrorCode` values name *why* an attempt failed, including `email_unverified` and `account_inactive`, and the history page renders `success | failed` and nothing finer. The finer answer belongs to M28's audit surface, where an administrator reads it with context — asserted by scanning the serialized body for both the field name and a code value. Offset paging rather than keyset: this page is small and numbered, and M32.3 moves the *audit* surface, which is the one that grows without bound.
- [x] **M8.6** — User activity log: writer hooks (profile/password/settings/security/integration/privacy) + `GET /me/activity` feed.
  - ✅ Verify: a password change produces an activity row of kind `password`.
  - **Verified through `POST /me/password`, not by writing a row.** The hooks are now one thing rather than three: `ACTIVITY_RECORDER` joined the shared kernel (`shared/activity`), on the same argument that put the mailer and the authz guard there — the six kinds are written from six different modules and M2.2's zone 1 forbids any of them importing another. The two pre-existing direct writers (password recovery, 2FA) were migrated onto it, **and that migration fixed a live defect rather than tidying**: both did a bare `await create(...)`, so a failing feed insert would have turned a *completed* password change or 2FA enrolment into a 500 the client retries — and the retry would present a password that no longer works. The recorder swallows and logs. One writer stays direct on purpose: `admin-invitation.service.ts` writes inside the activation transaction, where atomicity is worth more than the shared fail-soft behaviour, and the comment says so.
  - **The feed names what changed, never the value.** A phone-number edit records "Phone number updated" and the number appears nowhere — asserted by scanning the entry for the digits. A username is the stated exception, because it is a public handle by construction. The same rule governs the audit metadata beside it: field *names*, never their values.
  - **Remaining, and owned by their own milestones:** the `settings`, `integration` and `privacy` kinds have no writers yet because they have no endpoints yet — M22.2, M22.3 and M22.5 are where those slices land, and M22.5 states the hook as its own requirement.

**🔒 Exit gate M8:** ✅ **Settings→Security and the admin Users panel are fully served.** Every §2 route the two screens read exists: account document and patch (§2.1–2.2), password (§2.3, M6.5), sessions (§2.4–2.5), devices (§2.6), login history (§2.7), activity (§2.8), 2FA (§2.9, M6.4), platform users (§2.10). The suite is **981 backend tests, 0 failures**, of which 61 are new here and every one drives a booted application against a seeded Postgres. `test/authz-matrix.e2e-spec.ts` walks Nest's route table and confirms all fourteen new handlers carry a rule.

> ### Where the M8 routes live, and why it is not all one module
>
> The split follows what a route *needs*, not what its path says.
>
> | Routes | Module | Why |
> |---|---|---|
> | §2.1, §2.2, §2.8, §2.10 | `users` | Read and write `users` and `user_activity_log`, and nothing else |
> | §2.3, §2.4–2.7, §2.9 | `auth` | Need the hasher, the session revoker or the envelope key — the identity aggregate `IdentityRepository` documents as one thing rather than five tables. Zone 1 would not let `users` borrow them in any case |
> | §2.10's invite | `auth` | It is `POST /auth/admin-invitations` (ADR-035 §5) and already stronger than a second POST would be |
>
> **One route-path collision is now visible and belongs to M9.** `GET /me/profile` is taken by `PatientProfileController` (ADR-030, the patient's own profile) and API §3.1 assigns the same path to the *doctor* profile document. Two controllers cannot both claim it. M9.1 has to decide — most likely by role-dispatching inside one controller, since `/me/…` means "the caller" and the caller's profile shape legitimately depends on what they are. Recorded here rather than discovered at M9.
