## Milestone 13: Prescriptions (Pharmacy)

**Goal:** Safety-screened prescribing (API §7). **Highest-risk module — fail closed. 100% test coverage on the safety service.**

- [x] **M13.1** — Medication catalogue + monograph endpoints (typeahead: name/generic, filters, `allergyClass`, controlled/high-risk flags).
  - ✅ Verify: typeahead p95 < 100 ms on seeded data.
  - **Verified — 16 cases, p95 = 6.0 ms seeded and 7.3 ms at 505 rows**, against a 100 ms budget. The percentile is measured twice on purpose: the seed holds five medications, and a percentile over five rows measures the HTTP stack rather than the query — it would pass with a sequential scan. The second run loads 500 more, which is the order of magnitude a real formulary reaches, and is the measurement that could actually fail. `q` is one `OR` across `name` and `genericName` written as `contains` + `mode: 'insensitive'`, because that emits `ILIKE '%…%'` — the only predicate the two `gin_trgm_ops` indexes from M5.5 can answer. `startsWith` would have quietly lost both indexes and stopped matching mid-word, which is what a drug typeahead must do: "oxic" finds Am**oxic**illin. **`?highRisk=false` means false** — `z.coerce.boolean()` maps the string `"false"` to `true`, so the flag is an explicit `'true' | 'false'` enum and a spec asserts both directions. The catalogue carries `strengths[]` where the frontend's `Medication` carries `strength`: that type describes a medication *on a prescription*, where one strength was chosen, and the formulary is the other side of that choice (DB §F2). A medication with **no monograph is 404, not a 200 of eight nulls** — the latter renders as a drug asserted to have no contraindications, a clinical claim nobody made. Guarded by `prescriptions.create` rather than bare authentication: a formulary names the controlled substances the clinic stocks, and administrators deliberately do not hold that capability.
- [x] **M13.2** — `POST /prescriptions/safety-check`: allergy match (patient allergies × `allergyClass` ⇒ blocking critical), drug–drug interactions (severity-graded from `drug_interactions`), duplicate therapy; returns `{ warnings[], blocking }`.
  - ✅ Verify: seeded penicillin-allergic patient + Amoxil → `blocking: true` with critical allergy warning.
  - **Verified — the verify step passes through HTTP against the real seeded formulary**, and the screen carries **100% coverage** (statements 45/45, branches 22/22, functions 23/23, lines 36/36) enforced by `npm --prefix backend run test:safety`, which fails the build below 100 rather than printing a number. 46 cases: 31 unit over the pure screen, 15 e2e over the wiring. **The clinical rules are transcribed, not invented** — `frontend/src/features/doctor/pharmacy/model/safety-check.ts` is the *coded* screen and therefore rank-1 evidence, and it fixes the three gradings this milestone would otherwise have had to guess: an allergy is blocking **always** and `critical` only when the patient has a `severe` allergy on file; duplicate therapy is `critical` for the same generic and `warning` for the same category, neither blocking; `blocking` on the response means an allergy conflict stands, because allergy is the only warning marked blocking (UX §2.7, "no override in v1"). **One rule departs from the frontend on instruction:** interactions are graded from `drug_interactions` per this sub-item, not from `medications.interaction_note` — one canned string per drug says the same thing whatever the other drug is, which is ADR-010 §8's own argument for the table. **A bug the e2e spec caught and the fail-closed default was hiding:** Prisma returns enum *members* (`WARNING`) where the port speaks wire values (`warning`), and the consumer grades anything unrecognised as `critical` — so every interaction, `info` ones included, was being reported critical while the critical case passed for the wrong reason. All three seeded grades are now asserted. `INTERACTION_SOURCE` is a token, not a direct dependency, so M13.3's chaos test can substitute a failing one. **No `dosage` rule is implemented** — see PD-13.2 below.
  - **PD-13.2 — no source states a dosing rule, so none is invented.** `SafetyWarningKind` has a fourth member, `dosage`, and API §7.5 lists it. Deciding that a strength × frequency × duration is too much for a given patient is a clinical rule: `medication_monographs.standard_dosing` is free prose, there is no per-drug maximum column, no weight or renal-function input, and the coded frontend screen implements "the three specified categories" and no fourth. A threshold invented here would be a number nobody clinically approved deciding whether a prescription is safe. **Blocked pending a product/clinical decision**; the enum value stays available. A spec asserts the screen raises no `dosage` warning, so the day a rule is supplied, adding it is a deliberate act rather than a gap quietly closing.
  - **Known gap, recorded not papered over:** a medication the patient takes but this clinic never prescribed does not participate in the pairwise interaction check. `patient_medications.name` is `VarChar(120)` free text with no `medication_id`, and `drug_interactions` is keyed by medication id on both sides, so the join does not exist. Closing it is a schema change (`medication_id` on `patient_medications`) and not this milestone's to make.
- [x] **M13.3** — Create draft + `POST …/sign`: server **re-runs the safety check in-transaction**; allergy conflict → 409 `allergy_conflict` (**no override path exists**); severe interaction requires `{ acknowledgedWarningIds, reason }` else 409 `acknowledgment_required` (ack audited); safety source down → 409 `safety_check_unavailable` (**draft-only**).
  - ✅ Verify: all three 409 branches tested + chaos test with the interaction source disabled proves send is impossible but draft save works.
  - **Verified — 77 cases (17 e2e + 60 over the ADR-002 machine).** All three 409 branches are asserted through HTTP, and the chaos test boots a **second application** with `INTERACTION_SOURCE` bound to an implementation that always throws — the only honest way to disable the source, since breaking the database would take the rest of the request down and prove nothing about fail-closed behaviour. It asserts the verify step in both halves: `POST /prescriptions` still returns 201 with a `draft`, and `/sign` answers 409 `safety_check_unavailable` **and leaves the row a draft**, because the fail-closed rule is a *state* a client can filter on (ADR-002 §1), not an error code it can retry past. **"In-transaction" is literal**: `runInTransaction` opens the transaction, the screen runs against that `tx`, and every refusal throws out of it — so a refused signature writes nothing at all, which each 409 case asserts by re-reading the prescription and finding no timeline event, no warning rows and no `patient_medications` row. Screening first and signing in a second transaction reads almost the same and is not the same: a drug added to the patient's list between the two would be screened against and then ignored. **`allergy_conflict` has no override, and a spec proves it** by sending the acknowledgment fields that clear branch 2 and getting the same 409 — "no override path exists" is only true if nothing in the request body is one. **A tick with no reason is not an acknowledgment** (UX §2.7), and the reason is persisted on the warning row where a later review reads it. The status move is compare-and-set on `DRAFT`, so two racing signatures cannot both write a `prescribed` event. **Drafting is deliberately ungated** — a draft for an allergic patient saves fine; the harm is prescribing, not drafting, and the verify step says "draft save works" in as many words. `RX-…` gets its own sequence (migration `20260913160000`), starting at 200000, below API §7.7's worked example rather than colliding with it. §7.4 (detail) landed here too, because no verify step is assertable without reading the document back.
  - **PD-13.3 — "or the policy requires approval" is not implemented.** ADR-002 §3 routes `/sign` to `pending-approval` when the medication "is controlled **or the policy requires approval**". The controlled half is implemented from `medications.is_controlled`; the policy half has no field anywhere to express it — no scheduling or prescribing slice exists in `platform_settings` (the same absence ADR-033 D-1 records), and inventing one would mean deciding which medications need countersigning. `is_high_risk` deliberately does **not** route here either: high-alert means a mistake is unusually harmful, which is a reason to warn the prescriber, not a reason to require a second signature — conflating them would put every anticoagulant in an approval queue no source asks for.
- [x] **M13.4** — Lifecycle: renew (pre-filled draft), cancel/discontinue with reason (P4/audited), refill accounting, timeline events (8 kinds).
  - ✅ Verify: refill bounds enforced; renew copies the source Rx correctly.
  - **Verified — 28 e2e cases, both halves of the verify step with a `describe` each.** **Renew copies correctly**: the *therapy* is copied verbatim (patient, medication, strength, dosage, frequency, route) and the *course* is overridable (duration, quantity, refills, instructions, start date), with `endDate` always re-derived rather than copied. A renewal is a **new draft**, not a status change — ADR-002 §3 makes `completed`/`discontinued`/`expired` terminal, so a second course cannot be the first one moved backwards, and a spec renews a discontinued prescription to prove it. The new draft goes through `/sign` like any other, and a spec records a fresh allergy between renewal and signing to prove the screen re-runs: a renewal that inherited the source's verdict would be the easiest way to re-prescribe a drug the patient has since become allergic to. The `renewed` event lands on the **source**, where a reader looking at the finished course asks "what happened next". **Refill bounds** are enforced at three layers and all three are asserted: the schema (`MAX_REFILLS = 11` from `authoring.ts:159`, with the boundary of 11 accepted so the range is not over-tight), the service (`authoring.ts:213` — **a controlled medication cannot carry refills**, re-checked on renew because a renewal can change the count without going through create), and the CHECK `prescriptions_refills_remaining_within_allowed`, asserted directly against the database because a constraint nobody tests is one a later migration quietly drops. The allowance becomes the remaining count **at signature**, not at creation — a draft has authorised nothing — and stopping therapy clears it. **`/cancel` and `/discontinue` both write `discontinued`** (ADR-002 §4) and are told apart by the audit event and the timeline detail, which is the only place the distinction can live; the P4 reason is refused empty at the schema and by the CHECK. Stopping therapy also flips the patient's `patient_medications` row to `discontinued` in the same transaction. §7.3 (list) and §7.10 (notes) landed here; the list's `q` deliberately does **not** match patient names, which would turn it into a patient finder with none of §4's masking.
  - **D-13.1 — ADR-002 §3 and the M5.8 CHECK contradict each other on `draft → discontinued`.** The lifecycle table lists `draft` among the origins of `discontinued` and the M5.10 trigger permits it, but `prescriptions_signed_at_matches_status` is `(status = 'draft') = (signed_at IS NULL)` — so any row leaving `draft` must carry a signature, and a withdrawn draft was signed by nobody. Both artefacts derive from ADR-002 and they disagree on exactly this transition. **Not resolved here, because neither resolution is this milestone's to invent:** stamping `signed_at` would record that a clinician signed a prescription they explicitly withdrew — a false entry in a clinical record — and relaxing the constraint is a schema decision protecting ADR-002 §1's "every status past draft is reached through `/sign`". The path is refused in the service with 409 `draft_not_discontinuable` naming the reason, rather than left to surface as a constraint violation the caller reads as a 500. An abandoned draft may simply be left: it dispenses nothing, the expiry sweep ignores it, and it never reaches the patient's medication list. The machine's table still *allows* the transition, so the contradiction is recorded rather than papered over. **Needs an owner decision** — relax the CHECK, or amend ADR-002 §3 to drop `draft` as an origin.
  - **Three of the eight timeline kinds have no route in API §7, and none was invented.** `prescribed` (M13.3), `renewed`, `note-added`, `discontinued` and `expired` (M13.5) are all reachable and asserted. `dispensed`, `refill-requested` and `refill-approved` are in the enum and in the frontend's `PrescriptionTimelineKind`, but **API §7 defines no endpoint that would write them** — there is no dispense route and no refill-request route in the contract's table. A spec asserts their absence so the gap stays deliberate. Whoever adds them is defining new API surface, not implementing existing surface.
- [x] **M13.5** — Expiry sweep job (BullMQ): active → expired past endDate; refill-due computation.
  - ✅ Verify: job test with clock control.
  - **Verified — 13 cases, every one clock-controlled.** `sweep(now)` takes its clock as an argument rather than calling `new Date()`, which is what makes this a test of the job rather than of the system time; nothing sleeps and nothing depends on the system date. **The boundary is the assertion that matters**: a course ending *today* has not expired and one ending yesterday has, because `end_date` is a `DATE` and comparing it against a timestamp would expire a whole day's prescriptions at midnight UTC — a day early for every timezone behind it. A second case advances only the clock and watches the same row expire. Expiry covers exactly the three statuses ADR-002 §3 names (`active`, `dispensed`, `pending-approval`); `draft` is not swept because it was never prescribed, and the terminal statuses are already terminal. The pass is **idempotent** — a second run at the same instant moves nothing and writes no second timeline event — and each row moves under a compare-and-set, so a prescription a clinician discontinued mid-sweep stays theirs. Expiring writes the `expired` timeline event with **no actor** (nobody did this; the calendar did — `actor_name` is nullable for exactly this case), clears the refill counts, and completes the patient's `patient_medications` row in the same transaction. ADR-002 §3's row is unqualified, so **refills remaining do not exempt a course from expiry** — a repeat is a renewal, not an expiry that quietly does not happen.
  - **BullMQ is deliberately not added, and this is not a shortcut.** RM **M30.2** owns the "scheduled jobs registry: **prescription expiry**, retention purges, …" and M30.1 owns the queue registry, retries and dead-lettering. So M13.5 owns the *sweep* and M30 owns *when it runs* — the same split `shared/outbox` already records ("published messages are logged and discarded … the consumers are M19's channels and M30's workers, and neither exists"). Building a queue here would put the same infrastructure in two milestones and leave M30 removing it.
  - **PD-13.5 — no source states when a refill becomes `due`.** API §7's header lists `due` in the refill vocabulary; the frontend's mock assigns it **at random by weight** (`mock-data.ts:109-113`); UX names no window; there is no column, setting or default anywhere. So the *mechanism* is built and the *number* is the caller's: `sweep(now, { refillDueWindowDays })` computes `due` when given a window and, when not, marks nothing and returns `refillDueWindowDays: null` — distinct from "found none", so the absence of a policy cannot be read as an empty result. A hardcoded constant was ruled out for the reason ADR-033 Q4 gives about the same class of decision: it "is not a policy — it is a policy nobody can change without a deploy". Both paths are tested. **Needs an owner decision**, and a home for the value (a scheduling slice in `platform_settings` would be the natural one, and does not yet exist — the same absence ADR-033 D-1 records).
- [x] **M13.6** — Patient current-medication list materialization (Rx-linked + external entries).
  - ✅ Verify: signing an Rx adds a `patient_medications` row.
  - **Verified — 10 cases.** Signing writes the row **in the signing transaction**, not as a follow-up, so the three things that must never happen cannot: a draft materialises nothing (ADR-002 §1 — "it does not appear in the patient's medication list"), a **refused** signature materialises nothing because the row is rolled back with the refusal, and a prescription cannot reach `active` without its entry. The list then **keeps step**: discontinuing marks the entry `discontinued`, and the M13.5 sweep marks it `completed` — different acts, and the list says which, because "the course ran its length" is not "someone decided to stop it". **External entries** (`prescription_id` null) are never touched by any prescription operation: every lifecycle write filters on the prescription that owns the row, and two specs prove a self-reported drug survives both a discontinuation and a sweep. The read surface already existed from M10 (`GET /patients/{id}/medications`, §4.5); what it lacked was provenance, so `PatientMedicationView` gains **`prescriptionId`** on both the tab and the profile card. That distinction is clinical rather than bookkeeping — an Rx-linked row went through the M13.2 screen at signing and an external one never did, so a list rendering both identically invites a reader to assume the same assurance behind each.
  - **Note on test isolation:** the M13.5 sweep selects across the whole table, as a job must, which makes it the one thing in this suite able to reach another spec's fixtures. Both specs that run it are dated to **2025** so every other spec's prescriptions sit safely in their future.

**🔒 Exit gate M13:** safety service coverage report = 100%; all three 409 branches proven by API test. ⚠️ **The four-step prescribing stepper does not exist in the frontend** — `features/doctor/pharmacy` has zero mutations (frontend checklist §0.3, M39.5). The original "stepper completes against the API" gate cannot be met at this milestone; it moves to **M40**.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-13: PASSED on both testable conditions.** **Safety coverage = 100%** — statements 45/45, branches 22/22, functions 23/23, lines 36/36 on `safety-screen.ts`, and it is an *enforced* gate rather than a reported number: `npm --prefix backend run test:safety` fails the build below 100 (proven by temporarily introducing an uncovered branch — exit 1). **All three 409 branches are proven by API test** in `prescriptions-sign.e2e-spec.ts`, each re-reading the prescription afterwards to show the refusal wrote nothing, plus the chaos test booting a second application with the interaction source disabled. **190 new cases across M13**; the full backend suite is **1518 green across 74 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean.
  - **Four decisions are recorded and open, none of them invented:** **PD-13.2** (no dosing rule exists, so no `dosage` warning is raised), **PD-13.3** (ADR-002's "or the policy requires approval" has no field to express it), **PD-13.5** (no source states when a refill becomes `due`; the mechanism is built and the number is the caller's), and **D-13.1** (ADR-002 §3 and the M5.8 CHECK contradict each other on `draft → discontinued`). Each is described at its own sub-item above. **Three timeline kinds — `dispensed`, `refill-requested`, `refill-approved` — have no route in API §7**, and adding one is new API surface rather than unbuilt surface.

---

## Milestone 14: Laboratory

**Goal:** Lab order pipeline (API §8).

- [x] **M14.1** — Catalogue + analyte specs endpoints.
  - ✅ Verify: reference + critical ranges served per analyte.
  - **Verified — 17 cases in `lab-catalogue.e2e-spec.ts`, against the seeded catalogue rather than a fixture.** Both bounds *and* both thresholds are served per analyte, read back through HTTP as **numbers**: `ref_low` and its siblings are `DECIMAL(10,3)` and Prisma hands them over as `Decimal` objects, which `JSON.stringify` renders `{"s":1,"e":0,"d":[4]}` — a payload no client can chart. `lab-test-projection.ts` is the single place that conversion happens. **A one-sided threshold stays one-sided**: TSH has a `criticalHigh` of 50 and deliberately no `criticalLow`, because a suppressed TSH is not the emergency an extremely high one is, and a null coerced to 0 would mark every TSH critically low. `referenceText` is computed by `referenceTextFor`, the **same function** the released result's `ref_text` is snapshotted from, so the interval the order form previews and the interval printed beside a result cannot be worded two ways. The typeahead rides `lab_test_catalogue_name_trgm_idx`: `contains` + `insensitive` emits `ILIKE '%q%'`, which `pg_trgm` answers from the index, and a clinician typing "metabolic" finds **Basic Metabolic Panel** — `startsWith` would find nothing. `labs.review` guards it, which is the only laboratory capability the 22-permission union has; a patient account gets 403.
- [x] **M14.2** — Orders CRUD + pipeline transitions (ordered→collected→processing→completed, cancel) with timeline events (7 kinds).
  - ✅ Verify: transition matrix test.
  - **Verified — the matrix is asserted exhaustively, all twenty-five cells** (20 unit cases in `lab-status.machine.spec.ts`, which also carries M14.3's gate). `lab-status.machine.spec.ts` walks every ordered pair of the five statuses and compares against the six moves API §8 declares; a table tested only on its allowed edges passes just as happily when a backward edge is added by mistake, and a backward edge here means a released result becoming provisional. Separate cases state the properties rather than leave them incidental: no backward edge, no self-transition (a stale client re-collecting is the lost-update race), `completed` and `cancelled` terminal, and **cancellation reachable from every live step** — a tube is dropped, a patient leaves, an analyser rejects a sample. 46 further cases in `lab-orders-pipeline.e2e-spec.ts` drive the same matrix through HTTP and assert that **a refused transition writes nothing at all**, by re-reading the order rather than trusting the response. Every status move is **compare-and-set** on the status the caller read, so two clients advancing one order produce one 200 and one 409 rather than two timeline entries claiming the specimen was collected twice. **`LAB-…` gets its own sequence** (migration `20260915090000`), starting at 100000, below the frontend corpus's `LAB-100004` rather than colliding with it. §8.2's `doctor (own orders / care-scoped)` is implemented as the **disjunction it is written as** — an order this doctor placed *or* an order for a patient in their care: taking only the first would hide a locum's order on the caller's own patient, which is the case the critical-unreviewed chip exists for.
  - **`completed` writes a `verified` timeline event, not a `completed` one.** DB §E7's vocabulary has seven members and no `completed`; §8.6 names `resulted`/`verified` as the pair, so entering values is `resulted` and releasing the order is the verification. Inventing an eighth kind to match a status name would contradict the enum *and* the frontend's `LAB_TIMELINE_META`, which draws exactly seven. **All seven kinds are reachable and asserted**, `received` included: it is written on the null→value edge of `lab_specimens.received_at` and exactly once, so a specimen received at collection does not "arrive" a second time at `processing`.
  - **A defect the spec caught: two chain-of-custody CHECKs were reaching clients as 500s.** `lab_specimens_received_after_collected` and `lab_orders_collected_after_requested` (M5.8) are both satisfiable only if the timestamps run forwards, and both `collectedAt` and `receivedAt` default to the server clock — so a client sending only `receivedAt` was comparing its own instant against one the request had just minted, milliseconds later, and the constraint refused the row. `assertCustodyOrdered` now checks the **resolved** values and answers 422 naming the field, which is what a caller can act on. A draw entered an hour after the fact is still accepted, because that is a real case and the constraint is about ordering rather than about forcing the server's clock onto every event.
- [x] **M14.3** — Specimen capture; condition ∈ {Hemolyzed, Clotted, Insufficient volume} blocks `processing` → 409.
  - ✅ Verify: blocked-condition test.
  - **Verified — each of the three conditions has its own case, and the set is derived rather than retyped.** `BLOCKING_CONDITIONS` is every member of `LabSpecimenCondition` except `ACCEPTABLE`, computed from a `Record<LabSpecimenCondition, boolean>` — so a fifth condition added to DB §E4 fails to compile until someone decides whether it is runnable, and *defaults to blocked* rather than to runnable. A hand-written set of three would default the other way, which is the fail-open direction. The refusal is 409 `specimen_unusable` and its `detail` says **why the number would be wrong** — a hemolyzed sample releases potassium out of the red cells it broke, so a hemolyzed potassium reads high, sometimes critically high, for a patient whose potassium is fine — and ends with what to do next, which is recollect. **The gate is not in the status machine**, deliberately: `invalid_status_transition` tells a client its request was out of order and `specimen_unusable` tells a lab to redraw, and folding the second into the first would answer a clotted sample with a message about allowed statuses. A blocked specimen may still be **cancelled**: refusing that too would strand the order. A missing specimen gets its own code, `specimen_missing`, because "no specimen" and "bad specimen" are different problems with different remedies.
- [x] **M14.4** — Results entry: snapshot reference ranges at result time, compute flags, maintain `hasCriticalResult` (trigger).
  - ✅ Verify: a critical-high value sets the order's `hasCriticalResult`.
  - **Verified — 25 e2e cases plus 24 unit cases over the grading ladder.** The verify step binds three mechanisms together and each is asserted: a potassium of 7.2 grades `critical-high` against the seeded spec, the M5.10 trigger sets `lab_orders.has_critical_result`, and the projection reads it back. **Nothing writes that column by hand** — DB §14.5-6 forbids storing derived values and grants this one exception "for index use", so the repository inserts the results and reads the flag back, which is what keeps a stored derivation honest. A corrected value **clears** it, because the trigger recomputes on every change rather than only on insert; a chip still counting a value that has been corrected is a phantom alert. **The flag is the server's judgement**: `enterResultsSchema` has no `flag` field, and a body carrying one is stripped — a client that could assert `normal` on a potassium of 7.2 would be asserting that a result nobody needs to see today is fine, and RM M14.5's bulk path reads exactly that field to decide whether a checkbox may clear it. **The reference interval is snapshotted** onto each row and asserted from both sides: revising the catalogue leaves an already-released result with the interval and the flag it was judged by, and grades the *next* result against the new one. The ladder's boundaries get their own cases because they are where a phone call happens or does not — the reference interval is **inclusive** (5.1 is normal, which is what a reference *interval* means) and the critical thresholds are **inclusive** (6.0 is critical, because a threshold a result may sit on without raising the alert is a threshold one unit higher than written). The two conventions cannot collide: `critical_low < ref_low` and `critical_high > ref_high` are CHECKs, so the bands are strictly disjoint. **The batch is all-or-nothing** — five of six analytes recognised is a CBC silently lacking its platelet count — and every problem is collected into one 422 so a technician fixes them in one pass.
  - **PD-14.4 — a qualitative result cannot be critical, and the database is why.** `lab_results_numeric_flag_needs_value` (M5.8) is `flag IN ('normal','abnormal') OR value_numeric IS NOT NULL`, so a result with no numeric value can only be normal or abnormal. The frontend's demo corpus grades some qualitative options `critical-high` by a regular expression over the option text (`/malignant|isolated|cfu|incompatible|high-grade|lesion/i`), and the database refuses the row that rule produces. It is also the wrong instrument for the question: "No growth" contains no keyword and "isolated" appears in benign findings, and deciding that a positive culture is a critical result is a clinical rule rather than a pattern match. A spec asserts that **no** qualitative option grades critical, so the day a rule is supplied, adding it is a deliberate act. **Needs an owner decision** if qualitative criticals are wanted: it is a column on `lab_analyte_specs` plus a relaxed CHECK, and both are schema changes.
- [x] **M14.5** — Review workflow: critical results individually only (`bulk:false`); bulk allowed only when all normal; write failure surfaces (no silent success).
  - ✅ Verify: bulk request containing a critical row → 422; failed write returns 5xx (never 200).
  - **Verified — 26 e2e cases and 27 unit cases, both halves of the verify step with a `describe` each.** The property is one sentence — *a critical result cannot be marked reviewed by accident* — and it is structural rather than conditional: `assessReviewability` is the **only** producer of `ReviewableOrder`, that type carries a module-private symbol, and the write demands one. A `if (bulk && hasCritical) throw` somewhere in the service is one refactor away from being reordered below the write; a value that cannot be forged is not. `assertReviewable` is the runtime half, and a spec proves a cast does not survive it. **Bulk means normal, not non-critical**: a merely `high` potassium is refused too, because the point of the restriction is that a result a doctor should have *looked at* never gets cleared by a checkbox in a header row — the frontend's `partitionForBulkReview` reads `flag !== 'normal'`, and this is the transcription. The bulk branch is checked **before** the acknowledgment branch, so a client that ticked every critical id and supplied a note is still refused: bulk is not where a critical value gets reviewed, however completely the form is filled in. **A partial bulk request writes nothing and names every row it refused**, keyed by reference so a client can mark the offending rows individually — a bulk review that quietly skips the critical results is worse than one that refuses them, because the doctor believes their queue is clear. **"Write failure must surface" is a property of the transaction**: `markManyReviewed` is one transaction and the service deliberately does *not* catch, because a `catch` returning a partial success here is exactly the optimistic success the verify step forbids. A reference the caller named and the server cannot find is a 404 before any write is attempted; a row reviewed by someone else mid-flight loses its compare-and-set and is a 409 naming the difference. **A tick with no reason is not an acknowledgment**, and the acknowledged ids are normalised to the order's own critical results so a client sending ids from the row above cannot have them stored verbatim.
  - **D-14.1 — the acknowledgment has nowhere on the row to live, and is not durable yet.** `lab_orders` carries `reviewed_at` and `reviewed_by` and nothing else: DB §E3 has no acknowledgment columns, unlike DB §F4, which gives a prescription's safety warnings `acknowledged_by`, `acknowledged_reason` and `acknowledged_at` tied together by a CHECK. `LabTimelineKind` has no `reviewed` member either, so there is not even a timeline row to put the note on. The evidence is therefore dispatched to `AUDIT_DISPATCHER`, and a spec boots a second application with that port substituted to prove the call site is real and carries the right payload. **But the dispatcher's current implementation is `LoggingAuditDispatcher`**, the M2 stub that writes a log line — `audit_events` is not wired behind the token until M28, and that file says so itself. So today a critical acknowledgment is *required*, *recorded at the call site*, and *not retrievable*. M28 makes it retrievable with no call site changing; a column on `lab_orders` would make it retrievable beside the review it belongs to. **Needs an owner decision** — either is a schema or a milestone decision, and inventing a migration on a clinical table to satisfy a milestone is not this one's to make.
- [x] **M14.6** — Trends endpoint: per-analyte series per patient (max 3 overlays, reference band).
  - ✅ Verify: series shape matches the chart contract.
  - **Verified — 20 cases, asserting the field names as well as the values.** The chart contract is `AnalyteTrend` in `features/doctor/labs/model/trends.ts`, the type `AnalyteTrendChart` already plots, so `points[].labId`, `.at`, `.value` and `.flag` are asserted by name and the series carries flat `referenceLow`/`referenceHigh`/`referenceText` rather than the nested `referenceRange` a result carries — a chart series has one range for the whole series. **A trend needs a patient, not a test**, which is why the route hangs off `/patients/{id}` and is care-scoped like any other chart read. **The newest result's range wins** while each point keeps the flag it was given at its own time — `buildAnalyteTrend` overwrites the range on each pass through an ascending series, and a spec revises the catalogue mid-history to prove both halves at once. **The cap is a 422, not a truncation**: a client asking for four series and getting three back, with no indication which was dropped, would chart a patient's potassium and believe it was their sodium. Only **completed** orders are plotted — a value entered while the order is still processing has not been verified or released, and plotting it would put an unreviewed number beside released ones with nothing to tell them apart — and only **numeric** ones, because a series of "Negative" is not a line chart. An analyte with nothing to plot is named in `empty` rather than served as an empty series, which most chart libraries render as a flat line at zero: a clinical claim nobody made.
  - **Two things are deliberately not served: the series tone and the band's drawability.** `chart1…chart3` ladder by luminance and carry no clinical meaning — the clinical meaning is in the point's own flag — so a server that assigned colours would be deciding how a chart looks. And `trendBand` returns null unless every selected series shares one unit and one range, because "a band drawn under three units would be a band for whichever series happened to be first"; that rule is about what is being *drawn*, it is already coded and tested, and serving a second implementation of it would be two answers to one question. The server serves each series' own bounds, which is what "reference band included" asks for.
- [x] **M14.7** — Attach-to-record with identity check (`confirmedPatient` name+MRN must match → else 422) + attachments + summary incl. `criticalUnreviewed` count.
  - ✅ Verify: identity-mismatch test; count endpoint p95 < 50 ms.
  - **Verified — 20 cases in `lab-attachments.e2e-spec.ts` plus the summary's own `describe` in `lab-review.e2e-spec.ts`.** The identity check is the frontend's `assessAttachability` transcribed, and it is a real gate rather than a sentence in a dialog: the failure it guards against — a result filed onto the wrong patient's chart — is one of the few in this product that reading the screen again does not undo. **Name and MRN are compared independently**, so a right name with a wrong MRN is refused rather than passing on the strength of the half that matched; it means one of the two records is wrong about who this is, which is precisely when a human should stop. The server's check is **not a duplicate of the client's**: the client compares the ticked identity against the *record* it chose, and this compares it against the **order's own patient read from the database**, so a client with a stale snapshot passes its own check and fails this one — which is the direction that matters. **The mismatch response does not echo the correct name**, or the identity check would become a lookup: send a guessed name, read the real one out of the 422. A record on another patient and a record that does not exist produce byte-identical refusals. Every refusal is asserted to have written nothing.
  - **The attachment is a link, not a copy.** `record_attachments.file_id` points at the **same** `files` row the lab attachment does: one set of bytes, two references. Copying would double the storage and, worse, create a second object a later correction would not reach — a record showing a superseded result it believes is current. The row is `kind = 'lab-result'`, which is what RM **M15.1**'s verify step asks to see, and the record's own timeline gets an `attachment-added` event, because a document that appeared in a chart with nobody attached to it is not an attachment. A second attach of the same report is refused rather than doubled.
  - **§8.11 depends on M25, and both states are tested rather than one being assumed.** An attachment's bytes go through the `FILE_STORAGE` port, whose production binding is `UnconfiguredFileStorage` until M25 wires MinIO — so against the real stub the route answers **503 `service_unavailable`** and writes no `files` row, which a spec asserts by counting. That is the same answer `/api/v1/health` already gives for storage (API §20.1) and the same one the profile-photo surface gives. The `files` row is created **only after `put()` resolves**, so no row ever claims bytes that do not exist, and the checksum is computed from the bytes that were stored rather than from a client's assertion. A second spec boots an application with an in-memory implementation — the way RM M13.3's chaos test substitutes `INTERACTION_SOURCE` — and drives upload and attach-to-record end to end. Base64 is validated by round trip, because `Buffer.from(value, 'base64')` skips characters it does not recognise instead of failing, so a corrupted upload would otherwise decode to *something* and be stored as a file nobody can open.
  - **§8.10's `criticalUnreviewed` is answered by the partial index that exists for it.** `lab_orders_critical_unreviewed_idx` (M5.8) is `ON (requested_at) WHERE has_critical_result AND reviewed_at IS NULL`, and the repository's predicate is written to match it literally so no extra clause takes the query off it — that index is the whole reason DB §14.5-6 permits `has_critical_result` to be stored. The chip's meaning is asserted in both directions: an unreviewed critical result raises the count and reviewing it lowers it again, so the number means "critical results waiting on you" rather than "critical results". **The p95 < 50 ms half of the verify step is not asserted**, and is recorded rather than quietly claimed: a latency budget measured on a seeded test database with a handful of rows, inside a suite that boots an application per file, would be measuring the harness. It belongs with the performance work of **M35**, against a dataset with a realistic row count. What *is* proven here is the property the budget depends on — the count is six `count`s over an indexed predicate rather than a join or a scan.
  - **D-14.2 — the "lab role" half of §8.5 and §8.6 has no scoping model, so today only a doctor can reach them.** The contract marks §8.5 `doctor/lab role` and §8.6 `lab role`. Both routes are implemented and both are guarded by `labs.review`, which the `laboratory` bundle holds. But `PatientScopeService.canReach` returns false for every principal whose role is not `doctor`, by design — "every other platform role reaches patient data through the masked projections of API §4.11" — so a laboratory account has no care relationship to any patient and therefore no path to their order. Giving it one means deciding what scopes a technician: their department? every specimen in the building? That is an authorization design decision, and ADR-027's open item **RA-027.1** already records the adjacent fact that three of the five platform roles have permission bundles and no shell to log into. `requireDoctor` is where a non-doctor account stops, with the same non-disclosing 404 every other module gives. **Needs an owner decision**, together with RA-027.1.

**🔒 Exit gate M14:** labs list/detail/trends render; critical-review discipline proven by tests. ⚠️ Ordering and review have no UI — `features/doctor/labs` has zero mutations (M39.5).
  - **Gate status 2026-09-15: PASSED on the testable condition; the UI half moves to M40.** **Critical-review discipline is proven by tests** — 53 cases across `lab-review.spec.ts` and `lab-review.e2e-spec.ts`, including both halves of M14.5's verify step and the unforgeability of `ReviewableOrder` at runtime. The laboratory's clinical core carries **100% coverage** (statements 97/97, branches 77/77, functions 31/31, lines 78/78) over `result-grading.ts`, `lab-review.ts`, `specimen-condition.ts` and `lab-status.machine.ts`, and it is an *enforced* gate rather than a reported number: `npm --prefix backend run test:labs-safety` fails the build below 100, proven by temporarily introducing an uncovered branch — exit 1. **"Labs list/detail/trends render" cannot be met at this milestone** for the same reason M13's stepper gate could not: `features/doctor/labs` reads from `mock-lab-service.ts` and has zero mutations (frontend checklist §0.3, M39.5). Every endpoint those screens need exists and is tested through HTTP; wiring them is **M39.5**, and the rendering half of this gate moves to **M40** alongside M13's, where M40.6 already lists "M14 lab ordering and critical review". **225 new cases across M14** — 154 e2e over HTTP and 71 unit over the pure rules; the full backend suite is **1743 green across 83 files**, with typecheck, lint, build, `db:check` and both coverage gates clean.
  - **Three decisions are recorded and open, none of them invented:** **PD-14.4** (a qualitative result cannot carry a critical flag, because the M5.8 CHECK forbids it and a regex over option text is the wrong instrument for a clinical rule), **D-14.1** (the critical acknowledgment is required and recorded but not yet retrievable — `lab_orders` has no columns for it and the audit dispatcher is still M2's logging stub), and **D-14.2** (the `laboratory` role holds `labs.review` and has no care-scoping model, so §8.5's and §8.6's "lab role" is unreachable today). Each is described at its own sub-item above. **The p95 < 50 ms measurement in M14.7's verify step is deferred to M35**, where a realistic dataset exists to measure against.

## Milestone 15: Cross-module Integration Pass

**Goal:** The links between pharmacy ↔ records ↔ labs ↔ dashboard the UI expects.

- [x] **M15.1** — Lab report → record attachment flow wired end-to-end.
  - ✅ Verify: attached report appears in the record's attachments (kind `lab-result`).
  - **Verified — already satisfied by M14.7, and re-checked rather than rebuilt.** `test/lab-attachments.e2e-spec.ts` asserts the verify step literally: after `POST …/attach-to-record`, `GET /medical-records/{id}` carries one attachment of kind `lab-result` with the report's name. Two further properties make the flow end-to-end rather than merely present — the record's **own** timeline gets an `attachment-added` event naming the order (a document that appeared in a chart with nobody attached to it is not an attachment), and the `files` row is **shared, not copied**, so a later correction reaches both references rather than leaving the record showing a superseded result it believes is current. A second attach of the same report is refused rather than doubled. Nothing was added here; the sub-item was complete before M15 opened, and saying so is more useful than manufacturing a second spec over the same behaviour.
- [x] **M15.2** — Record-medication ↔ prescription linkage.
  - ✅ Verify: prescribing from a record links both ways.
  - **Verified — 11 cases in `records-prescription-link.e2e-spec.ts`, and the milestone is where the link stopped being one-way.** `prescriptions.record_id` has been written since M13.3 — §7.6 accepts an `MR-…` handle and the prescription's view has always carried `recordId`. **The record knew nothing:** `record_medications` existed, carried a `prescription_id` column, and no code ever set it. So the chart — the surface a clinician actually opens — could not say what was prescribed at the encounter. Signing now materialises the record entry **in the signing transaction**, exactly as M13.6's `patient_medications` row is, and for the same three reasons, each asserted: a draft materialises nothing (ADR-002 §1), a refused signature materialises nothing because the write rolls back with it, and a prescription bound to no visit touches no record. It also writes **`medication-prescribed`** on the record's timeline — the first writer of a kind the enum has carried since M5.7. The entry then **keeps step**: `discontinued` when therapy is stopped, `completed` when the M13.5 sweep ends the course, because "the course ran its length" is not "someone decided to stop it". A hand-documented medication (`prescription_id` null, never screened by §7.5) is never touched by any prescription operation, which is M13.6's external-entry rule applied on this side, and `RecordMedicationView` gains **`prescriptionId`** so a reader can tell the two apart — an Rx-linked row went through the safety screen at signing and a typed one did not.
  - **A defect the linkage exposed, and closed.** `PATCH /medical-records/{id}` with `medications` replaces the encounter's medication list, and the replacement soft-deleted **every** row. So correcting a typo in one typed line would silently drop every prescribed drug out of the chart while `prescriptions.record_id` still pointed back at it — the link would read one way only and the encounter would under-report what was prescribed at it. The replacement now touches only entries with `prescription_id` null. An Rx-linked row leaves the chart by discontinuing the prescription (§7.9), which carries a reason and an audit entry; a typo fix carries neither. Two cases pin it, and both fail without the filter: the edit preserves the prescribed drug, and an empty array still clears what the author typed (the protection must not over-reach into "you may never remove a medication line again").
- [x] **M15.3** — Patient profile tabs aggregate live data from the fact modules.
  - ✅ Verify: profile Labs/Prescriptions tabs show M13/M14 data.
  - **Verified — 12 cases in `patient-profile-aggregation.e2e-spec.ts`, both halves against the same chart so they cannot drift apart.** The two were in very different states. **Prescriptions** was already live: M13.6 made signing materialise the `patient_medications` row, so `GET …/medications` has served the real M13 list since then — asserted here anyway, because the verify step is about the *profile* and a tab reading the right table but never exercised from the profile's surface is not evidence. **Labs had no source at all**: `PatientLabStudiesTab` reads a frontend store and the M14 orders behind it had no patient-scoped read anywhere on the API. `GET /patients/{patientId}/labs` is that read, and the laboratory owns it for the reason `LabTrendController` records — the path is patient-child but the catalogue categories, the released-result counts and the M14.5 review state are this module's.
  - **"Aggregates live data" is tested as a property, not a snapshot.** The tab has to *move* when the fact module moves, so the M14 pipeline is driven and the profile re-read after each step: an order appears the moment it is placed (the state a clinician opens the tab to find — "did anyone send it?"), reads as `collected` then `processing` as the laboratory advances it, carries the M14.4 critical flag from the M5.10 trigger rather than recomputing it, and **stops saying unreviewed once the result is reviewed** — which is the frontend's own claim for this tab, made true. The display date is the frontend's `completedDate ?? collectedDate ?? requestedDate` resolution, transcribed, because dating a completed study by when it was ordered would misdate the result. Grouping iterates the **catalogue vocabulary** rather than the patient's rows, so sections do not reshuffle as studies accumulate. Care-scoped like every other chart read: a colleague gets **404**, byte-identical to a patient that does not exist (ADR-022).
- [x] **M15.4** — Doctor action-queue endpoint (pending requests + unreviewed criticals + unsigned notes + stale messages, sorted urgency→age).
  - ✅ Verify: each source feeds the queue in a seeded scenario; response < 200 ms.
  - **Verified — 18 e2e cases plus 13 unit over the ordering rule; both halves of the verify step met.** `GET /dashboard/doctor/action-queue`, the Row 2 widget of UX §2.1. A **standalone widget endpoint** rather than part of the composite, because §16.2 explicitly allows one ("widgets may also be fetched individually") and UX §2.1's loading state *requires* it — the queue is priority-fetched before the rest of the page, and a queue that could only arrive inside an eight-widget composite would be as slow as the slowest widget beside it. M40 composes this rather than replacing it.
  - **Each of the four sources is driven into existence through the API that owns it**, then found in the queue: a pending appointment request, an unreviewed critical potassium of 7.2, an unsigned consultation note, a stale thread. A fifth case seeds all four against one doctor, which is the verify step's "seeded scenario" literally. Rows **leave** the queue when dealt with — signing the note, reviewing the result — which is what distinguishes a queue from a list.
  - **Scope differs per source, and each difference is the owning module's own rule.** Own book for requests (§5.1); ordered-by **or** care-scoped for labs, the disjunction `lab-order.repository.ts` writes, because taking only the first would hide a locum's order on the caller's own patient; own **authorship** for notes, not care scope, because M12.4 refuses a colleague's signature with 403 `not_note_author` and a colleague's note here would be a row with no action behind it; own threads for messages. Each has a negative case.
  - **The sort is a pure module with its own spec.** "Urgency then age" is the one rule in the widget, and the queue is **oldest-first** — inverting every other list in this application, which is exactly the detail a refactor tidies the wrong way. The unit spec states the properties over arrangements the e2e cannot cheaply build (antisymmetry, total order, no age leaking across bands, stability under re-ordered input); two e2e cases prove the rule is actually *reached*, since a correct sort that is never called is the failure a unit test cannot see.
  - **The messaging source is real and, today, empty.** `conversations`, `conversation_participants` and `messages` exist from M5.9, and the predicate — a thread the caller participates in, not archived, whose newest live message was written by someone else more than 24 hours ago — is derived entirely from columns the schema already defines. **M20 owns the messaging feature**, so nothing writes those tables in a running deployment and this source contributes no rows. That is the honest state and is different from omitting it: M20 inherits a working queue rather than a fourth source still to wire. "Unanswered" is expressed as "the last word was not mine" rather than `unread_count > 0`, because a doctor who *opened* a thread and did not reply has read it and still owes an answer; archived threads are excluded, since a queue that kept surfacing one would make the control meaningless. Four negative cases cover archived, answered-last, under-24-hours and a system message.
  - **Response < 200 ms**, measured with all four sources populated and after one warm request, in `doctor-action-queue.e2e-spec.ts`. Four queries in parallel rather than in series is what buys it.

> **Defect found during M15, owned by M14 — `lab_timeline_events` has no
> tiebreaker (RA-M14.1).** The lab order's projection orders its timeline
> by `occurred_at DESC` alone (`lab-order.repository.ts`), and the
> `received` and `processing` events are written **inside one
> transaction**, so both take the same `now()` to millisecond precision.
> When they tie, Postgres returns them in arbitrary order — a clinician's
> chain of custody can render "processing" above "received", and
> `lab-orders-pipeline.e2e-spec.ts`'s exact-order assertion fails
> intermittently.
>
> It is **pre-existing**: neither the repository nor that spec is touched
> by M15/M16/M17, and the test passes in isolation. M15 surfaced it by
> adding three more spec files, which raises contention and makes the
> same-millisecond tie more likely — roughly one full-suite run in three
> on this machine.
>
> **Deliberately not fixed here.** The right repair is a decision about
> *what* breaks the tie — a monotonic per-order sequence column (a schema
> change) versus an arbitrary but stable sort key — and that belongs to
> the laboratory milestone with its own branch and migration, not to a
> cross-module integration pass. Recorded so it is not lost.
>
> **Closed at both sites, by two different repairs on purpose.** A scan of
> every `$transaction` that writes timeline events found a **second
> site**: creating a record writes `created` and `diagnosis-added` in one
> transaction (`record.repository.ts`), so that pair always ties as well.
>
> - **Lab timeline** — `2d6861c` stamps `received` one millisecond before
>   `processing`, explicitly rather than by column default. Receipt
>   genuinely precedes processing, so the interval is true, and
>   `lab-orders-pipeline.e2e-spec.ts` asserts the timestamps.
> - **Record timeline** — the projection orders by
>   `[{ occurredAt: 'desc' }, { kind: 'desc' }, { id: 'desc' }]`. The two
>   events are one act rather than two steps, so inventing an interval
>   would record a time that never happened. `RecordTimelineKind` declares
>   `CREATED` first and Postgres sorts a native enum by declaration order,
>   so `kind DESC` puts creation last; `id` is a stability backstop only.
>   No schema change. `test/record-timeline-ordering.e2e-spec.ts` writes
>   tied events earliest-first — the reverse of what a reader is owed — and
>   is proven load-bearing both ways: with the sort key removed the tie case
>   fails, and with `kind` promoted above `occurredAt` the time-first case
>   fails instead.

> **Second pre-existing flake, found while running the M18 gates — a
> *class* of cross-suite teardown race (RA-M10.1).** A suite's
> `harness.reset()` deletes its users by email suffix;
> `doctors.user_id` is CASCADE, so that deletes its doctors too — and
> several clinical tables are **RESTRICT** on `doctor_id`. If any
> *other* concurrently-running suite has attached a row to that doctor,
> the reset fails with a foreign-key violation and the suite reports a
> **teardown** failure while every one of its tests passed.
>
> Observed twice in five full-suite runs, each time a different victim
> and a different constraint — `appointments_doctor_id_fkey` (victim:
> `ai-assistant`) and `prescriptions_prescribing_doctor_id_fkey`
> (victim: `appointments-book`, a file that creates no prescriptions at
> all). The rotating victim is the signature of the class.
>
> **One instance is proven**: `patient-appointments.e2e-spec.ts` lines
> **132 and 372** resolve the doctor with
> `harness.prisma.db.doctor.findFirstOrThrow()` — **no `where` clause at
> all** — and create a `CONFIRMED` appointment against whichever row the
> database returns first. That accounts for the appointments variant
> exactly. **The prescriptions variant has a different origin that is
> not yet pinpointed**: `prescriptions-safety`, `prescriptions-sweep` and
> `schema-labs-pharmacy` were each checked and all three scope their
> doctor correctly, so at least one further unscoped binding remains to
> be found. Recorded as unfinished rather than guessed at.
>
> This is the exact anti-pattern `test/support/patient-fixture.ts`
> documents at length — "they originally borrowed the seeded demo
> records … `findFirstOrThrow()` … it fails perhaps half the time under
> load, passes every time when the file is run alone" — fixed for
> *patients* at ADR-030 and never fixed for *doctors*.
>
> It is **pre-existing and not caused by M15–M18**: every helper involved
> is untouched by them, and M18 changed two markdown files only. What
> changed is the *odds* — three more spec files means more concurrent
> suites for an unscoped lookup to land on.
>
> **Deliberately not fixed at M18.** M18 is ADR-only. The repair is to
> scope each offending lookup the way `test/support/patient-fixture.ts`
> already scopes patients — by the suite's own tag — and it needs a sweep
> for the remaining instance plus repeated full-suite runs to show the
> flake is actually gone, which is its own piece of work rather than a
> line smuggled into a documentation milestone.
>
> **Partially closed 2026-09-17, during the M15–M17 re-audit. The proven
> instance is fixed; the unproven one is still unproven.**
> `test/support/doctor-fixture.ts` is the doctor-shaped counterpart of
> `patient-fixture.ts`: a tagged `doctors` row plus the `users` row it
> hangs off, created in `beforeAll` and torn down explicitly in
> `afterAll`. Its user deliberately sits **outside** the harness's own
> email domain, so a `harness.reset()` between tests cannot cascade the
> fixture away mid-file — which would be the same failure, self-inflicted.
>
> `patient-appointments.e2e-spec.ts` now uses it. Both former
> `findFirstOrThrow()` sites are gone: `seedAppointment` hangs its
> `CONFIRMED` appointment off this file's own practitioner, and the
> field-leak assertion reads **the doctor actually on the appointment**
> by id. That second change fixes a defect of its own — asserting that a
> response does not contain an arbitrary other practitioner's licence
> number is vacuous, so the case passed whatever the projection did.
> `notifications.e2e-spec.ts` had the same borrow in patient form
> (`patient.findFirstOrThrow()`, feeding assertions over that row's `mrn`
> and `fullName`) and now owns a `PT-Tnotif0` record instead; its FK is
> `SET NULL` rather than `RESTRICT`, so it was never the teardown class —
> it is fixed as the same anti-pattern, not as the same failure.
>
> **The prescriptions variant remains unpinpointed, and is now recorded
> as un-reproduced as well.** All five direct writers of
> `prescribing_doctor_id` in the suite were re-checked by grep (as were
> the two of `lab_orders.ordered_by`) and every one is correctly scoped —
> `prescriptions-safety` and `prescriptions-sign` resolve the doctor from
> the caller's own `/me/profile`, `prescriptions-sweep` takes it as an
> argument from the same, and `schema-labs-pharmacy` and `schema-platform`
> use their own `createClinicFixture` tag. Every remaining prescription in
> the suite is created through `POST /prescriptions`, which can only name
> the authenticated doctor. Two hypotheses were tested and both are
> **ruled out**: the seed's demo accounts sit at `@medora.health` and no
> suite's reset suffix (`<suite>.m6.test`) can match them; and
> `auth-app.ts`'s module-level `suiteDomain` cannot leak between files,
> because `vitest.config.ts` sets no `isolate: false` and the default
> forks pool gives each spec file its own module registry. One
> full-suite run before the fix and **three consecutive runs after it**,
> against one database built from empty by `db:deploy` + `db:seed`, were
> all **1893 green across 91 files** with no teardown failure of either
> variant — and the third ran under deliberate CPU contention from two
> concurrent `npm ci` jobs, which is the load the flake prefers.
> That is not proof the class is gone — it was two in five runs at
> its worst and these runs are on one machine — so RA-M10.1 **stays
> open** against the test-consolidation work that owns it (M34), with
> the search narrowed rather than finished.
>
> **A residue source closed, and the remaining sites named.**
> `account-security.e2e-spec.ts` reset in `beforeEach` but not in
> `afterAll`, so the **last** case's doctor and user stayed in the
> database permanently — one leftover per run, enlarging the pool every
> unscoped lookup draws from. Its teardown now resets too.
>
> A parenthesis-balancing scan for `findFirst`/`findFirstOrThrow` with no
> `where` clause (it finds calls a plain grep for `findFirstOrThrow()`
> misses) identified further sites of this class that are **still
> open**, recorded here for M34 rather than fixed piecemeal:
>
> - `patient-api.e2e-spec.ts` ~272 — the worst: it **suspends** an
>   arbitrary `VERIFIED`+`ACTIVE` doctor and restores it, so for the width
>   of the suspension another suite's own doctor vanishes from a
>   patient-facing list that suite may be asserting on. ~351 and ~373 read
>   an arbitrary doctor of the same filter, which its owner can delete
>   mid-request.
> - `doctor-administration.e2e-spec.ts` ~584 and
>   `doctor-availability.e2e-spec.ts` ~455 and ~507 — an appointment is
>   created against an arbitrary `patients` row, and
>   `appointments.patient_id` is RESTRICT, so it can block that patient's
>   owning suite at teardown.
>
> The repair is the rule `patient-fixture.ts` and `doctor-fixture.ts`
> already state — each suite owns the rows it hangs data off. An
> alternative that hands suites deterministic handles onto the seeded demo
> records (`MED-DEMO-*`, `PT-100x`) was considered and **not adopted**:
> `patient-fixture.ts` exists precisely because suites borrowing `PT-1001`
> and `PT-1002` raced each other, and the `patient-api` case would still be
> mutating a shared row.

**🔒 Exit gate M15:** doctor Overview action queue fully live.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-16: PASSED on the server half; the render half moves to M40.** The queue **is** live: four sources, care- and ownership-scoped per source, sorted urgency then age, inside the latency budget, with 18 e2e and 13 unit cases behind it. "Fully live" in the sense of *rendering on the doctor's Overview* cannot be met here for the same reason M13's stepper gate and M14's render gate could not — `features/doctor/dashboard` reads `model/mock-data.ts` and has no action-queue widget at all (frontend checklist §0.3, M39.5). The endpoint every such widget needs exists and is tested through HTTP; wiring it is **M39.5**, and the rendering half joins M13's and M14's at **M40**, where M40.1 already carries the Overview. **57 new cases across M15**; the full backend suite is **1893 green across 91 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean. (One pre-existing M14 spec flakes under full-suite load — see RA-M14.1 above; it passes in isolation and is unrelated to this work.)
