## Milestone 16: AI Assistant **[R2 — new]**

**Goal:** The doctor-only clinical assistant (API §11, DB §H1–H4). Revision 1 created the `ai` module skeleton and the tables but never implemented the endpoints. All routes require can:`ai.use`; **admins have no entry point**.

> ⚠ *Multi-Hospital (2026-10-07):* AI context becomes hospital-bound; cross-hospital data only under a valid grant or Break-Glass, re-checked every turn; no raw cross-hospital retention after authorization ends, including in `ai_messages` (C1/C2/C3). Doctor-approved content saved to the requesting hospital's record is that hospital's data (OQ-01). Phase 28, `BACKEND_CHECKLIST.md` §I.

- [x] **M16.1** — `AiGateway` in `shared/`: provider-agnostic adapter owning every LLM call; keys server-side only; no other module talks to a provider. Prompt/completion logging under PHI rules with retention limits.
  - ✅ Verify: grep proves exactly one module imports the provider SDK; a fake adapter drives all tests.
  - **Verified — the grep is a test, not a habit.** `AI_GATEWAY` is a kernel port in `shared/ai`, bound by `AiKernelModule` and reachable only through `shared/ai/index.ts` (M2.2 zone 2, added to `SHARED_PUBLIC_API`). `ai-containment.spec.ts` runs the grep the verify step asks for **as an assertion over the shipped source**: only `shared/ai/` may import any of seven provider SDKs, only `shared/ai/` and `config/` may name `AI_API_KEY`, and the AI module never names the concrete gateway class (which is what lets a test substitute a fake, and what stops a module pinning a binding the kernel chooses). None of the seven packages is installed, so the list is **forward-looking on purpose**: it holds the boundary on the commit that first adds a provider, rather than being written afterwards once the import is in the wrong place. Comments are stripped before matching — the first draft failed on its own prose explaining why `AI_API_KEY` is unreachable, and a containment test that cannot tell code from the comment describing it is one that punishes documentation.
  - **A fake adapter drives every test**, exactly as the verify step asks. `ai-assistant.e2e-spec.ts` overrides the token with a scriptable fake that can stream cleanly, fail partway, or hang until the caller aborts — the three outcomes M16.4 has to distinguish. No test reaches a provider and none could.
  - **The production binding is `UnconfiguredAiGateway`, which refuses.** The same choice `UnconfiguredFileStorage` makes, for a sharper reason: a stub returning canned prose would be *worse* than a failure, because the answer would arrive in a clinical assistant's own voice with nothing on screen to say a model was never consulted — a clinician reading "no interaction found" would have no way to know whether anything looked. `/health` already reports `ai: unconfigured` from the same `ConfigService.isAiConfigured` (ADR-016), so the endpoint and the binding agree by construction. Everything except generation still works without a key, which the last `describe` of the e2e drives against the ordinary application.
  - **"Prompt/completion logging under PHI rules" resolves to logging the metadata and not the text**, and `ai-transcript.log.ts` records the argument at length. A patient-bound prompt *is* PHI (M16.3 resolves the binding into it); MG §11.6 and DB §10 forbid clinical content in application logs; and ADR-007's retention has `autoPurge: false`, so a line written today is still there in a year. Logging the text would write an unbounded, unpurged copy of chart content into a sink with none of the chart's access controls — and **outside** the soft delete of `ai_conversations`, so deleting a thread would leave the PHI in the logs. What is logged is shape and cost: conversation, user, whether a patient was bound (**a boolean, never an id** — enough to answer "were any patient-bound generations in flight during the window" without the log becoming a list of which patients were discussed), character counts in and out, duration, outcome. `retentionDays` rides on every line so a pipeline can expire these independently; it takes ADR-007's shortest existing period rather than inventing one. The transcript itself is not lost — `ai_messages` holds it verbatim, inside the database, under the same care scoping and soft delete as the rest of the chart.
- [x] **M16.2** — Conversations: `GET /ai/conversations` (`q`, `favorite`) · `POST` `{ context }` · `PATCH …/{id}` (title, favorite) · `DELETE …/{id}` (soft) · `GET …/{id}` with messages.
  - ✅ Verify: CRUD round-trip matches `features/doctor/ai/model/types.ts`; delete is soft (row survives).
  - **Verified — the round-trip is asserted against the frontend's shapes, and the soft delete is asserted from both sides.** A created thread matches `AiConversation`; `?favorite=true` and `?q=` filter; `DELETE` answers 204, the thread is **gone from the API** (404 on re-read) **and still in the database with its tombstone** — which is the verify step's "row survives", checked directly rather than inferred. A second delete answers 404 rather than reporting success for nothing. `q` searches **titles only**: searching message bodies would make the parameter a PHI search surface across every thread the caller owns, and the frontend's own list filters on title.
  - **Scoped by ownership, not care.** A thread is the doctor's own working notes with an assistant; the sharing model this application has is about the *chart*. A colleague reaching for one gets 404, identical to a thread that does not exist. `messageCount` is on the list row rather than the messages themselves — a doctor with fifty threads should not download every word of every one to render a sidebar, but an empty thread and a fifty-turn thread must not look alike in it.
- [x] **M16.3** — Context binding, server-authoritative: client sends only `{ mode: general|patient|record|labs|pharmacy, patientId?, refId? }`; server resolves the bound entities **through `PatientScopeService`**; partial or failed resolution ⇒ **409, no generation** (fail closed); one patient maximum per conversation.
  - ✅ Verify: doctor A binding doctor B's patient → 403; a deleted `refId` → 409 with nothing generated; a second distinct `patientId` on one conversation → 422.
  - **Verified, with one deliberate departure from the verify step's wording.** Doctor A binding doctor B's patient answers **404, not 403** — and that is ADR-022's requirement rather than a shortcut: a 403 confirms the patient exists, and walking `PT-1000`, `PT-1001`, … against a 403/404 split would disclose the clinic's roster without opening a chart. API §0.4 permits "403/404" precisely so the server can pick the non-disclosing one, and every patient-child route has made the same choice since M7.2. The spec asserts the refusal is **byte-identical** to a genuinely absent patient's.
  - **A deleted `refId` answers 409 with nothing generated**, asserted by counting `ai_conversations` before and after — "no generation" in its strongest available form, which is that no thread exists either. The check order is deliberate and documented: **shape** (422 — a client can fix it), then **scope** (404 — non-disclosing), then **resolution** (409 — well formed, permitted, still unbindable). Collapsing scope into resolution would be the security bug.
  - **A `refId` belonging to another patient is refused**, which is the leak the entity queries' patient clause exists to close: without it, binding another chart's `LAB-…` under a chart the caller legitimately holds would succeed.
  - **"One patient maximum" is enforced where it can actually be enforced.** A `general` thread carrying a `patientId` is 422 at creation. Beyond that, **the context is not patchable at all** — `PATCH` accepts title and favourite only, so there is no route through which a thread's patient could be moved, which is how the rule would otherwise be defeated (the history stays, the binding moves, and turns about one patient end up under another's context). A case sends `{ context }` to `PATCH` and asserts both the 422 and that the binding did not move. DB §H1's CHECK is the backstop.
  - **Re-resolution runs before every send, not only at creation.** A care assignment revoked after a thread was opened refuses the next answer — asserted by revoking mid-test. An authorization check that only ran at creation is one that expires silently.
- [x] **M16.4** — `POST /ai/conversations/{id}/messages` streaming over SSE (`text/event-stream`: token deltas, final `done` with message id/status); cancellable → status `stopped`; errors → status `error`.
  - ✅ Verify: an SSE client receives deltas then `done`; disconnecting mid-stream persists `stopped`; the 30 s client timeout is never hit because the response streams.
  - **Verified — all three halves, and the middle one found two real bugs.** Deltas arrive as deltas (three chunks in, three out — never a cumulative string, which would make the transport quadratic in the answer's length) followed by a final `done` carrying the message id and status. The question is persisted **before** anything is generated, because a question that reached the server is a question that was asked whatever the provider then does. A provider failure persists `error` **with the partial text**, since an empty thread after a failed send is indistinguishable from a send that never happened.
  - **Bug 1 — the wrong `close` event.** The handler originally aborted on `request.on('close')`. That fires when the request *body* has been fully read — about a millisecond into every single call, measured — so it cancelled every generation the instant it started. It reads like the right event and is not; the disconnect is `response.on('close')`, guarded by a `completed` flag so a normal `end()` is not read as a hang-up.
  - **Bug 2 — a `finally` that ran too late.** With the abort wired correctly the status was still `complete`, because the disconnect decision sat *after* the `for await` loop. A generator `return()`ed at a suspended `yield` — which is exactly what the controller does on disconnect — resumes only its `finally` and never the statement following the loop. Moving the decision into the `finally` fixed it. Both fixes are load-bearing: reverting either turns the test red.
  - **The route writes its own response rather than using Nest's `@Sse()`**, for three reasons it records: a context refusal has to be an ordinary 404/409 with the error envelope (under `@Sse()` the headers are written on subscribe, so the refusal would arrive as a 200 carrying an error-shaped event that no client error handler would catch); the disconnect has to reach the generator; and flushing has to be immediate. `X-Accel-Buffering: no` and `Cache-Control: no-transform` are asserted on the response — without them a proxied deployment streams perfectly in development and arrives all at once in production, which is the 30-second timeout the verify step says must never be hit.
- [x] **M16.5** — `GET /ai/prompts` (templates) + `GET/PUT /me/ai-settings` (temperature 0–1 in 0.1 steps, responseLength concise/balanced/detailed, language en/ar, autoContext, streaming, favoritePromptIds) + `POST /ai/messages/{id}/feedback` `{ rating: up|down }`.
  - ✅ Verify: settings round-trip; out-of-range temperature → 422.
  - **Verified — the round-trip, and the step as well as the range.** A saved document reads back identically. `-0.1`, `1.1` and `2` are 422. So is `0.37`: `AI_TEMPERATURES` on the frontend is an eleven-element array, and an API accepting off-step values would silently contradict its own slider. The step check is compared with a tolerance rather than an exact modulo, because `0.3 * 10` is `2.9999999999999996` in IEEE 754 — a case with its own test, since an exact test would reject a value the slider emits.
  - **An unsaved user gets the documented defaults rather than a 404** — a settings drawer opening on a 404 has nothing to render, and `DEFAULT_AI_SETTINGS` already says what such a user has. `favoritePromptIds` is the one field that cannot be transcribed as a literal: the frontend's default names two **slugs** and the column is `uuid[]`, so the ids differ per deployment and are resolved from the catalogue by slug. A favourite naming a template that does not exist is refused rather than silently dropped — a client whose list was quietly trimmed would show the star it just set disappearing.
  - **The thumbs are scoped through the owning conversation**, not by message id alone: a message id is a uuid a caller could guess at, and rating someone else's answer would write to a thread they cannot read. Only an **assistant** turn can be rated — a thumb on your own question is a rating of yourself — and all three refusals answer one 404, because distinguishing them would confirm which messages exist. `feedback` is readable back on the message, so a refresh does not show the thumb un-pressed and invite the doctor to rate the same answer twice.
- [x] **M16.6** — Containment: the AI module has **no write access to clinical tables**. Saving to a record happens only through M12.6 notes with `aiAssisted: true`.
  - ✅ Verify: the AI module's DB role/service cannot insert into `medical_records*` (test asserts the failure); a saved AI note carries permanent `aiAssisted` provenance.
  - **Verified structurally, with the database half named as an obligation rather than claimed.** The verify step names a "DB role" — a PostgreSQL role granted SELECT but not INSERT on the clinical tables. **That role does not exist: M37.3 owns the database roles**, and it is the same milestone M12's gate is still waiting on for its DELETE grant. Writing one here would be building M37 inside M16 and would leave M37 removing it. **The obligation transfers to M37.3**, recorded at that sub-item.
  - **What exists now is weaker in one respect and stronger in another, and both are stated plainly.** Weaker: a source assertion cannot stop a compromised process, which a grant can. Stronger: it fails in review, on the commit that introduces the write, and names the file. `ai-containment.spec.ts` asserts against the shipped source that no write method is called on any of seventeen clinical models; that the **only** models written are `aiConversation`, `aiMessage` and `aiUserSetting` (the positive form, so the forbidden list cannot go stale in the direction that matters); that clinical tables are reached only through `AiContextRepository`, which **has no write method at all**; and that no raw SQL is issued, since raw SQL names a table rather than a model and would pass every other rule while writing anything. Proven load-bearing by injecting `medicalRecord.create` into the module — three independent rules fail.
  - **The prompt builder is where containment meets the wire**, and has its own unit spec asserting the *negative*: no MRN (the name is already in the thread's title bar; the MRN identifies the person across systems, which is what makes it the more dangerous of the two to hand to a third party, and it adds nothing to an answer), no uuids, and the bound entity named without its contents. The model is also **told** it cannot write to the record — containment is structural, but a model that believes it is writing to the chart phrases its answers as though it had.
  - **`aiAssisted` provenance** was built and verified at **M12.4**, which asserts the flag survives signing. This milestone adds no path around it: the only route from an assistant's output into a chart remains a note a doctor wrote and signed, in the records module, behind `records.edit`.

**🔒 Exit gate M16:** streaming round-trip green; context fail-closed proven; no path exists from AI output into clinical data without a doctor-signed note.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-16: PASSED on all three conditions.** **Streaming round-trip green** — deltas then `done`, with all three terminal states (`complete`, `stopped`, `error`) driven and persisted, including the disconnect case that cost two real bug fixes. **Context fail-closed proven** — shape 422, scope 404 (byte-identical to an absent patient, ADR-022), resolution 409 with the conversation count unchanged, and re-resolution before every send so a revoked assignment stops the next answer. **No path exists from AI output into clinical data without a doctor-signed note** — asserted mechanically against the source by `ai-containment.spec.ts` and proven load-bearing by injection; the database-grant half is M37.3's, named rather than claimed. **85 new cases across M16** — 38 e2e over HTTP and 47 unit over the containment rules and the prompt builder; the full backend suite is **1893 green across 91 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean. (One pre-existing M14 spec flakes under full-suite load — see RA-M14.1 above; it passes in isolation and is unrelated to this work.) ⚠️ The frontend still reads `mock/mock-ai-service.ts` through its `service.ts` seam (D-09); wiring it is **M39.5**, and the assistant's UI half joins M13's, M14's and M15's at **M40**.

## Milestone 17: Billing (deferred — flag-gated)

**Goal:** Placeholder only — billing is `featureFlags.billing = false` and no payment entity exists in the frontend.

- [x] **M17.1** — ADR documenting the deferral + DB §13.6 table sketch (invoices, invoice_lines, payments).
  - ✅ Verify: ADR accepted.
  - **Verified — ADR-037 accepted 2026-09-16.** Two earlier decisions touched this and neither was sufficient: **ADR-010** scoped group 6 OUT with the trigger "billing turns on", and **ADR-018 §2** picked the alternate over a 404. Neither carried the *sketch* M17.1 names, and neither said what the alternate contains — so a reader knew billing was deferred and still could not implement the endpoint depending on it. ADR-037 records the three tables with their columns, types and FK actions, and marks four of them as decisions rather than defaults: `payments.invoice_id` is **RESTRICT, not CASCADE** (a received payment is a financial fact; deleting the invoice must not delete the record that money changed hands); `invoice_lines` carries three nullable provenance FKs, all SET NULL, so a line survives the order it was raised for (the treatment M5.11 gave `patient_medications.prescription_id`); `amount_paid` is stored and trigger-maintained as DB §14.5-6's **second** permitted derived-value exception, alongside `lab_orders.has_critical_result`; and voiding carries a reason, because every other destructive act here is P4. Money is `numeric(12,2)`, which DB §14.3 names explicitly as the type "if billing lands".
  - **RA-037.1 is now closed — ADR-039 accepted 2026-09-18 (product owner).** The two vocabularies are frozen: `invoice_status` is **`draft | issued | paid | cancelled`** and `payment_method` is **`cash | card | insurance`**. Four values are **rejected with reasons**: `partially-paid` (an invoice must ultimately be fully paid — a partial settlement is a fact about the payments received, not a state the invoice rests in), `void` (one act, one word — `cancelled` wins), `disputed` (the patient is told the expected cost and accepts it *before* treatment, so there is no negotiation to model), and `transfer` on the payment side. **Overdue is derived, never stored** — `status = issued AND due_at < now() AND paid_at IS NULL` — so there is no enum member, no boolean and no trigger for it; the sketch gains `paid_at timestamptz NULL`, which is the owner's own predicate transcribed rather than an addition to it. **Issuance has one source of truth**: `status` is the lifecycle, `issued_at` is the timestamp *of* the transition (`issued_at IS NULL ⟺ status = draft`), so the two can never disagree. ADR-039 also fixes what ADR-037's dependent clauses become — `voided_reason` → `cancelled_reason` (P4 intent unchanged), and `amount_paid` stands as a column while its "partially-paid filter" rationale does not. **MG §18.4 is satisfied for the day the tables land, and still no code ships**: no enum in `01-enums.prisma`, no migration, no DTO, no endpoint. ADR-010 group 6 remains OUT and `isBillingEnabled()` still returns `false`.
  - **Insurance and refunds are constrained, not implemented.** Insurance is a legitimate payer category and is deliberately **not** an immediate patient payment — settling a claim and taking a card at the desk are different events. **No coverage percentage is approved for Medora** (not 80%, 90%, 95%, or any other): coverage depends on the policy and plan, the covered service, network rules, authorization and the applicable terms. Refunds return to the patient and **respect the original payment source** — a card refund goes back to the same card subject to the provider's capabilities, never an arbitrary destination; small cash refunds may be returned in cash and large ones by transfer to a verified bank account, with the threshold **TBD**. Full card numbers, CVV and PINs are **never** stored; provider tokens and non-sensitive metadata are the permitted representation. The invoice-total / insurance-responsibility / patient-responsibility / authorization / claim / settlement split is recorded as **future design only**, and the conceptual figures in ADR-039 §6 are illustrative, not rates.
  - **What the original open item said, retained.** One item was left open rather than invented: RA-037.1. The sketch needs two new enums, `invoice_status` and `payment_method`. MG §18.4 requires enums frozen *before* the schema, and **no source states these vocabularies** — so the values in the ADR are its proposal, not a transcription, and must be confirmed with a product owner before the tables are created. Recording that is the point of the sketch; guessing quietly would be the failure.
- [x] **M17.2** — Revenue widget endpoints return the flag-off alternate data (API §12.1 note).
  - ✅ Verify: `category=revenue` with flag off → **the alternate payload, not 404 and not 500**. (API §12.1 says "404/flag-off alternate"; the M0 ADR picks the alternate payload — the frontend renders alternates, it does not handle a 404 here.)
  - **Verified — 11 cases in `reports-revenue-alternate.e2e-spec.ts`.** `GET /reports/analytics?category=revenue` answers **200** with the alternate; the verify step's two negatives are asserted literally alongside it. The alternate's content is the contract's rather than invented: UX §2.1 states the doctor Overview's rule directly — the revenue card is "visible only when the clinic enables per-doctor billing; otherwise the slot shows **'Top visit types'** instead" — and the admin equivalent (UX §3.1's Storage & Usage) already ships on the frontend. The payload **names which alternate to render** rather than leaving a client to infer it from the payload's shape, so two clients cannot choose differently.
  - **`billingEnabled` is on the payload rather than implied by absence.** A client inferring the flag from missing figures would render an empty chart on a deployment whose billing is *on* but whose month was quiet; the flag distinguishes the two states. The counts are the caller's own (API §12's "doctor scope = own data only"), asserted with a colleague who sees none — and an account with no `doctors` row gets an empty list at 200, because an empty practice is not an error.
  - **The flag is a constant with one reader, mirroring the frontend**, and `billing-flag.ts` records why it is not a `platform_settings` row: the frontend's own header says the flag "has no consumer, so flipping it to `true` today does nothing", and a switch in an admin panel with nothing behind it is worse than an honest constant, because a setting that appears in a panel implies something happens when it changes. M24 moves it when there is something to govern. Every caller goes through `isBillingEnabled()`, so that move is an edit to one function body.
  - **The other eight categories answer 501 `not_implemented`, not a half-built snapshot.** They need the full `AnalyticsSnapshot` — eleven members, ten unrelated to billing — and **M21** owns it; building them here would be M21 arriving early under M17's name, and M21 would then be replacing code rather than writing it. 501 is the honest status: the route exists and the category is a real part of the contract. A 404 would deny the category is part of the API, a 422 would blame the client, and an empty snapshot would show a doctor a dashboard of zeros as though it were their practice. A category outside the contract's nine is still 422.

**🔒 Exit gate M17:** nothing built ahead of product need; contract slot reserved.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-16: PASSED on both conditions, and the first one is the harder.** **Nothing built ahead of product need** — no billing table exists, no billing entity, no admin switch, and eight of nine analytics categories are explicitly unimplemented rather than stubbed. What was added is the smallest thing that answers a request the contract already permits a client to make. **The contract slot is reserved** with a real answer behind it: 200, self-describing, naming the alternate and the flag's state, carrying the doctor's own practice mix. M21 extends rather than replaces — it fills the 501s and keeps this alternate for `revenue` while the flag is off. **11 new cases**; the full backend suite is **1893 green across 91 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean. (One pre-existing M14 spec flakes under full-suite load — see RA-M14.1 above; it passes in isolation and is unrelated to this work.)

## Milestone 18: Payments (deferred with M17)

- [x] **M18.1** — Same treatment as M17 — ADR only; re-plan when billing turns on.
  - ✅ Verify: ADR accepted.
  - **Verified — ADR-038 accepted 2026-09-16, and M18 ships no code.** "Same treatment as M17" resolves to *less* code here, not the same amount, and the reason is the decision itself. M17 produced an ADR **and** a small endpoint because API §12.1 already lets a client ask for `category=revenue` — a contract slot existed, so something had to answer it. Payments has no such slot. Audited across every surface: **zero** payment or invoice mentions in `BACKEND_API_CONTRACT.md`, **zero** in `UX_ARCHITECTURE.md`, **zero** in `frontend/src/**` (the "Stripe" hits are settings-nav *layout* references and "refund policy" is a message-flagging keyword), and one row in `patient-app/PATIENT_APP_AUDIT.md` that explicitly excludes it (OOS-4). There is no request a client can make that would go unanswered, so there is nothing to reserve — and building a surface anyway would be speculative architecture with no consumer, which this milestone's own gate forbids.
  - **The ledger is not re-decided; the processing is deliberately not sketched.** ADR-037 already carries the `payments` table as part of DB §13 group 6, so ADR-038 does not restate it — two sketches of one table can drift. What ADR-038 adds is the distinction M17 had no reason to draw: that table records **that money was received**, and nothing anywhere records **how money is taken**. Six decisions are enumerated and left open as **RA-038.1** — processor/PSP, PCI DSS scope (hosted fields versus card data on our own origin is a security-posture decision with audit consequences), refunds and the authority to issue them, webhook idempotency, settlement and reconciliation, and multi-currency. The ledger could be sketched because DB §13 and §14.3 constrain it; processing has no upstream source at all, so a sketch would be invention rather than transcription.
  - **RA-037.1 was untouched by M18, and is now closed by ADR-039 (2026-09-18).** The vocabularies are decided, and notably **not** the ones ADR-037 proposed — declining to promote them was the right call. The re-planning order in ADR-038 §5 therefore advances by exactly one step: RA-037.1 is answered, **RA-038.1 is not**, and a new **RA-039.1** carries fifteen rows (refund approval authority, refund reason vocabulary, cash refund threshold, insurance coverage/benefit rules, insurance authorization and claim state machines, refund ledger shape, partial-settlement representation, concurrency control, `Idempotency-Key` implementation, and RA-038.1's five processor rows re-confirmed open). **No migration may be written until those close.**
  - **The design-readiness review found two real gaps, both named rather than deferred silently.** ADR-039's audit confirms the substrate is ready where it matters — money as `numeric(12,2)` with `@db.Decimal` already in routine use, the `AuditEvent` contract fixed ahead of M28 over partitioned `audit_events` (ADR-024), `$transaction` in use across eight modules, `payments.reference` as the processor seam, and `patient_insurance`/`insurance_providers` already IN per ADR-010 group 5 (they record *who* the insurer is, not what it owes). **Gap 1: idempotency.** `BACKEND_API_CONTRACT.md` §0.5 promises "state-changing POSTs accept `Idempotency-Key`; replays return the original result" and **nothing in `backend/src/**` implements it** — ADR-014 assigns it per-endpoint and no owning milestone has shipped one. A payment endpoint is the first place a replayed POST charges twice, so this must land *before* any payment write. **Gap 2: concurrency.** No row-level lock, explicit isolation level or optimistic version column exists anywhere in the source or schema, so `amount_paid`'s trigger and any balance check would be read-modify-write races. Both are RA-039.1 rows.
  - **The vocabulary audit came back clean.** Swept 2026-09-18 across backend source, Prisma schema, migrations, seeds, tests, DTOs, services, repositories and the API contract: **no** `partially-paid`, **no** `void`, **no** `disputed`, **no** stored `overdue`, **no** billing `transfer`, and **no** hard-coded coverage percentage anywhere. The only occurrences of the rejected vocabulary in the whole repository were ADR-037 §2's own sketch — a labelled proposal with no code behind it, now superseded and annotated in place. (The `overdue` hits in `dashboard/services/action-queue.ordering.ts` are the action queue's waiting-time prose; the `transfer` hits are a MIME `Content-Transfer-Encoding` header and the AI containment rule. Neither is billing.)
  - **What M18 itself recorded, retained.** RA-037.1 is untouched *by this milestone*. `invoice_status` and `payment_method` remain open and awaiting the product owner. ADR-038 does not promote ADR-037's proposed values, extend them, or add a third enum — MG §18.4 requires those vocabularies frozen *before* any schema, so they are confirmed rather than assumed.

**🔒 Exit gate M18:** documented deferral.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-16: PASSED.** The deferral is documented in ADR-038, and the gate is met **by the absence of code rather than in spite of it** — no table, no endpoint, no DTO, no feature flag of its own. The deferral is also now falsifiable: RA-038.1 is a six-row checklist, so anyone proposing to build payments can be asked which rows have been answered and by whom. No test was added, because no behaviour changed; the suite, typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` were re-run to confirm the documentation-only change regressed nothing.

## Milestone 19: Notifications

**Goal:** Event fan-out + feed + preferences (API §10).

- [x] **M19.1** — Transactional domain-event outbox → BullMQ consumer.
  - ✅ Verify: event rows commit with the domain transaction; rollback test shows no orphan events.
  - **Verified — `domain_events` lands and the M2 stub retires.** ADR-019 §2 accepted a log-and-discard outbox because nothing consumed it yet, and recorded under *Cost carried forward* that the replacement owes "a writer that enlists in the caller's transaction". `PrismaOutbox` is that writer. `OutboxPort` gains `publishWithin(tx, message)`; the old `publish` stays for the M6–M16 call sites that publish after their own write has already committed, and its doc comment now says plainly that it opens its own transaction and what that costs.
  - **Only `publish` catches its own errors.** A `publishWithin` that swallowed one would commit the domain rows without the event and silently reintroduce the bug the table removes — so it propagates, and the caller rolls back. Both halves are asserted: the rollback case by a thrown error, by a failing later statement (a real FK violation, not a stand-in), and by making the outbox write itself fail.
  - **`dedupe_key` is UNIQUE over a nullable column**, which is exactly what at-least-once delivery needs: a message naming a key is deduplicated, one that does not never is. The write is `createMany` with `skipDuplicates`, so a legitimate retry collapses rather than poisoning the transaction it sits in — asserted by republishing inside a transaction that then commits other work. `status` is closed by a CHECK because a fix-up script writing a fourth value would be skipped by the drain forever.
  - **The first real call site moved with it.** `lab_result.critical` is published *inside* the result-entry transaction, from the trigger's own answer. An alert published after commit can outlive a rollback of the results it announces, and a clinician would be paged about a value no chart holds. The repository takes an `emit` callback rather than the outbox itself — it owns the transaction, not the decision about what to publish.
  - **⚠️ The BullMQ half is M30.1's and is not built.** That milestone owns the queue registry, retries and dead-lettering; nothing here adds a queue client or a timer. **11 cases** in `outbox-transactional.e2e-spec.ts`.
- [x] **M19.2** — Notification creation rules per category/priority incl. critical pinning (pinned until acted on).
  - ✅ Verify: a critical lab result creates a pinned critical notification.
  - **Verified end to end** — a potassium of 7.2 through `POST /lab-orders/{id}/results` becomes a pinned, `critical`, `laboratory` notification addressed to the ordering doctor, with nothing stubbed between the two.
  - **The rules table is transcribed, and its gaps are listed rather than filled.** Every row names the source that says a notification happens and who receives it: API §5.4 ("patient notified after commit"), §5.5, §5.10 ("both parties notified"), §3.2 ("admin notification"), §8.7 with UX 2.9. Eleven topics have a rule. **Ten deliberately do not** and sit in `UNSOURCED_TOPICS` — no source says who hears about a completed appointment or a signed note, and inventing a recipient is how a clinician ends up with a feed they stop reading. An event with no rule is claimed, counted and dropped; that is not an error, because publishing an event and choosing to page someone are different decisions.
  - **Priority is the field almost nothing states.** `critical` appears in exactly one source — UX 2.9, quoted on the `pinned` column — so `lab_result.critical` is the only critical rule and **pinning is derived from priority** rather than set as a second switch that could disagree with it. The frontend's `mock-data.ts` assigns priority with a weighted random and pins by `index % 23`; it is a real source for the category×kind pairings and not for priorities, and the file says so. **Grading the other ten rules above `normal` would be inventing the product rule this milestone was careful not to invent.**
  - **Correctness does not depend on there being one backend process.** Events are claimed with `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` inside the statement that marks them, so two instances draining concurrently cannot both page the same doctor — driven by two overlapping drains, not argued. There is no in-flight set in process memory that a second replica would not share. Raw SQL because Prisma cannot express it, which is ADR-023 §4's rule exactly.
  - **`drain()` is deliberately not on a timer.** A `setInterval` in a request-serving process is the ad-hoc periodic behaviour **M30.1**'s exit gate forbids; that milestone owns the schedule. **15 cases** in `notification-fanout.e2e-spec.ts` plus the three exit-gate cases below.
- [x] **M19.3** — Feed endpoints (filters, read/unread/archive/favorite/pin, read-all, clear-read) + `GET /notifications/badge-counts` (Redis-cached).
  - ✅ Verify: badge endpoint p95 < 50 ms under a 100-client polling test.
  - **The endpoints are complete; the verify step's own subject is not, and it is M31's.** `/archive`, `/favorite`, `/pin` (§10.2), `clear-read` (§10.3), the `q` filter (§10.1) and `/summary` (§10.6) all ship. **`badge-counts` is served from two indexed aggregates on every poll — not from Redis.** RM **M31.1** names "badge counters" among its caches, and the 100-client p95 belongs with the cache it measures; asserting a latency budget against a database read here would be measuring the wrong thing and would pass or fail on the test machine's disk. **Carried to M31.1 as RA-M19.1.**
  - **2026-11-05: now served from Redis** (M31.1, `badge-counts`), invalidated by all nine writers; see `docs/CACHING.md`.
  - **The row actions are explicit, not toggles.** They take `{ value }` defaulting to `true`. §10.2 calls the client optimistic with "rollback on failure", and a toggle cannot be rolled back safely: two clicks racing settle on whichever state the second write computed, and the undo would then flip a value the client never saw. Each route writes exactly one column, so "mark favorite" cannot silently unpin — asserted directly.
  - **`clear-read` archives rather than deletes**, and answers how many rows it moved. The frontend port names it `archiveAllRead` and returns "how many it moved" because the toast reports a number; a delete would make that toast the record of something irrecoverable.
  - **Every write stays scoped by `recipientId`**, so someone else's notification and a missing one answer the same 404 — and the stranger's row is asserted untouched afterwards, because a 404 that still wrote would be worse than one that leaked.
- [x] **M19.4** — Preferences endpoint: channel×category matrix; `categories.security` locked true (422 otherwise); quiet hours never suppress critical lab alerts.
  - ✅ Verify: both rule tests.
  - **Verified — both rules, each where it belongs.** `categories.security` is `z.literal(true)`, so switching it off is a 422 through ordinary validation, with DB §G7's CHECK underneath; both are asserted, because the column's own comment explains why the rule is deliberately in two places — "a UI-only guarantee is one API client away from a user who never learns their password was changed". The service adds no third copy to drift.
  - **Quiet hours are not validated at the preferences endpoint at all**, and that is the decision. The rule is about the moment a notification is *sent*, so it lives in `channel-selection.ts` where the dispatcher applies it: the user may set any window they like, and what the window may not do is silence a `critical` notification. It is expressed against **priority rather than the laboratory category** — a critical alert that waited until 07:00 is the failure the rule prevents, whatever raised it — and an `important` lab alert is still quiet-hours material. Urgency overrides the clock, never the user's channel choice.
  - **A read with no saved row returns the column defaults rather than 404**, the same choice M16.5 made for `/me/ai-settings`: a settings screen opening on a 404 has nothing to render. Writes `upsert`, so two tabs saving together do not race a primary-key violation between an existence check and an insert. **23 cases** in `notification-preferences.e2e-spec.ts`, covering the midnight-wrapping window, a non-wrapping one, an empty one and the boundary minutes, plus 13 unit cases in `channel-selection.spec.ts`.
- [x] **M19.5** — Channel workers: email (SMTP from platform slice), SMS stub, push stub; `notification_deliveries` tracking rows.
  - ✅ Verify: mailhog receives an email for an email-enabled category; a delivery row records per-channel status.
  - **Verified off the wire.** The harness's `SmtpSink` is a real SMTP server on a loopback port, so "an email was sent" is read from the DATA payload rather than from a spy. Every channel gets a row and every row gets a status with its reason.
  - **Three things this got wrong first, each worth recording.** (1) **`$queryRaw` does not map enums back**, so `channel` arrives as `inApp`, not `IN_APP` — comparing against the member name type-checks, and would have left every email unsent while the run reported success. (2) **`delivered_at` is governed by an equivalence, not an implication**: `(status = 'delivered') = (delivered_at IS NOT NULL)`. Stamping a timestamp on a `sent` row is rejected by the database, and that is the right rule. (3) It follows that **email accepted by a relay is `sent`, never `delivered`** — the relay took it, the mailbox has not acknowledged it — while **in-app genuinely is `delivered`**, because the notification row *is* the delivery and there is no provider in between. `provider_ref` stores the relay's message id so a future delivery report can move a row on.
  - **The status CHECK gains `sending`**, because a claim needs a non-terminal state to move rows to: once the lock is released, a claimed row is otherwise indistinguishable from an unclaimed one and the worker is back to a read-modify-write race. It is deliberately not terminal — a row left in it by a crashed worker has an unknown outcome, which is exactly what M30.1's retry policy acts on. The frontend's `DeliveryStatus` union is already a subset of this vocabulary (it has no `skipped`) and no endpoint projects the column, so nothing on the wire changed.
  - **SMS, push and desktop record `skipped` with the reason `no-provider`.** A stub marking them `sent` would be a lie an administrator could not detect; `failed` would send someone to look at a healthy relay. `Mailer` gains `trySend`, which reports the outcome rather than swallowing it — ADR-025's own header anticipated this ("M19 makes delivery durable") — while `send` keeps its fire-and-forget contract for auth mail and delegates to it, so the two cannot drift in what they put on the wire. **8 cases**, including two overlapping runs sending exactly one email.

**🔒 Exit gate M19:** approving an appointment produces a notification only after commit; bell badge live in both shells.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-18: PASSED on the server half; the shell half is M40's, as M13–M16 already found.** **A notification is produced** — `appointment.confirmed` reaches the patient as an `appointments` notification, driven end to end through a linked `patients.user_id`. **Only after commit**, and the mechanism is structural rather than a flag the sender checks: a rolled-back approval leaves no `domain_events` row, so there is nothing to claim and nothing can be sent. A patient record with no account is silence, not a failure — ADR-030 links a record to an account where one exists and does not require one. ⚠️ **"Bell badge live in both shells" cannot be met here**: the doctor shell reads `notifications/model/mock-data.ts` through its service seam and the patient app has no live feed wired, so the badge's *endpoint* is live and tested while its rendering joins M13's, M14's, M15's and M16's at **M40** (wiring is M39.5). **1963 green across 96 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean — up from the 1893-across-91 baseline, and the pre-existing M14 spec (RA-M14.1) passed in this run too.

#### RA-M19.1 — the badge latency budget belongs with its cache

**Status 2026-11-05: the cache exists** (M31.1, `badge-counts`). The latency budget itself is M32.1/M32.2's load test.

M19.3's verify step asks for "p95 < 50 ms under a 100-client polling test" on `GET /notifications/badge-counts`. The endpoint ships and is O(1)-cheap by construction — two indexed aggregates returning integers, no row loaded — but **the Redis counter the budget assumes is M31.1's**, which names "badge counters" among the caches it owns. Measuring a p95 against a database read on whichever machine CI happens to run would assert the disk, not the design, and would have to be re-measured the moment the cache lands. **Carried to M31.1**, where the cache and the number that justifies it arrive together.

## Milestone 20: Messaging

**Goal:** Internal messaging (API §9).

> ⚠ *Multi-Hospital (2026-10-07):* threads are hospital-scoped and participant-checked (AF-16); cross-hospital consultation (OD-13) never bypasses grants, Break-Glass, audit, employment or revocation. `BACKEND_CHECKLIST.md` §E–§F.

- [x] **M20.1** — Conversations/participants/messages with folder semantics (per-participant unread/star/pin/archive/mute/draft).
  - ✅ Verify: folder queries match `MessagingFolder` semantics (tests per folder).
  - **Verified — one test per folder, and the semantics are transcribed rather than designed.** `messaging/model/selectors.ts`'s `inFolder` is the source: five of the six folders are "not archived, and …", which is what makes archiving *filing* rather than another flag. **`sent` is the one the database cannot answer alone** — it asks whether the **last** message is the caller's, which is a property of the page that was read. The repository narrows to threads the caller has written in and the final predicate runs over the loaded page; the reported total is the narrowed one, because promising pages that render empty is worse than a smaller number. Asserted both ways: a reply from the other participant takes the thread *out* of the author's Sent.
  - **Per-viewer state is asserted to be per-viewer.** Starring, archiving and drafting are checked from the other participant's side each time, because DB §14.5's reason for putting them on `conversation_participants` is exactly that a draft on the thread row would be visible to everyone in it.
  - **Two authorization questions, both asked.** `can:messages.send` decides whether messaging exists for a role; the `conversation_participants` join decides which threads. A doctor holding the capability still gets **404** on a colleague's thread — 404 and not 403, because a 403 confirms the thread exists and these threads are between named people about named patients (ADR-022's rule). The membership lookup is the composite primary key, so the security property and the fast path are one clause.
  - **The thread kind is derived, not taken from the client** — a `direct` thread with five participants is a state no reader expects — and the author is added without being asked, because a client that had to include itself would eventually forget and create a thread invisible to everyone including its author.
- [x] **M20.2** — Send with delivery-state progression (sent→delivered→read) + failed-retry.
  - ✅ Verify: read receipt updates ✓✓ state and unreadCount.
  - **Verified, and the concurrency half is driven rather than argued.** Unread counts move by `{ increment: 1 }` — one atomic UPDATE. Reading the count and writing back `count + 1` is the read-modify-write race that loses a message every time two people answer at once, and the loss is *invisible* because the badge is merely one too low; two concurrent sends assert it does not happen.
  - **Reading "up to" a message recomputes rather than zeroes.** A blanket zero would silently mark the newer messages seen; the test reads up to the first of three and asserts two remain unread.
  - **A read receipt is never given for your own message.** Opening a thread moves `sent → delivered` — the moment the client demonstrably has it — and `read` needs the explicit §9.6 call, because scrolling past a message is not the reader saying they saw it. Marking your own message read would show a receipt nobody gave, and the test asserts `receipted: 0`.
  - **The send is one transaction**: the message row, every other participant's badge and the thread's `last_activity_at`. Split apart, a crash between them leaves a thread whose preview will not sort to the top, or a badge for a message that was never written.
- [x] **M20.3** — Patient-thread rules: delete → 405; edit ≤5 min with `editedAt`; history retained.
  - ✅ Verify: edit at minute 6 → 403/409; delete attempt → 405.
  - **Verified — and the verify step's "403/409" resolves to 409, deliberately.** The message exists and the caller may edit it in principle; what expired is the *moment*. That is a state conflict, not a permission (ADR-013: "a stale document is 409"). A 403 would tell an author they are not allowed to edit their own message, which is false.
  - **The window is policy read at edit time, not a CHECK.** The schema's own comment records why: a constraint would make yesterday's messages illegal the moment an administrator widened the window. `EDIT_WINDOW_MS` is the one place it lives, and M24 moves it to platform settings.
  - **Delete answers 405, and membership is checked first** so a non-participant still gets 404 — a 405 would confirm the thread exists. 405 rather than 403 is the contract's own choice and the honest one: 403 says "not you", inviting an administrator to try; 405 says the *method* does not exist here. The history is asserted intact afterwards, including `deleted_at` still null.
  - **A message id from another thread is 404, not 403.** A message id is a uuid a caller could guess at, and confirming it belongs elsewhere is an oracle on threads they cannot read.
- [x] **M20.4** — Attachments with scan gate (download 403 `scan_pending` until clean).
  - ✅ Verify: gate test.
  - **Verified — the gate ships; uploading does not, and the reason is structural.** The check is an explicit `scannedAt === null` refusal rather than a truthiness test on a nullable timestamp: the column's comment says null means *not yet scanned*, which the download path must not read as clean, and the two readings differ on exactly that case. The refusal is **403 `scan_pending`** as §9.10 spells it — a 404 would send a clinician looking for a file they can see in the thread. **Membership is checked before the gate**, so a non-participant gets 404 and the gate's own message never confirms the attachment exists.
  - **The route answers a short-TTL signed URL, not the bytes** — ADR-019 §2's reasoning: streaming every attachment through the API turns the app into a file server and puts PHI in response timings for no benefit.
  - **✅ Upload path completed, and a binding defect fixed (2026-11-05) — RA-M20.1's two blockers are gone** (M25 stores, M25.3 scans).
    - **The defect.** `attachmentIds` on `POST /conversations/{id}/messages` was applied as `UPDATE message_attachments SET message_id = <new> WHERE id IN (…)` — by id alone. `message_attachments.message_id` is `NOT NULL`, so every such row already belonged to a message: a participant holding any attachment's id could **move it out of another thread into their own** and download it there. No row could be created through the API at all.
    - **The fix**, by the rule M12.5 already wrote down ("the bytes go through `POST /files` … this route links the upload, the shape messaging uses for `attachmentIds`"): `attachmentIds` are **file ids** of `POST /files` uploads of kind `message_attachment`. The send creates the `message_attachments` rows in its own transaction, under `FOR UPDATE` on the files, and accepts only the author's own, live, never-attached uploads; anything else is one **422 on `attachmentIds`** and the whole send rolls back. No migration.
    - **§9.10's `POST /conversations/{id}/attachments` is served by that path rather than by a second upload route** — one upload path, as M12.5 decided for records, so per-kind validation, the checksum, the byte-signature check and the scan queue live in one place. The download (`GET /attachments/{id}/download`) is unchanged.
    - Verified: `test/message-attachments.e2e-spec.ts` (7) — upload → send → `403 scan_pending` → clean → signed URL for the file's own key; another account's upload, another kind, a re-send, a duplicate, an unknown and a malformed id are each 422 with nothing sent; **the cross-thread re-bind is refused and the attachment stays on its message**; two concurrent sends of one upload are one 201 and one 422. And live, through MinIO and clamd (M25.4).
  - **⚠️ Uploading is M25's.** *(Original entry, superseded above.)* `POST …/attachments` needs `FILE_STORAGE.put`, whose stub rejects every call with 503 until then — deliberately, per ADR-019 §2 ("a `put()` that resolved without writing would hand back a file id for bytes that do not exist"). **And the scanner is M25.3**: nothing in this module may set `scanned_at`, or the gate would be a door with its own key taped to it. A test drives a *scanned* attachment through to that 503, which is the proof the gate opened. See **RA-M20.1**.
- [x] **M20.5** — Own-thread full-text search.
  - ✅ Verify: search hits only the caller's threads.
  - **Verified — and the scope is in the query, not applied to its results.** The predicate is `conversation.participants.some({ userId })` on the `Message` query itself, so a message in a thread the caller is not in is never loaded. A search that read every message and then dropped the ones it should not have seen is one refactor away from leaking them. `messages_body_trgm_idx` is the GIN index the `contains` runs on. A one-character query is 422 rather than a scan a caller can ask for repeatedly.
- [ ] **M20.6** — Presence (Redis TTL heartbeat, coarse online/away/offline).
  - ✅ Verify: TTL expiry flips presence.
  - **Status 2026-10-24: still not built — the blocker below is gone, and a different one stands.** The project now has a Redis client: `RedisPermissionCache` (M7.1, AF-25) and BullMQ (M30.1) both run on the validated `REDIS_URL`, so "there is no Redis client" is no longer true. What remains is the **consumer**. Presence is a field of API §9.9 `GET /contacts` — "directory (role, department, presence, lastActiveAt, favorite)" — and **that route is not implemented and has no roadmap item**. Building it is not transcription: `favorite` has no table in the schema (a schema addition), and a staff directory has to honour the existing *profile visibility in clinic directory* privacy preference (UX 2.11), whose effect on who appears is not specified. The contract also names no heartbeat route. A TTL key nothing writes and nothing reads would satisfy the verify step and serve nobody. **Needs:** a roadmap item for §9.9 with the favourites table and the visibility rule decided; presence then lands with it.
  - **🚫 BLOCKED on M31 — not built, and not faked.** *(Original entry, kept for the record; superseded by the status above.)* The verify step *is* the Redis TTL: "TTL expiry flips presence". There is no Redis client in the project — `shared/authz/in-memory-permission-cache.ts` says so in its own header, and **M31** owns the Redis layer. A per-process `Map` would satisfy a single-instance test and then report a colleague offline to whichever replica did not hold their heartbeat, which is worse than no presence at all: a doctor would see "offline" and choose not to message someone who is sitting at their desk. **Carried to M31.1** as RA-M20.2.
- [x] **M20.7** — Admin oversight: metadata logs (patient masked), content-access request gate (justification → policy → optional second-admin approval → watermarked read-only view + audit ref), safety-flags queue workflow (Open→Under review→Resolved, P4).
  - ✅ Verify: content view without an active grant → 403; every granted view writes an audit row with a ref. ⚠️ No frontend consumer — ADM-06 is unbuilt (M39.6).
  - **Verified on both halves. This milestone is transcription, because ADR-006 decided it in full** — mechanism *and* default — after the project owner exercised authority the documents could not supply.
  - **Content access is a grant, not a capability.** `communications.viewContent` is necessary and not sufficient (ADR-006 §1). The three capabilities are applied separately per route, and the seeded matrix proves the separation is real rather than asserted: `doctor` holds `viewMetadata` and neither `viewContent` nor `resolveFlags`, so a doctor reaches the metadata list and is refused the other two. Guarding the controller with one capability would have collapsed that, and the test that would have caught it is written the way round that catches it.
  - **The audit write and the content read share one transaction**, because ADR-006 §5 requires it in those words and names the consequence: if the audit write fails, no content is returned. It is written on **every** read, not once per grant — the test reads twice and counts two. A refused read writes nothing.
  - **Expired, denied and another administrator's grant are each "no grant".** The control is that *this* person justified *this* access, so grants do not lend.
  - **Issuing a grant fails closed, and that is the honest state.** ADR-006 §4 requires an expiry; **RA-006.3** records that no source gives a duration, names the 15-minute PHI-reveal window only as a *precedent*, and says whether it suits message content "is a product question. Not decided." So `COMMUNICATION_GRANT_TTL_MINUTES` has no default and the endpoint answers **503 naming the ADR**. Choosing a number here would be inventing the most privacy-sensitive timeout in the platform on no evidence — and the platform already fails closed on far less (a prescription cannot be sent when the interaction service is merely unreachable). The suite asserts the 503 and creates grants directly to exercise the gate.
  - **⚠️ The audit row is a dispatcher call, not an `audit_events` row.** `LoggingAuditDispatcher` is still the M2 stub — ADR-019 records that "M28 must replace it with a writer that enlists in the caller's transaction" — so there is no table to count yet. What ADR-006 §5 actually requires, that no code path returns content without an audit write in the same transaction, is a property of the *call*, and that is what is asserted. The day M28 lands these assertions become assertions about rows without changing what they mean.
  - **Defect found and fixed (2026-10-23): the transaction claimed above did not hold once M28 landed.** M28 bound the dispatcher to `PrismaAuditWriter`, which writes on its *own* connection and never throws (RA-M28.2). Called inside this `$transaction` callback it joined nothing and swallowed a failure — so a ledger that refused the row still let the bodies out with a 200. Reproduced by restoring the old call: `expected 200 to be 503`, with the message text in the response. The view now writes through `TransactionalAuditWriter` on the read's transaction; a refused insert rolls back and answers **503 `audit_unavailable`** with no content. `messaging-oversight.e2e-spec.ts` now counts real `audit_events` rows instead of dispatcher calls (the substitution the bullet above anticipated) and drives the refusal against Postgres.
  - **Second-admin approval is policy and both modes are built** (owner decision, 2026-08-12, closing RA-006.1). Self-approval is refused whichever mode is on — it follows from the two-person purpose. The env layer is what **M24.2** puts a platform setting over (RA-006.2).
  - **Resolving a flag is compare-and-set.** Two administrators resolving at once produce one write and one **409**, rather than the second silently overwriting the first's note — asserted, with the first note still in place afterwards.
  - **Open owner decision (raised 2026-11-14, API contract audit) — not changed.** `GET /admin/conversations` (API §9.11, which the contract titles "Admin oversight") is guarded by `communications.viewMetadata`, and the seeded matrix grants that capability to **doctor** and **reception** as well as admin (`shared/authz/permission-catalogue.ts`, transcribed from `frontend/src/types/auth` and parity-tested). So a doctor or a receptionist can list the metadata (participants, patient masked, counts) of every conversation on the platform, not only their own; the payload audit observed a doctor reading 12 rows. Whether the matrix or the contract's "admin" label is right is the owner's call; the owner instructed on 2026-11-14 that the matrix is not to be changed here.

**🔒 Exit gate M20:** messaging center fully live; no audit-free path to content.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-18: PASSED on the second condition; the first is met on the server for six of seven sub-items.** **No audit-free path to content** — the only route that returns message bodies is `GET /admin/conversations/{id}/content`, it requires an active grant, and its audit write shares the read's transaction. Metadata never selects a body; the flags queue reads the stored excerpt rather than the message. **"Messaging center fully live"** holds for threads, folders, receipts, the edit window, the 405, search, the download gate and oversight — and does **not** hold for presence (M20.6, blocked on Redis/M31) or attachment upload (M25). ⚠️ The frontend half is unchanged: `messaging/model/*` is still a mock seam and ADM-06 is unbuilt, so wiring is **M39.6** and rendering joins M13–M16's at **M40**. **2034 green across 98 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean.

#### RA-M20.1 — attachment upload needs M25, and the scanner needs M25.3

**✅ Closed 2026-11-05** — see M20.4.

The gate is built and tested; the two things on either side of it are not. `FILE_STORAGE.put` rejects with 503 until **M25**, so `POST /conversations/{id}/attachments` is not routed — a 503 that reads like an outage is worse than an absent route. `scanned_at` is set by the malware scan **M25.3** owns, and no code in the messaging module writes it, which is what keeps the gate from holding its own key.

#### RA-M20.2 — presence is blocked on Redis, and a per-process stand-in is worse than nothing

M20.6's verify step is the TTL itself. No Redis client exists (`in-memory-permission-cache.ts` records the same gap for M7.1's cache), and **M31** owns the layer. A per-process map passes a single-instance test and then tells half the replicas that an online colleague is offline — a doctor would decline to message someone sitting at their desk. **Carried to M31.1**, with the heartbeat and the cache arriving together.

## Milestone 21: Reports

**Goal:** Analytics (API §12).

- [x] **M21.1** — Per-category aggregate endpoints (range/compare/filters, deltas vs comparison period).
  - ✅ Verify: snapshot shapes validated per `CategoryView`; doctor scope = own data only (cross-doctor test fails).
  - **Verified on both halves, and the scope half is driven with two doctors** — one books three appointments and orders a lab, the other books nothing and must see zeroes. Doctor scope is a predicate on every query rather than a filter afterwards: a snapshot assembled clinic-wide and trimmed later is one refactor from showing a colleague's numbers. Patients are counted **distinct**, so three visits by one person is one patient rather than three.
  - **`CATEGORY_VIEW` drives the work.** It names, per category, which charts and KPIs belong to it — "selecting a category focuses the page on ONLY that domain's analytics" — so asking for `laboratory` runs the lab queries and not the heatmap. Unrequested members come back **empty rather than absent**, because `AnalyticsSnapshot` requires all eleven and an absent member would make every consumer test for undefined on a field its own type says exists.
  - **One field is deliberately missing from the wire shape: `KpiDatum.icon`.** It is typed `LucideIcon` — a React component reference — which cannot cross a wire and has no server-side meaning. The stable `id` is what `CATEGORY_VIEW`'s `kpiIds` already name, and the client maps it to an icon the way it maps a category to one today.
  - **Windows are half-open, `[from, to)`.** A closed interval double-counts the boundary instant, so a row landing exactly on midnight would appear in both this period and the comparison one and inflate both sides of a delta. The comparison period is the same length immediately before — anything else makes a percentage meaningless, since a part-month against a whole one shows every KPI collapsing on the first. Growth from zero answers **100, not Infinity**; a chip rendering `Infinity` is a defect.
  - **`compare=true` decides whether to pay for the second window**, because the deltas double the query count and a server that always paid would make the common case twice as slow for a number the page was not showing.
- [x] **M21.2** — Insights computation (7 kinds).
  - ✅ Verify: seeded scenario yields expected insights.
  - **Verified — and nothing here has a threshold.** No source says *when* an insight is worth showing; "flag a workload rise above 20%" is a product rule and none exists. So each insight reports what the data says with its direction taken from the sign of the movement, and a card reading "unchanged" is a true and useful statement. A card that appeared only above an invented cutoff would be a product decision made in a service file.
  - **Critical results invert the tone** — any unreviewed critical result is `error` and none is `success` — which is not a threshold but the same rule the lab module enforces everywhere else (UX 2.9's pinning). **Revenue says billing is unavailable rather than reporting zero**, because a zero is indistinguishable from a quiet month on a clinic that bills; the same reasoning ADR-037 §3 applied to the revenue category.
- [x] **M21.3** — Saved reports CRUD (filter JSONB snapshots).
  - ✅ Verify: round-trip restores identical filters.
  - **Verified — and the document is *validated* JSONB, not arbitrary JSON.** A filter snapshot is replayed into the analytics endpoint when the report is opened, so a document naming a category the server does not know would fail later, at read time, on a report the user believed they had saved. Ownership is the predicate on reads and writes alike, so another doctor's report is 404 on `GET` and on `DELETE`.
- [x] **M21.4** — Scheduled reports + cron executor + run history (sent/failed) + failure notifications.
  - ✅ Verify: forced failure records a `failed` run and notifies.
  - **⚠️ PARTIAL — the schedule ships, the executor is M30's, and the verify step therefore cannot be met here.** API §12.4 says "executor is a cron job" and **M30.2 lists scheduled reports among the jobs its registry owns**. What is built is the schedule, its recipients, pause/resume, and the run-history endpoint; what is not built is anything that runs. The history stays empty until M30 writes to it — asserted as empty, which is visible rather than hidden behind a fabricated row. **A forced failure cannot be recorded by a component that does not exist**, so that half is carried to M30.2 as **RA-M21.1**.
  - **Recipients are lower-cased on write**, which is ADR-028's own instruction: `recipients` is `text[]` rather than the `citext[]` DB §I2 asked for, because Prisma 7 cannot read a citext array at all, and that ADR moved the case-insensitive comparison to "a normalisation rule ... by the endpoint that writes them". This is that endpoint. `nextRunAt` is arithmetic on the frequency, with **calendar** months so a monthly schedule on the 31st does not slide earlier every time; the time of day is the creation time's own clock, which is a derivation from the user's action rather than an invented default.
- [x] **M21.5** — Exports: CSV sync; PDF/Excel async job → file → notification (`report_exports` row per artifact).
  - ✅ Verify: async export completes within job SLA and notifies with the file id.
  - **⚠️ PARTIAL — CSV ships; the asynchronous half needs two milestones that do not exist.** The contract itself splits this route in two. CSV serialises the same `AnalyticsSnapshotView` the JSON endpoint returns — a second query path would be a second place for the doctor scope to be right and the first to drift — and is returned as a download rather than inside the `{ data }` envelope, because it is bytes rather than a resource a client reads fields off.
  - **Status 2026-10-24: the carry is still open, for a narrower reason.** M30.1's runner and M25's `S3FileStorage` binding both exist now. What stops the asynchronous half is **M25.3's scan gate**: a generated artefact is a `files` row whose `scan_result` is null until a scanner rules on it, no scan engine is bound (RA-M30.2), so the download route would refuse every export with 403 `scan_pending` — a notification pointing at a door that does not open. `reports.jobs.ts` records the same blocker for the scheduled-report executor. Whether a server-generated artefact may bypass a gate built for uploads is a security-policy decision, not an implementation detail, and it is not taken here. *(PDF and Excel would also add a rendering dependency.)*
  - **`excel` and `pdf` answer 501.** They need **M30.1**'s job runner and **M25**'s storage (`FILE_STORAGE.put` rejects with 503 until then, deliberately — ADR-019 §2: "a `put()` that resolved without writing would hand back a file id for bytes that do not exist"). `report_exports` is the row ADR-010 group 15 scoped IN for exactly that artefact, and it stays empty until there is an artefact to point at. A 404 would deny the format is in the API, a 422 would blame the client, and a fabricated job id would promise a file that never arrives. **The verify step's SLA and notification are carried to M30.1 as RA-M21.2.**

**🔒 Exit gate M21:** reports page renders live data across categories.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-18: PASSED on the server; the page is M40's, as M13–M16 already found.** All nine categories answer live data — the eight M17.2 left as 501 now compute real aggregates over the caller's own practice, and `revenue` keeps ADR-037 §3's alternate while billing is off, which is M17's own "M21 extends rather than replaces". ⚠️ **One existing assertion was inverted, deliberately and with the reason recorded in the test**: `reports-revenue-alternate.e2e-spec.ts` asserted the 501 for `category=patients`. The 501 is gone because the snapshot exists, not because the assertion was relaxed. ⚠️ The frontend still reads `reports/model/mock-data.ts` through its seam, so "renders" joins M13's, M14's, M15's and M16's at **M40**. **2081 green across 100 files** at the time of the commit, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean.

#### RA-M21.1 — the scheduled-report executor is M30.2's, and its verify step goes with it

M21.4 asks for a forced failure to record a `failed` run and notify. Both belong to the component that runs the report, and API §12.4 names it a cron job while **M30.2** lists scheduled reports among the jobs its registry owns. The schedule, the recipients, the pause/resume and the history endpoint ship; nothing runs them. **Carried to M30.2**, where the executor and the failure path arrive together.

#### RA-M21.2 — asynchronous exports need M30's runner and M25's storage

M21.5's verify step names a job SLA and a notification carrying a file id. The job runner is **M30.1** and the artefact needs `FILE_STORAGE.put`, which is **M25**. `excel` and `pdf` answer 501 in the meantime rather than returning a job id nothing will honour. **Carried to M30.1.**

**Status 2026-11-05: runner, storage and scanning all exist now** (M30.1, M25.2, M25.3 — a generated artefact would be stored, scanned and served). What remains is producing the bytes: no PDF or spreadsheet writer is in the dependency tree, and this project treats a new dependency as needing authorization (the M32.3 compression precedent). `excel` and `pdf` keep answering 501 until one is approved.
