## Milestone 22: Doctor Settings Center **[R2 — new]**

**Goal:** The per-user settings document (API §13, DB §J1–J3). Revision 1 covered account/security (M8) and profile (M9) but never the `SettingsDocument` itself — the eleven-section Settings Center is the frontend's most-used non-clinical surface and had no owning milestone. Reused verbatim by admins as "My Settings".

- [x] **M22.1** — `GET /me/settings` → the full `SettingsDocument` (account, security meta, privacy, workflow preferences, integrations, appearance/accessibility).
  - ✅ Verify: shape validated against `features/doctor/settings/model/*` + `settings/schemas.ts`.
  - **Verified — and one section is the model's shape rather than the contract's prose.** §13.2 names `privacy` as "profileVisibility, surveyParticipation…"; **neither field exists**. `PrivacySettings` in `settings/model/types.ts` is `{ dataSharing, analytics, cookies, aiDataUsage }` and `user_settings` carries exactly those four columns — the model and the database agree with each other and not with that sentence, and the source-of-truth order puts the frontend model first. The contract's list ends in an ellipsis, which reads as illustrative rather than exhaustive.
  - **One document, two tables, one version.** `user_settings` holds privacy and workflow; `user_interface_preferences` holds appearance and accessibility; the `updatedAt` the client carries is the **later** of the two. A per-table version would let a stale privacy slice look current because the *other* table had been written recently, and the 409 §13.2 asks for would never fire for the section actually behind.
  - **A user who never opened Settings gets the column defaults rather than 404**, the same choice M16.5 and M19.4 made: a settings screen opening on a 404 has nothing to render.
- [x] **M22.2** — `PATCH /me/settings/{section}` slice updates … Zod-validated per slice; optimistic concurrency → 409.
  - ✅ Verify: one test per slice incl. out-of-range `textScale` → 422; stale `updatedAt` → 409.
  - **Verified — four slices, one test each, and a fifth that is the one worth having.** Appearance and accessibility share a single row, so the suite saves both and asserts the first survived: a slice that wrote the whole row would have reset it to defaults, and no per-slice test alone would notice.
  - **`textScale` is bounded 90–130 *and* stepped by 5.** A value between stops renders at a size no control can return to — the reasoning M16.5 applied to the AI temperature's eleven-element array. 140, 85 and 103 are each 422.
  - **Versions are compared as instants, not strings**, so a client that round-trips the value through a date library is not spuriously 409'd. `updatedAt` is **required** rather than optional: a patch that could omit it would let any client opt out of the concurrency check by leaving a field off. An unknown section is **404** — `/me/settings/payroll` is a resource that does not exist, not a malformed request. Writes `upsert`, so two tabs saving at once cannot race a primary-key violation.
- [x] **M22.3** — Integrations: `POST /me/integrations/{id}/connect` · `/disconnect`; OAuth server-side, tokens encrypted at rest, **write-only** (never returned).
  - ✅ Verify: a connected integration returns status but never token material (response-shape assertion).
  - **Verified, and the guarantee is structural rather than a projection to remember.** `oauth_tokens_enc` is named by **exactly one method** in the repository — the one that writes it. No read path selects the column, so there is nothing to forget to strip. The assertion is written against a **connected row holding real bytes**, because asserting it on a row with no token to leak is the shape of test that gives false confidence; neither the bytes nor either spelling of the column name reaches the wire.
  - **Disconnect clears the column** rather than leaving it: a disconnected integration holding live credentials is a grant the user believes they revoked.
  - **⚠️ `connect` answers 501.** "OAuth server-side" needs a client id, a secret and a redirect URI per provider; **none is configured and no source names one**. Fabricating a connection would show a doctor a calendar as connected that syncs nothing. Disconnect *is* built, because revoking needs no provider. See **RA-M22.1**.
- [ ] **M22.4** — `POST /me/data-export` — own-activity export request (async job → file → notification).
  - ✅ Verify: request → job → file id delivered by notification; export contains only the caller's data.
  - **Status 2026-10-24: still 501 — the two named prerequisites exist, and the blocker is now the scan gate.** M30.1's queue registry and M25's storage binding have shipped. A generated export would still be a `files` row the M25.3 gate refuses to serve until a scan engine (RA-M30.2) rules it clean, so the notification would carry a file id the user cannot download — the same blocker M21.5's asynchronous half and `reports.jobs.ts` record. Two further gaps, both small and both decisions: there is no row to track a *personal* export (`report_exports` carries a `ReportCategory`, which an activity export is not), and "own activity data" is not enumerated — `user_activity_log` and `login_events` are the caller's own account data already served by `/me/activity` and `/me/login-history`, the natural reading, but not a recorded one.
  - **Status 2026-11-05: the scan-gate blocker is gone; the two decisions remain.** M25.3 now binds a real engine (ClamAV) and production requires one, so a generated export would be scanned and then downloadable. What still stops M22.4 is what the 2026-10-24 entry already named: no row tracks a *personal* export, and "own activity data" is not enumerated — both owner decisions, not code.
  - **🚫 BLOCKED — answers 501, and cannot be honestly stubbed.** *(Original entry.)* The contract describes "async job → file → notification": the job runner is **M30.1**'s queue registry and the file needs `FILE_STORAGE.put`, which rejects with 503 until **M25**. Returning a job id would promise a user their own data on a queue nothing drains. See **RA-M22.2**.
- [x] **M22.5** — Writer hooks: every settings write emits a `settings` activity row (M8.6) and, for security/privacy slices, an audit event.
  - ✅ Verify: changing a privacy slice produces both rows.
  - **Verified on the activity half from the feed itself, and the audit half is a dispatcher call rather than a row** — `LoggingAuditDispatcher` is still the M2 stub and ADR-019 assigns the durable writer to **M28**, so there is no table to count yet.
  - **A privacy change is filed under the `privacy` kind, not `settings`.** The feed groups by kind and a privacy change filed under "Settings" is invisible in the filter a user would look in; the test asserts the feed contains `privacy` and *not* `settings` for that write. Both kernels swallow their own failures, because each is an after-the-fact record of a mutation that already succeeded — a feed that stops recording is a real defect, just not one worth failing a clinician's save over.

**🔒 Exit gate M22:** all eleven Settings sections load and save against the API; no token material ever leaves the server.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-18: PASSED on the second condition; the first holds for every section that has a server side.** **No token material leaves the server** — structurally, as above, and asserted against a row that has a token to leak. **Loading and saving** works for privacy, workflow, appearance, accessibility, the integration list and the activity feed; account and security are M8's and profile is M9's, already shipped. Two sections cannot be completed here: **integration connect** needs an OAuth client nobody has configured (RA-M22.1) and **data export** needs M30 and M25 (RA-M22.2). ⚠️ The frontend reads `mock-settings-service.ts` through its seam, so rendering joins the rest at **M40**. **2107 green across 101 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean.

#### RA-M22.1 — integration connect needs an OAuth client, and none is configured

§13.3 says "OAuth server-side". That needs a client id, a client secret and a redirect URI **per provider** — google-calendar, outlook, zoom, teams — and no source names one, no environment carries one, and no ADR chooses a consent model. `connect` therefore answers 501 naming what is missing, while `disconnect` ships because revoking needs no provider. A fabricated connection would show a doctor a calendar as connected that syncs nothing, which is worse than an honest refusal.

#### RA-M22.2 — own-activity export needs M30's runner and M25's storage

M22.4 is an async job producing a file announced by a notification. The runner is **M30.1**, the file is **M25**, and `POST /me/data-export` answers 501 until both exist. The verify step's "export contains only the caller's data" is a property of a component that has not been written; asserting it against a stub would assert nothing.

## Milestone 23: Administration Center **[R2 — new]**

**Goal:** Clinic operations (API §18, DB §K1–K12). Revision 1 created the module skeleton and the tables but no implementation milestone — sixteen resource groups with zero owner. The frontend side is **live and writing today** (Phase B gave every collection localStorage-backed CRUD), so these endpoints have real consumers waiting.

**All routes:** admin role.

- [x] **M23.1** — `GET /administration/overview` → `ClinicOverview` (todaysPatients/Appointments, waiting, completed, cancelled, doctorUtilization %, averageWaitingMinutes, clinicOccupancy %, hourlyActivity[]).
  - ✅ Verify: aggregates match a hand-computed seeded scenario; p95 < 200 ms.
  - **Verified on the aggregates.** Eight counts go through **one** `$transaction`, so the page costs one round trip and every figure describes the same instant — run sequentially, a busy clinic could return a "completed" count from after the "total" and show more completed appointments than appointments. `hourlyActivity` carries all 24 buckets including the empty ones, because a histogram that skipped quiet hours would draw a clinic that closes at random. Percentages answer **0 rather than NaN** at a zero denominator: a clinic with no rooms is not 0/0 percent occupied, it is not occupied. `averageWaitingMinutes` is derived from check-in times against the request's own clock, so two reads a minute apart disagree — which is what a waiting time is.
  - **⚠️ The p95 is not asserted.** A latency budget measured on whichever machine CI happens to run asserts the disk rather than the design, and the shape that would make it meaningful — a warmed cache — is **M31.1**'s, exactly as RA-M19.1 records for the badge endpoint. The query count is what this milestone can control, and it is one round trip.
- [x] **M23.2** — Staff · Departments · Rooms · Resources CRUD …
  - ✅ Verify: derived fields are computed, never stored; capacity ≤ 0 → 422; deleting a department with staff → 409.
  - **All three verified, and the derived-field test is written the way that catches a regression rather than the way that passes.** The stored `resources.status` is set to a **lie** directly — `IN_STOCK` on a shelf holding zero — and the response is asserted to say `out`. A test that only checked the happy path would pass against an implementation that simply read the column. `Department.staffCount` is a relation count, never a column.
  - **No derived field appears in any request schema**, so there is no shape a client could send one in: a create carrying `status: 'in-stock'` on an empty shelf is ignored, and that is asserted rather than assumed.
  - **The department refusal is 409, not 422.** The request is well-formed and would be legal on an empty department; what refuses it is the state of the world, which is ADR-013's distinction. The `detail` names the count so an administrator knows how much reassignment is involved.
  - **The stored `status` column is kept in step on every write** as DB §14.5-6's "index use" exception, so the value stays filterable — but the *response* always carries the computed answer, which is why a drifted column cannot mislead a reader.
- [x] **M23.3** — Schedule · Holidays · Shifts …
  - ✅ Verify: `start >= end` → 422; the seven weekday rows can be updated but not created or deleted.
  - **Verified — and the second half is guaranteed by the shape of the route rather than by a check.** `PUT /administration/working-days` takes the whole week; there is no `POST` and no `DELETE`, and the suite asserts both answer 404. `clinic_working_days` is keyed by the `Weekday` enum, so seven is all the table can hold. A body naming six days, or seven with a duplicate, is refused rather than partially applied, and the write is one transaction — a schedule half-changed with no record of which half is not a state a clinic can be in.
  - **Two real API gaps surfaced here as 500s and were fixed rather than tested around.** `holidays_name_date_key` is unique on `(name, date)`: the violation is now caught and answered **409**, because a pre-check cannot see an uncommitted row and only the constraint tells the truth when two administrators save the same public holiday at once. A soft-deleted holiday still occupies that index, which the code comments and the suite both record.
- [x] **M23.4** — Queue · Waiting room state machines …
  - ✅ Verify: illegal transition (in-room→called) → 409; waiting minutes derived from timestamps, not stored. **Patient identifiers masked by default** (M7.3).
  - **All three verified.** `waiting → called → in-room` is enforced by **compare-and-set**, not read-then-write, so two receptionists calling the same patient at once produce one transition and one 409 rather than the second silently overwriting the first — driven with two concurrent requests and asserted as exactly one 200 and one 409. Every backwards or skipping move lands on the same refusal, including the `in-room → called` the verify step names.
  - **Derived minutes are asserted against a stored lie too**: a queue entry 30 minutes old with a 10-minute estimate is stored with `delayed: false`, and the response is asserted to say `true`. Waiting minutes come from `enqueued_at`/`checked_in_at` against the request's clock.
  - **The MRN is masked and the display name is not**, which is the whole of the decision: these two surfaces face a room full of people, and a queue nobody can be called from is not a queue.
- [x] **M23.5** — Announcements · Policies · Document templates …
  - ✅ Verify: editing a policy increments its version and stamps `updatedAt` server-side; an expired announcement drops out of the active list.
  - **Verified.** A policy starts at version 1 and is bumped **from the stored value** on every edit; `version` is absent from the request schema, because a client that could set it could also freeze it, and a version that never moved would be indistinguishable from a policy nobody had edited. A non-numeric stored version restarts at 1 rather than becoming `NaN` — the column is a varchar and a hand-edited value must not poison every later edit. `updated_at` is `@updatedAt`, so no client value can reach it.
  - **Expiry is a filter, not a delete**: an expired announcement leaves the active list and is still retrievable with `includeExpired=true`. **DB §K10's `announcements_expires_after_creation` CHECK** — "an announcement that expires before it is posted is never seen" — is now a **422 naming the field** rather than a 500 from the driver, with the CHECK still the backstop for any writer that does not come through this route.
  - **`usageCount` is equally unreachable from any request**, and a patch trying to set it to 500 leaves it at 0 — asserted.
- [ ] **M23.6** — `GET /administration/platform-status` → `PlatformStatus` … sourced from monitoring (M38.1) + DB.
  - ✅ Verify: with billing off, `revenueMonth` returns the M17.2 alternate; system health reads real probe data, not constants.
  - **🚫 BLOCKED — answers 501, and the verify step is the reason.** "System health reads **real probe data, not constants**" is precisely what cannot be satisfied: `PlatformStatus` carries uptime, CPU, RAM, storage and API response time, and those probes are **M38.1**'s. A route returning plausible constants would pass a smoke test and mislead the one reader who matters — an administrator deciding whether the platform is healthy. The billing half is already settled (ADR-039 keeps billing deferred, and M17.2's alternate is live), but half a payload is not this endpoint. See **RA-M23.1**.
- [x] **M23.7** — `GET /administration/audit-events` — thin recent feed (full surface is M28).
  - ✅ Verify: returns the most recent N events, same projection rules as M28.
  - **Verified — newest first, with the limit clamped to 100 so a caller cannot ask the endpoint to scan the table.** The projection carries actor, role, category, event type, severity, summary and target, and **no payload**: `audit_events.payload` can hold context an administrator may read in M28's full surface behind its own controls, and a thin recent feed is not where that gets decided. The full surface — filters, export, incident bookmarks — is **M28**.

**🔒 Exit gate M23:** every one of the sixteen administration sections in `features/admin/` reads and writes against the API; no section is served from localStorage.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-18: PASSED on the server for fifteen of the sixteen groups; the frontend half is M39.6/M40's.** Staff, departments, rooms, resources, the working week, holidays, shifts, the queue, the waiting room, announcements, policies, document templates, the clinic overview and the audit feed all read and write against the API. **`platform-status` does not**, and cannot until M38.1 (RA-M23.1). ⚠️ "No section is served from localStorage" is a *frontend* statement: Phase B gave every collection localStorage-backed CRUD, and swapping those seams is **M39.6**. The endpoints those seams need now exist, which is the half this milestone owns. **2148 green across 102 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean.

#### RA-M23.1 — platform status needs M38.1's probes, and constants would be worse than 501

M23.6's verify step demands that system health read real probe data. Uptime, CPU, RAM, storage and API response time have no source in this repository — `/health` reports service *reachability* (ADR-016) and nothing measures the host. **M38.1** owns monitoring. The route answers 501 naming the dependency, because the failure mode of the alternative is an administrator reading invented numbers and concluding the platform is fine.

## Milestone 24: Platform Settings **[R2 — new]**

**Goal:** The sixteen-panel platform configuration document (API §19, DB §L1–L4). Revision 1 referenced this milestone from M3.3 ("once M19 exists") but never wrote it — and M19 was Messaging, so the reference pointed at the wrong milestone. **This milestone unblocks M3.3's policy layering.**

**All routes:** can:`settings.manageSystem` (integrations panel: can:`integrations.manage`). Every write is audited; risky slices require `confirm: true`.

- [x] **M24.1** — `GET /platform-settings` → the full `AdminSettingsDocument` (11 form slices + users/integrations/apiKeys/backups collections).
  - ✅ Verify: shape validated against `features/admin/admin-settings.types.ts`.
  - **Verified field by field, not key by key.** The suite carries the eleven field lists transcribed from that file and asserts each slice's key set **exactly** — a missing key is a panel with an undefined input, and an extra one is a field the frontend drops on its next save, so `toEqual` on the sorted keys is the assertion that catches both. The four collections are there, and so is a `revisions` map: §19.2 requires optimistic concurrency and `AdminSettingsDocument` has **no version field**, because the mock wrote to localStorage where there is one writer by construction. It sits beside the slices rather than inside them so every slice's shape still matches the interface (ADR-040 §4).
  - **A missing slice answers 503 naming it, never a substituted default.** `PolicyService` enforces what is *stored*; a panel showing a shipped default that nothing is enforcing would be a lie about the platform's security policy.
  - **The `users` collection is read by this module's own repository**, not borrowed from the users module — M2.2 zone 1's own wording ("query it through your own repository"). The *management* endpoints stay at `/admin/users` where §19.3 puts them.
- [x] **M24.2** — `PATCH /platform-settings/{slice}` for slice ∈ general/organization/branding/localization/notifications/security/email/sms/backup/retention/privacy — Zod-validated per slice, optimistic concurrency; **security and retention slices require `confirm: true`** plus an audited consequence text.
  - ✅ Verify: one test per slice; security slice without `confirm` → 422; stale `updatedAt` → 409 with both change sets.
  - **All three verified, and the concurrency one is verified the way that would catch its own removal.** The staleness check produces the *useful* 409 — §19.2's both change sets, as JSON in `detail`, the same envelope §15.3's matrix conflict uses. It is **not** what makes the guarantee: two saves in the same instant both read the same revision and both pass the check. The revision is therefore in the `WHERE` clause of the update, so exactly one row moves, and **two simultaneous saves are driven concurrently and asserted as exactly one 200 and one 409** — the test that fails the day the write becomes a plain update.
  - **The confirmation is checked before the revision, deliberately.** Both are refusals, but only one is fixable by reloading; telling an administrator the page is stale when the real problem is an unconfirmed change sends them round a loop that never ends. The refusal is asserted to have written **nothing**.
  - **`defaultSection` is a closed enum of the twenty-two `AdminSectionSlug` values**, not a string. A free string would let an administrator set a landing page the shell cannot route to, and the failure would appear as a blank screen at their next sign-in rather than as a rejected save.
  - **An unknown slice is 404, not 422** — `/platform-settings/payroll` is a resource that is not there. The rule M22's settings controller set.
- [x] **M24.3** — API keys: `POST /platform-settings/api-keys` → **full key returned exactly once** `{ key, prefix }`, hash stored; `GET` lists prefix-only; `POST …/{id}/revoke`.
  - ✅ Verify: the plaintext key never appears in a second response or in the DB (grep the row); revoked key authenticates nothing.
  - **Both verified, and the first is a property of the schema rather than a rule the code remembers.** `api_keys` has `prefix` and `key_hash` and no column a plaintext could go in (DB §L3), so there is no second response that *could* carry it. The verify step's "grep the row" is done literally: the row is serialised whole and searched for the issued secret. SHA-256 rather than Argon2id, and ADR-040 §6 gives the reason — 256 bits of `randomBytes` has no missing entropy to buy back, and a memory-hard KDF on the verification path would make every API call a deliberate CPU burn. The comparison is constant-time.
  - **"Revoked authenticates nothing" is asserted against `ApiKeyService.verify`**, which is where the decision lives. Every rejection — unknown prefix, wrong secret, revoked key — answers identically, so the failure shape cannot be used to enumerate valid prefixes. A second revocation is **409**, not a silent 200: the ledger must not record one act twice.
  - ⚠️ **No route accepts an API key.** What a key is *allowed to do* is specified nowhere — see **RA-M24.1**.
- [x] **M24.4** — Integrations: `GET /platform-settings/integrations` · `PATCH …/{id}` (config write-only, encrypted) · `POST …/{id}/test-connection` → result trail · enable/disable (P4 consequence summary).
  - ✅ Verify: config values are never returned after write; **test-connection is SSRF-guarded** — private/link-local/metadata addresses rejected (M37.2 re-tests this).
  - **Write-only is structural.** `config_enc` is named by two repository methods — the one that writes it and the one the probe reads it with — and no projection reaching a response selects the column, so there is no shape to forget to strip. The suite asserts the secret is absent from the write response, absent from the list, and **absent from the stored bytes**, which is the assertion that would catch a plaintext column.
  - **The SSRF guard is a pure module with 38 cases of its own**, written the way an attacker writes them: the metadata service as `169.254.169.254`, as `::ffff:169.254.169.254` and as `::ffff:a9fe:a9fe`; loopback across all of `127/8`; RFC 1918 at both edges of the `172.16/12` range; NAT64; `localhost` by name without consulting a resolver, because a resolver that answered otherwise **is** the attack. Non-http schemes and embedded credentials are refused. A hostname is resolved and **every** answer is checked, not the first. The route-level tests assert the guard runs **before anything is fetched** by checking the probe was never called.
  - ⚠️ **Not a complete SSRF defence, and the code says so.** DNS rebinding — an address that passes and then resolves differently for the socket — needs the connection pinned to the checked address, which is **M37.2**'s hardening pass.
  - **The "result trail" is the audit ledger** (ADR-040 §7): DB §L2 defines no trail relation and every probe is already an audited administrative action. `lastSyncAt` is deliberately left alone — a successful probe is not a sync, and moving that timestamp would make a broken integration look healthy.
  - **Disabling a connected integration needs `confirm: true`**, §19.5's "P4 consequence summary", on M24.2's argument: the consequence is not visible on the row being toggled. Re-enabling does not — turning a service back on is not the change whose consequence is invisible.
- [ ] **M24.5** — Backups: `GET /platform-settings/backups` · `POST` (manual) · `POST …/{id}/restore` behind an out-of-band confirmation policy.
  - ✅ Verify: manual backup produces an artifact row; restore requires the documented confirmation and is audited.
  - **🟡 PARTIAL — the catalogue is live, taking and restoring a backup answer 501.** `GET` reads `backups` and is complete, `Decimal` converted at the boundary so a size never reaches a chart as `{ s, e, d }`. The other two are blocked, and the verify step's own word is why: a `backups` row is **not** a backup. It carries `storage_ref`, a pointer into object storage, and `size_mb`, the size of the artifact it points at. Producing that artifact needs somewhere to put it (**M25**, whose `FILE_STORAGE` port rejects with 503 today precisely so an id is never handed back for bytes that do not exist) and something to run a dump outside a request (**M30.2**, where the roadmap already lists "backup trigger"). An administrator reads this panel at exactly one moment — after something has gone wrong — and a row claiming a recovery point that is not there is worse than an empty list. Restore is blocked twice: §19.6's out-of-band confirmation policy is stated by **no source**, and a `confirm: true` boolean invented here would be an in-band confirmation wearing its name. See **RA-M24.2**.
- [x] **M24.6** — The `general` slice carries the maintenance-mode flag; the middleware honoring it lands in M38.3.
  - ✅ Verify: flag persists and is readable by the config service (M3.3 layering test now passes end-to-end).
  - **Verified end to end against a real PostgreSQL, and M3.3's twenty-milestone placeholder is now filled.** `PrismaPlatformPolicySource` reads the `security` slice's five fields and the `general` slice's maintenance flag; `PolicyService` layers both over the environment. The suite proves an administrator's `PATCH` reaching `PolicyService.current()`, including `false → true` on `twoFactorRequired` and `singleSessionPerUser` — the direction a `??` or a truthiness check gets wrong.
  - **Registered through a delegate token `config/` owns** (ADR-040 §2), because lint zone 6 forbids `config/` importing the kernel that holds Prisma. The rejected alternative — a second `@Global` module exporting the same token — would have made *which layer of the security policy applies* a property of `app.module.ts`'s import order.
  - **Two env defaults were added under the flag**, `MAINTENANCE_MODE` and `MAINTENANCE_MESSAGE`, on ADR-020 §4's established reasoning: M3.3 cannot layer over a default that does not exist. Documented in `.env.example`, which `env.spec.ts` parses and checks for completeness.
  - **Per-key resilience is proven against a hand-edited row**, written past the API on purpose: a stored `loginAttemptLimit: -4` costs that one setting and leaves `sessionTimeoutMinutes` standing. One nonsensical column must not take down a policy.

#### RA-M24.1 — an API key authenticates nothing, because nothing says what it may do

M24.3 issues, lists, revokes and **verifies** credentials, and `verify()` is
proven to reject a revoked key. No route accepts one, and that is deliberate.
API §19.4 describes issuance and revocation and says nothing about
authentication; the roadmap's verify step says a revoked key "authenticates
nothing" without saying what a live one authenticates. Whether a key acts as a
user or as the platform, which of the 22 capabilities it carries, whether it may
reach PHI at all, and what rate limit applies are product decisions with no
source. Wiring a credential into a PHI API on a guess is exactly the invention
the working rules forbid. **Blocked on a product decision**; the verification
half is built and tested so that the day the model is stated, the guard is the
only new code.

#### RA-M24.2 — a backup catalogue row is not a backup

M24.5's `POST` and restore are 501. Two dependencies and one unstated policy:
object storage (**M25**) has to hold the artifact `backups.storage_ref` points
at; the dump has to run outside a request (**M30.2**, "backup trigger"); and
§19.6's out-of-band confirmation policy for restore is named by no source —
second administrator, time delay, signed token and support ticket are all
plausible and they are not the same product. The catalogue read is complete, so
the panel renders; the two actions refuse and name what is missing.

#### RA-M24.3 — three slices that describe server behaviour are stored and read by nothing

*Recorded 2026-10-31 from a sweep of every slice's readers.* M24.2 validates, stores and audits all eleven slices, and the panel shows each as the platform's configuration. For the slices the *client* renders (branding, organization, localization's display formats, privacy's banner) that is the whole job. Three describe what the **server** does, and no server code reads them:

| Slice | Fields | What actually decides | Effect of saving the panel |
|---|---|---|---|
| `email` | `fromName`, `fromAddress`, `replyTo`, `smtpHost`, `smtpPort`, `signature` | `SMTP_HOST`, `SMTP_PORT`, `SMTP_FROM` (env), read by `SmtpMailer` | None |
| `sms` | `enabled`, `senderId`, `defaultCountryCode`, `deliveryReports` | No SMS transport exists (`SMS_PROVIDER` defaults to `stub`) | None |
| `notifications` | `emailEnabled`, `smsEnabled`, `inAppEnabled`, `dailyDigest`, `criticalOnly`, `quietFrom`/`quietTo` | Per-user preferences only (`channel-selection.ts`, M19.4) | None |

`security` (five fields), `general` (maintenance), `retention` (`auditLogDays`) and `localization` (`timezone`) are read; `security.sessionTimeoutMinutes` and `passwordExpiryDays` are the exceptions in the M37.2 findings log.

**Not wired, because each needs a decision:**
- **`email`.** A database-driven SMTP host lets anyone holding an administrator session send every password-reset and invitation link to a server they choose; a database-driven `fromAddress` lets them send as any address. Options: (a) keep transport in env and make the slice's transport fields read-only in the panel; (b) layer only the non-transport fields (`fromName`, `replyTo`, `signature`) over env, ADR-020 style; (c) layer everything, accepting the risk above. **Recommendation: (b)**, with (a)'s read-only display for host, port and address.
- **`notifications`.** What a platform switch means against a user's own preference is unspecified: a kill switch over every user, a default for new accounts, or a floor. `criticalOnly` and the platform quiet hours also have to agree with M19.4's rule that quiet hours never suppress critical lab alerts.
- **`sms`.** Nothing to wire until an SMS provider is chosen ([`EXTERNAL_INTEGRATIONS.md`](EXTERNAL_INTEGRATIONS.md)).

Until decided, the panel presents these as live settings that do nothing, which the frontend owner should know.

**🔒 Exit gate M24:** all sixteen panels load and save; secrets are write-only; M3.3's DB-over-env layering is proven against real platform settings.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-18: PASSED on the server, with backups partial.** Fifteen of the sixteen panels read and save against the API — the eleven form slices, users, integrations, API keys and the audit panel (M23.7's feed; the full surface is M28). **Backups render and do not yet take or restore** (RA-M24.2). **Secrets are write-only and structurally so:** no projection reaching a response selects `config_enc` or `key_hash`, and the plaintext API key has no column to live in. **M3.3's DB-over-env layering is proven against the real `platform_settings` rows**, which is the clause this gate has been waiting on since M3. ⚠️ "All sixteen panels load and save" is half a *frontend* statement — swapping `admin-settings.api.ts`'s localStorage seam is **M39.6**. **2233 green across 104 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean.

## Milestone 25: File Storage

**Goal:** The files service every module references (API §17, DB §11).

- [x] **M25.1** — Multipart upload with per-kind type/size validation + checksum.
  - ✅ Verify: wrong MIME for kind → 422; checksum stored.
  - **Both verified, and the refusal is proven to have stored nothing.** `POST /files` takes the seven kinds of API §17.1 — not the eighth, `backup_artifact`, which the `files_kind_check` constraint carries and no client may mint. The rules live in one table in `shared/files` with 17 cases of their own, including the two a hand-written check usually misses: a `Content-Type` carrying a parameter (`image/jpeg; charset=binary`, which a browser really sends) and a **zero-byte** upload, which is a failed upload and would otherwise put a file id in a clinical record pointing at nothing.
  - **The type check runs before the port**, so a rejected file is a 422 naming the field rather than a 503 from a store that was never going to be asked — and the suite asserts the object count did not move.
  - **The checksum is computed here, over the bytes that arrived.** A checksum the client asserts proves nothing about what was stored. The adapter additionally sends it as `x-amz-checksum-sha256` so the store refuses a truncated write, which makes a partial upload a failed `put()` rather than a silently corrupt object.
  - ⚠️ **The numbers are guardrails, not product rules**, and the table says so: API §17.1 requires per-kind validation and no source states the limits. `profile_photo` is the exception — `image/jpeg|png|webp` at 5 MB is **transcribed** from the route M9.2 already shipped. **SVG is absent from every image list deliberately**: it is a document that can carry script, and a branding asset is served to every user on the sign-in page.
  - **The bytes are now checked against the declared type (2026-10-25, M37.2 file-upload abuse).** The allowlist read only the client's `Content-Type`, so an SVG or HTML file declared `image/png` was stored and served as `image/png` — which made "PNG, JPEG and WebP cannot carry script" true of the header and not of the file. `shared/files/file-signature.ts` checks each format's leading signature (JPEG `FF D8 FF`, the 8-byte PNG header, `RIFF….WEBP`, `%PDF-` within Acrobat's 1024-byte window) on both upload paths, `POST /files` and `PUT /me/profile/photo`, after the type and size checks and before storage: 422 naming the field, nothing stored. `text/csv` and `text/plain` are accepted explicitly — a NUL-byte heuristic would refuse legitimate UTF-16 text and stop nothing a browser renders. A signature is not a parse; the scanner (M25.3) still decides `clean`. `file-signature.spec.ts` asserts a rule for every type any allowlist names, a real header and an impostor per type; `files.e2e-spec.ts` and `doctor-profile.e2e-spec.ts` assert the 422 at both routes. Three fixtures were corrected, not loosened: `Buffer.from('\x89PNG …')` UTF-8-encodes `\x89` as two bytes and was never a PNG; a 4-byte PNG prefix became the full 8; the EICAR file now sits behind a PDF header, which is where a real payload would be.
- [x] **M25.2** — S3 adapter + signed short-TTL download URLs with `Content-Disposition`.
  - ✅ Verify: URL expires (test with clock).
  - **✅ Verified against real MinIO (2026-11-05) — RA-M25.1 is closed.** This environment now runs Docker. `test/s3-live.e2e-spec.ts` against `pgsty/minio:RELEASE.2026-08-04T00-00-00Z` (the compose image): put, get the same bytes, presigned download served with the signed `Content-Disposition`, a 1-second URL **refused 403 once expired** by the store itself, idempotent delete — and, new, the `HeadBucket` health probe (`ping`) resolving for the bucket and rejecting for a missing one. 3/3. Commands in `EXTERNAL_INTEGRATIONS.md`. AWS S3 itself has not been run.
  - **🟡 PARTIAL — the adapter is built and contract-tested; no object store exists in this environment to round-trip against.** `S3FileStorage` uses the AWS SDK v3 against `S3_ENDPOINT` with path-style addressing, which is what makes MinIO and S3 one code path rather than a branch. Its spec sends **real SDK requests over a real socket** to a server that records what arrived, so the key layout, the bucket addressing, the content type, the integrity header and the error mapping are asserted against bytes on the wire. What is not asserted is that a real S3 accepts them — see **RA-M25.1**.
  - **The expiry assertion is exact rather than approximate**, because presigning never touches the network: the signature is local arithmetic and the window is *encoded in the URL*. The spec reads `X-Amz-Expires` and `X-Amz-Date` back and checks the signing instant against the clock that produced it, and that two TTLs do not sign identically. The half that genuinely needs a server is S3 **rejecting** an expired URL, which is the store's behaviour and not this code's.
  - **`Content-Disposition` is baked into the signature**, so whoever holds the URL cannot turn an attachment into an inline render. The filename is stripped of quotes, backslashes, CR and LF — an unescaped one is a header-injection hole in every S3-compatible store, and that case is asserted.
  - **The key never carries the filename.** `<kind>/<yyyy>/<mm>/<uuid><ext>`: filenames collide, carry PHI ("ahmed-hassan-biopsy.pdf") and arrive containing path separators. A `../../etc/passwd` upload is asserted to produce a key containing neither. The kind prefix makes a per-kind lifecycle rule expressible without reading the database; the date prefix keeps one prefix from accumulating millions of objects, which is what keeps M25.5's sweep tractable.
  - **Every failure is a 503 that does not repeat the provider's words** — the SDK's message names the bucket, the endpoint and the access key id, and none of that belongs in an API response. That is also what keeps the pre-M25 contract true here: routes that answered 503 under the stub still answer 503, now for an accurate reason.
- [x] **M25.3** — Malware-scan gate (queued; download 403 until `scan_result='clean'`).
  - ✅ Verify: pre-scan download blocked.
  - **✅ Complete (2026-11-05) — an engine is bound, and the box's own condition ("ticks when a `MalwareScannerPort` implementation is bound in a deployment") is met.**
    - **`ClamAvMalwareScanner`** (`shared/files/clamav-malware-scanner.ts`): clamd's `zINSTREAM` over `node:net` — no new dependency. `stream: OK` → `clean`, `… FOUND` → `infected`, every other reply, a timeout or a refused connection → `unavailable`, which writes nothing (fails closed). Bound by `FileStorageKernelModule` when `CLAMAV_HOST` is set (`CLAMAV_PORT` 3310, `CLAMAV_TIMEOUT_MS` 30 000); otherwise the unconfigured scanner, unchanged. **Production refuses to boot without `CLAMAV_HOST`**: no file could ever be downloaded, and the clinic would learn it one attachment at a time. Logged: the file id and the signature name, never bytes or a filename.
    - **A defect only a real engine could expose, fixed.** The sweep took "the oldest 25 pending" every run, and a row it cannot finish — object gone, or clamd refusing that file for size — stays pending (correctly). Twenty-five such rows would have pinned the head of the queue for ever and no newer upload would ever be scanned; this database already held 83 pending rows from other suites with no object behind them. `MalwareScanService` now resumes from a keyset cursor on `(created_at, id)` and wraps, so every pending row is examined within ⌈pending ÷ 25⌉ runs. New case in `malware-scan.e2e-spec.ts`, proven load-bearing (fails with the cursor disabled).
    - **Verified against real clamd** (`clamav/clamav:1.4.3`, bundled signatures 27927 — freshclam is refused by this environment's egress): `test/storage-live.e2e-spec.ts` uploads a PDF, asserts the download is **403 `scan_pending` before the scan**, runs the real `files.malware-scan` sweep, and gets `clean` and the stored bytes from MinIO; uploads EICAR and gets `infected` and a download that stays refused. `clamav-malware-scanner.spec.ts` (12) asserts the framing on the wire against an in-process clamd (chunk sizes, terminator, empty object) and every failure branch. Note that clamd does **not** flag EICAR embedded behind a PDF header — the EICAR file spec requires it at offset 0 — so the live infected case uploads raw EICAR as a `text/plain` message attachment.
  - **Status 2026-10-24: still PARTIAL, for a narrower reason.** The scanner is now scheduled: `files.malware-scan` runs every minute from the M30.2 registry. What remains is **RA-M30.2**: no scan engine is bound, so the unconfigured scanner writes no verdict and every file stays `pending`, which is the gate working. The box ticks when a `MalwareScannerPort` implementation is bound in a deployment.
  - **🟡 PARTIAL — the gate is built and verified; the scanner is M30.2's.** The roadmap puts "malware scans (M25.3)" in **M30.2**'s scheduled-job registry, so nothing sets `scan_result` yet and every uploaded file is undownloadable. That is the correct behaviour of a gate whose scanner has not run, and it is asserted as such rather than worked around.
  - **The gate turns on `scan_result = 'clean'`, and that fixed a live defect.** `GET /attachments/{id}/download` (M20.4) gated on `message_attachments.scanned_at IS NOT NULL` — which opens for a file the scanner marked **infected**, a gate that reports success on the one file it exists to stop. DB §11's own column comment states the rule ("null means not yet scanned, which is not the same as clean"). Both surfaces now read `files.scan_result`, and the infected case is asserted on both.
  - **The scope check runs before the gate**, asserted with an out-of-scope caller on an unscanned file: a `403 scan_pending` reachable by someone who may not see the file is an existence oracle for every id an attacker cares to try.
- [x] **M25.4** — Retro-wire all consumers (photos, patient documents, record/lab/message attachments, verification docs, **branding assets from M24**, exports from M21/M22) off the M9 stub onto the real adapter.
  - ✅ Verify: each consumer's round-trip test green against MinIO.
  - **✅ Verified against MinIO, with the real engine in the loop (2026-11-05).** `test/storage-live.e2e-spec.ts` boots the application with `S3FileStorage` on a MinIO bucket and `ClamAvMalwareScanner` on clamd — nothing overridden — and drives each consumer through its own routes: patient document (`POST /patients/{id}/documents` → its download), record attachment (§6.7), lab attachment (§8.11, inline upload → `GET /files/{id}/download`), message attachment (`attachmentIds` → `GET /attachments/{id}/download`, as the recipient), branding asset (read by another user), profile photo (`PUT /me/profile/photo` → the signed `photoUrl`) and verification document. Each is scanned by the real sweep and the URL its route signs is fetched from MinIO and compared **byte for byte**. 4/4 cases. **Exports from M21/M22** inject the same port and produce no file yet — their asynchronous halves are M21.5's and M22.4's, and nothing about the binding changes when they do.
  - **One consumer could not round-trip until M20.4 was corrected** — see M20.4: message attachments had no way in through the API.
  - **🟡 PARTIAL — the wiring is done and two real defects fell out of it; the MinIO round trip is not runnable here.** The retro-wiring is one line: `FileStorageKernelModule` binds `FILE_STORAGE` to `S3FileStorage`. **No consumer changed**, which is the return on ADR-019 §2's decision to define the port at M2 and stub it — profile photos, patient documents, record, lab and message attachments, verification documents, branding assets and report exports all inject the port and needed nothing.
  - **What the swap surfaced was worth the milestone on its own.** `GET /attachments/{id}/download` was passing `message_attachments.file_id` to `signedDownloadUrl` — a **database key**, which addresses no object — so every URL it would ever have produced pointed at nothing. The stub rejected before the key was used, so no test could see it. Fixed, and the spec now asserts the signed URL's path contains the file's `storage_key`.
  - **The two download surfaces also disagreed about how long a leaked link is dangerous for**: M20.4 chose 60 seconds, M25 independently wrote 300. One constant now lives in the kernel and the stricter value won.
  - ⚠️ **"Green against MinIO" is not runnable in this environment** — see **RA-M25.1**. The consumers' own suites are green against an in-memory port, which is the pattern M14.7 established and proves the platform's half of the round trip.
- [x] **M25.5** — Orphan-cleanup job.
  - ✅ Verify: soft-deleted file's object removed by the job.
  - **Verified (status reconciled 2026-10-24) — the one blocker below, "the schedule is M30.2's", closed when M30.2 shipped.** The sweep is the registered job `files.orphan-sweep` (every six hours, `files.jobs.ts`, whose `run` calls `OrphanSweepService.sweep()`). `background-jobs.e2e-spec.ts` asserts that it is in the registry's exact set, that it runs through `POST /administration/jobs/files.orphan-sweep/run` and moves its metrics, and that a manual run is audited as `job.trigger`. `files.e2e-spec.ts` § "M25.5 — the orphan sweep collects the objects" asserts what the run does: the soft-deleted file's object is removed, `storage_key` is emptied, the row survives, and a refused delete leaves the key for the next run. No code changed. Only the checkbox lagged the work.
  - **🟡 PARTIAL — the sweep is built and verified; the schedule is M30.2's.** `OrphanSweepService.sweep()` does the work and registers **no timer**: M30.2 owns the scheduled-jobs registry and already lists orphan cleanup, and M30's exit gate is "all periodic behaviour runs from the registry, none ad hoc" — a timer added here is the thing that gate fails on. The module exports the service so the registry can call it.
  - **Verified end to end**: a soft-deleted file's object is removed, and the row's `storage_key` is emptied — which is what distinguishes "deleted, object still there" from "deleted, object collected", and what makes the sweep resumable after a crash mid-batch. **The row survives**, because a clinical audit needs to know a file existed and was deleted long after the bytes are gone.
  - **The failure path is asserted, not assumed**: with the store refusing a delete, the batch continues, the row keeps its key, and the next run collects it. Marking a row swept after a failed delete would leave bytes nothing will ever collect.

#### RA-M25.1 — no object store is reachable from this environment

M25.2's and M25.4's verify steps name MinIO. There is none here and one cannot be
obtained: the Docker daemon is absent (`/var/run/docker.sock` does not exist), and
the MinIO binary download is refused by the egress policy with a 403 at the
proxy — an organisation policy denial, not a transient failure, so it was not
retried.

What that blocks is narrow and worth stating exactly: **that a real
S3-compatible store accepts these requests and rejects an expired URL.**
Everything up to the socket is verified — the requests are formed by the real
AWS SDK and asserted as they arrive at a server, and the presigned window is read
back out of the URL it is encoded in. The platform's own behaviour around the
bytes is verified end to end against an in-memory port.

Unblocking is a deployment matter, not a code one: point `S3_*` at a reachable
bucket and re-run `files.e2e-spec.ts` with the override removed. No consumer
changes, because none of them names a store.

**✅ Closed 2026-11-05.** Docker now runs here; the live spec passed against MinIO (M25.2).

**2026-10-30 — the live check is now a spec, not an edit.** `test/s3-live.e2e-spec.ts` runs the
adapter against a real store when `S3_LIVE_ENDPOINT` is set (skipped otherwise): put, get,
presigned download with the signed `Content-Disposition`, **403 once expired**, idempotent
delete. It passed against the **s3rver emulator**, which proves the spec, not MinIO or AWS. The
MinIO commands and variables are in [`EXTERNAL_INTEGRATIONS.md`](EXTERNAL_INTEGRATIONS.md).
RA-M25.1 stays open until it is run against MinIO or S3.

**🔒 Exit gate M25:** no stub storage remains; all uploads scanned + signed-URL served.
  - **✅ Gate PASSED 2026-11-05.** No stub storage remains (since 2026-09-18). **All uploads scanned**: with an engine bound, every upload is judged by the sweep, which now cannot be starved, and production refuses to run without one. **Signed-URL served**: every consumer's download route signs a URL MinIO serves (M25.4). Verified by the two live specs above (7/7 against MinIO and clamd) plus the always-on suites.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-18: PARTIAL.** ✅ **No stub storage remains** — `FILE_STORAGE` is the S3 adapter everywhere, and `UnconfiguredFileStorage` is kept only as the honest binding for a deployment with no bucket. ✅ **Signed-URL served** — both download surfaces hand out a presigned short-TTL URL with the disposition in the signature. ❌ **"All uploads scanned" is not true and cannot be**: the scanner runs on the queue and the queue is **M30.2**, so every file is `pending` and undownloadable — the gate working, not the gate missing. ⚠️ The MinIO round trip is **RA-M25.1**. **2284 green across 107 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean. No migration — DB §11 already carried `files`, its `scan_result` CHECK and the eight kinds.

## Milestone 26: Search

**Goal:** Role-scoped global search (API §16.1).

- [x] **M26.1** — Doctor scope: patients/appointments/records grouped results (trigram-backed).
  - ✅ Verify: care-scoping holds (no other doctor's patients in results).
  - **Verified in both directions, which is the only way this test catches anything.** Two doctors, one patient each, and **both patients carry the same distinctive surname** — so the query matches two rows in the database and each caller must get exactly one. A test that only asserted "I find mine" would pass against an implementation with no scoping at all.
  - **Care scope is a `WHERE` clause, not a filter applied afterwards.** Every doctor query joins `patient_doctor_assignments` and restricts to live assignments (`revoked_at IS NULL`), so another doctor's patient is never fetched and then hidden — the second shape leaks through a count, a timing, or the first refactor that drops the filter.
  - **Appointments and records are the caller's own, not their patients'.** A colleague's encounter with a patient I also treat is their work, and the page it links to would not show it — so those two queries key on `doctor_id` rather than on the assignment join.
  - **"Trigram-backed" is satisfied where the trigram is**: `patients_full_name_trgm_idx` and `patients_mrn_trgm_idx` already exist (DB §8) and serve the name and MRN match. **No migration was added**, and the reason is that the index which would "speed up" the other two queries is one the planner would not use: inside a single doctor's scope the selective predicate is the assignment join, and a GIN index over `appointments.reason` loses to it on any realistic row count.
  - **The clear MRN is carried, deliberately.** It is what `GET /patients` already gives this doctor for a patient on their own panel, and an MRN is what a clinician reads off a wristband. Masking it here while showing it one route away would protect nothing and would empty the hint of the one thing a hint is for.
- [x] **M26.2** — Admin scope: entities + administration sections/settings panels (`SearchResult` shape).
  - ✅ Verify: "backup" finds the Backup settings panel. The frontend already builds this index client-side (`features/admin/useAdminSearchIndex.ts`) — the server result shape must match it so the seam swaps cleanly.
  - **Verified literally**, and the panel index is held to the frontend by a parity spec rather than by care: it reads `admin-settings.types.ts` and `routes/index.ts` and asserts every panel and every one of the twenty-two `AdminSectionSlug` values is indexed, **and that nothing is indexed which the router does not serve** — the direction that catches a stale entry, which would be a 404 a user reaches from search.
  - ⚠️ **The parity spec reads the frontend as text, not by importing it**, and that is a dependency decision rather than a shortcut: `admin-settings.types.ts` imports `lucide-react` for the panel icons, and the backend must not acquire a rendering dependency to run a test. Two length assertions guard the failure mode that matters — if the frontend restructures those declarations the spec fails rather than passing vacuously on zero matches.
  - **The admin scope carries no patients at all**, which is not an omission: an administrator holds no care relationship (ADR-022), the frontend's own admin index carries none either, and this is what keeps the whole endpoint off the PHI surface. Asserted — a search for a patient's name as an administrator returns nothing, and the term appears nowhere in any group.
  - **`scope` is a filter, never a grant.** A doctor sending `?scope=admin` is answered in the doctor scope rather than refused — the parameter is a hint from a shell that knows which index it is rendering, and one that could widen authorization would be the whole of the bug. Asserted.
  - **A role with neither index is a 403, not an empty result.** An empty result tells a patient account the feature works and found nothing, which is a different and untrue statement.
  - **Every group is present even when it matched nothing**, so a client can tell "no patients matched" from "patients were not searched" — which it cannot do from a missing key. The per-group `limit` is per group for the same reason: the client interleaves, and it cannot if the server truncated across groups first.

#### Observed intermittency — `lab-review.e2e-spec.ts`, one test, under full-suite load

Recorded rather than dismissed, with the evidence, because "flaky" is not a
diagnosis.

**What fails.** `never returns a reviewed list shorter than the request` —
one test of twenty-six — with `Error: ECONNRESET: Connection reset by peer`.
No status code, no body, no database state involved.

**What was measured.** Six consecutive runs of the file alone: **26/26 green
every time.** Ten full-suite runs across M24–M26: this test failed in two of
them, and one earlier run failed a different suite's test once
(`doctor-administration`, also non-reproducing). It is the only test in its
file that issues two multi-request flows **concurrently** (`Promise.all` over
two `releasedOrder()` helpers); every sibling awaits them in turn.

**What the server logged: nothing.** The full-run output carries no Nest
error, no 5xx and no exception for the request that reset. A handler that
threw would be a 500 through `AllExceptionsFilter`; this never reached a
handler's reply.

**The reading.** A transport-level reset of the in-process test client under
CPU starvation — this host has **4 vCPUs** and the suite boots **111 Nest
applications**. The likely mechanism is an idle keep-alive socket closed by
the server (`keepAliveTimeout`, 5 s) exactly as the next request is written
onto it, which a browser retries for an idempotent request and supertest does
not.

**Two mechanisms measured and ruled out** at M27, because both would have been
real defects rather than flakes:

- **Connection exhaustion.** `pg_stat_activity` sampled every six seconds
  across a full run peaked at **25 of `max_connections` 100**. M24.6 added a
  database read to every policy resolution, which made this the first
  hypothesis; it is not the cause.
- **PostgreSQL closing connections.** The server log across the same runs
  carries no termination, no fatal and no timeout — only the constraint
  violations the specs deliberately provoke.

**What the investigation did fix.** A second intermittent symptom appeared at
M27 — a genuine **500** from `GET /doctors` — and the log said
`{"err": {}}`, because `Error.message` and `Error.stack` are non-enumerable
and the exception was handed to the logger raw. Every unhandled 500 in this
platform was being recorded without a cause. `AllExceptionsFilter` now
describes the exception explicitly (name, code, stack, and the message for
anything that is not a Prisma error, whose message can echo a row — MG
§11.6). Its new spec caught a leak in that very fix on the first run: a
stack **begins** with `Name: message`, so withholding the message and keeping
the stack withheld nothing. The frames are now taken without the first line.

**Not claimed as fixed.** Nothing was changed for it, because nothing
identified is wrong in the API. It is re-checked at every gate from here on,
and if it survives M29's validation it is reported as an open item rather
than rounded to green. Two neighbouring failures in the same runs **were**
real and were fixed at the root — a cleanup keyed on the wrong foreign key
(`prescriptions-medication-list`) and an `EXPLAIN` assertion that named one
of two equally-applicable indexes (`schema-platform`); both are described
where they live.

**Diagnosed and fixed — RA-M10.2: concurrent supertest requests raced for
the harness's listener.** The keep-alive reading above was wrong. `app.init()`
wires the application but binds no port, so `getHttpServer()` handed back an
**unlistening** server — and supertest, given one of those, calls `listen(0)`
itself and `close()`s it again when that one request ends. Two requests in
flight together shared a listener that the first to finish took away, and a
connection still in the accept queue when it went was reset by the kernel.
That is why it never reached a handler, never logged, and only hit the one
test issuing requests concurrently. M30 serialised that test's seeding, which
removed the trigger but not the race; `auth-register.e2e-spec.ts` still fires
two requests at once.

Reproduced in isolation rather than argued from the source: 40 batches of one
fast request constructed first (so it owns the listener) and 24 slow ones,
against a plain `http.Server` with this repository's supertest, gave **599
resets in 1000 requests** unbound and **0 in 1000** bound once first.
`createAuthHarness` now binds once, so supertest never starts or stops
anything. Specs that build their own Nest application outside the harness are
not covered by this, and none of them issues concurrent requests today — a
property of the current tests, not a guarantee.

**🔒 Exit gate M26:** ⌘K search live in both shells.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-19: PASSED on the server; the shells are M39.6/M40's.** `GET /search?q=&scope=` answers both scopes, grouped, in the `SearchResult{id,label,hint,section,path}` shape `layouts/search-context.ts` types — `path` is always present, because a result a user cannot navigate to is a row that does nothing when clicked. ⚠️ "Live in both shells" is a *frontend* statement: `useDoctorSearchIndex` and `useAdminSearchIndex` still build their indexes client-side, and swapping those seams is **M39.6**. The endpoint they will swap onto exists and matches their shape, which is the half this milestone owns. **2305 green across 109 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean. **No migration** — see M26.1 on why the trigram index this would seem to need is one the query would not use.

---

## Milestone 27: Dashboard & Shell Composites **[R2 — new]**

**Goal:** The doctor Overview composite and the shell's cheap polling endpoints (API §16.2–16.3, §20.3). Revision 1 built the action queue (M15.4) and badge counts (M19.3) but never the dashboard endpoint that carries the other eight widgets — the first screen every doctor sees had no owning milestone.

- [x] **M27.1** — `GET /dashboard/doctor` composite: KPIs (todayCount + nextAt, pendingRequests, totalPatients, completedWeek + delta), todaySchedule[], actionQueue[] (from M15.4), trends (14-day series), satisfaction, recentActivity[], quickActions meta.
  - ✅ Verify: shape matches `features/doctor/dashboard/model/mock-data.ts`; p95 < 200 ms on seeded data; **KPI values are final, never eventually-consistent** (master guide §12 — the UI does not animate counts).
  - **The shape matches the mock's *data*, and deliberately not its props.** `mock-data.ts` carries `icon: LucideIcon`, `tone: StatisticTone`, `href` and pre-formatted strings like `time: '20 min ago'`. An icon is a React component and a tone is a design token; neither belongs on the wire. And a relative time computed on the server **freezes** at whatever it said when the response was built, while the widget re-renders on a timer — so every entry carries its instant and the client formats it, which is the rule M15.4's action queue set for `waitingSince` and the reason it refused to send "3 days ago". Asserted: every timestamp in the response parses as a date.
  - **"Final, never eventually-consistent" is enforced by there being nothing to be stale.** No counter is stored or incrementally maintained; every figure is a `COUNT` against an indexed predicate, and the five KPI counts run in **one transaction** so they describe one instant — run separately a busy clinic could return `completedWeek` from after `todayCount` and show more appointments completed than booked. **M31.1** will have to keep this property when it caches them.
  - **`nextAt` is the next appointment still ahead of the request's clock**, not the first of the day, and `isNext` on the schedule is computed the same way — a stored flag would be wrong from the moment it was written, and "next at 09:00" about an appointment that already happened is what makes a dashboard untrusted.
  - **All fourteen trend days are returned, including empty ones**, and all five satisfaction buckets, for M23.1's reason: a series that skipped quiet days draws a clinic that closes at random, and a histogram missing "1 star" reads as a scale that starts at 2. `average` is 0 rather than `NaN` at a zero denominator.
  - **`recentActivity` comes from clinical rows, not from `user_activity_log`.** That table's seven kinds are *account* events — profile, password, login, settings, security, integration, privacy — and this widget shows a note signed, a prescription issued, a result released. Nor `audit_events`: that is the administrator's ledger and **M28** owns its writer. The feed merges three sources newest-first and then cuts, so it is the most recent work across all three rather than the most recent of each — asserted.
  - **An account with the role and no `doctors` row gets an empty Overview that says so**, rather than one indistinguishable from a failed query.
  - ⚠️ **The p95 is not asserted**, for the reason RA-M19.1 already records for the badge endpoint and M23.1 for the clinic overview: a latency budget measured on whichever machine CI runs asserts the disk rather than the design, and the shape that would make it meaningful is **M31.1**'s warmed cache. What this milestone controls is the query count, and the KPI block is one round trip.
- [x] **M27.2** — Per-widget endpoints (`/dashboard/doctor/schedule`, `/action-queue`, …) so the shell can load widgets independently with per-widget errors.
  - ✅ Verify: each widget endpoint returns the same slice the composite carries; one widget failing does not fail the others.
  - **Verified by comparing the two answers field for field**, not by inspection: the spec fetches the composite and all five widget routes and asserts each widget's body `toEqual` the composite's own slice. They cannot disagree because the same method computes both — and this is the assertion that fails the day a widget route grows its own copy of the logic.
  - **The independence is the routes', not the composite's**, and the distinction is deliberate. §16.2 asks for both shapes because UX §2.1 loads widgets separately so one slow widget cannot blank the first screen. The composite itself stays **all-or-nothing**: a caller that asked for one document and got eight fields of nine cannot tell which is missing, and a half-rendered Overview is worse than an error.
- [x] **M27.3** — `GET /dashboard/doctor/critical-alerts` (row-0 banner: unreviewed abnormal labs, allergy conflicts on today's patients, urgent reschedules) — **failure must be visible: fail loud, never an empty success**.
  - ✅ Verify: with the source query forced to fail the endpoint returns 5xx, never 200 with `[]` (this is a safety surface).
  - **Verified by forcing it.** `CriticalAlertsRepository` is its own provider *so that a spec can substitute one that throws* — the spec boots a second application with it replaced, and asserts the route answers ≥ 500 and that the body carries no `alerts` key at all. Nothing between the query and the exception filter catches anything: no try/catch in the repository, none in the service, and `Promise.all` rather than `allSettled` — which would have returned the two sources that worked and said nothing about the third.
  - **The reasoning, stated once because it is the opposite of every other read surface here:** an empty banner is indistinguishable from a safe one. A doctor who sees no alert concludes there is none. The composite failing costs a page; this failing silently costs a patient.
  - **"Allergy conflicts" reads `patient_clinical_alerts`, and does not re-derive anything.** DB §B12 calls that table "fixed, non-dismissible banner content" — the row a conflict is recorded on. The safety screen runs where prescribing happens (M13.3), in a module this one may not import, and re-implementing a patient-safety rule to put it in a banner would be two implementations of one rule.
- [x] **M27.4** — Summary/count endpoints across modules used by cards and chips: `/appointments/summary`, `/medical-records/summary`, `/prescriptions/summary`, `/lab-orders/summary` (incl. `criticalUnreviewed`), `/notifications/summary`.
  - ✅ Verify: each is O(1)-cheap (index-backed count), p95 < 50 ms.
  - **Four already existed** (M11, M12, M14, M19); **`/prescriptions/summary` was the gap** and now exists. All five are asserted to answer 200 for a real doctor, and the lab summary's `criticalUnreviewed` is asserted against a seeded critical order.
  - **The six prescription fields are the frontend's `summarizePrescriptions` verbatim** (`pharmacy/model/selectors.ts`) — including `newToday` meaning *created* today rather than signed today, because that is what the function being replaced counts. Changing a definition while keeping its name would silently alter a card an existing screen already shows. The six counts run in one transaction, for the KPI block's reason.
  - ⚠️ **The p95 is not asserted** — same reasoning as M27.1. What is true and checkable is that every count is against an indexed predicate and none scans a table.
- [x] **M27.5** — `GET /meta/enums` (API §20.3): the server's enum vocabulary, for contract tests.
  - ✅ Verify: a contract test diffs `/meta/enums` against the frontend unions and fails on drift.
  - **Read out of `pg_enum`, not transcribed.** Every other vocabulary in this codebase is a transcription held to its source by a parity spec, because the source is in another tree. This one publishes the *database's* own vocabulary, so it reads it from the database — and cannot drift from the schema, because it is not a copy of the schema. The labels are the `@map` values, which are exactly the wire spellings (ADR-023 §2), in `enumsortorder` rather than alphabetical: a status machine reads top to bottom, and a sorted diff would report a false difference the first time somebody inserted a value in the middle.
  - **The contract test diffs twice, because drift against either source is a different bug.** Against `01-enums.prisma`: every declared enum served with exactly its mapped values in order, **and nothing served that is not declared** — which catches the case no frontend diff can see, a running database and a committed schema disagreeing about a vocabulary. Against the frontend: for appointment status, prescription status and appointment priority.
  - **The frontend relationship is a superset, not equality, and the test says which extras are allowed.** API Appendix B records that the server carries `awaiting-patient` (ADR-001) and `draft`/`dispensed` (ADR-002) which the frontend unions do not have yet, so the assertion is that the extras are **exactly** those — a third extra value is unplanned drift and fails. `appointment_priority`, which no ADR widened, is asserted equal.
  - ⚠️ **Status 2026-11-01: the frontend-side fix is on `main` and `frontend`, not on this branch, and is held back on purpose.** `main` and `frontend` carry `e226c9da` ("the enum drift spec meets the frontend that caught up"). On those branches the frontend unions now include `awaiting-patient` and `draft`/`dispensed`, `PrescriptionStatus` has moved to `frontend/src/features/shared-domain/prescriptions/types.ts`, and the spec asserts **set equality**. The spec reads `../../frontend/src` from the **checked-out** tree. On `backend` that tree is older: the union file still lives at `features/doctor/pharmacy/model/types.ts`, and the unions are still 7 and 5 values long. So the superset assertion above is correct here, and porting `e226c9da` by itself would fail both cases: the prescription case cannot find its file, and the appointment case does not find `awaiting-patient`. **Do not port it on its own.** It arrives through branch synchronisation (CLAUDE.md, *State of the integration branches*). Measured at `origin/main` = `a493117`: `backend` is 841 behind and **0 ahead**, and under `backend/` the two differ only by `e226c9da`'s two files (this spec and Appendix B item 2). Catching `backend` up is therefore a fast-forward to `main`, with no merge commit and nothing to resolve. That stays true only while `backend` has no commits of its own: once any task branch merges into it, catching up becomes a merge, so measure again first. Pushing to an integration branch needs the owner's explicit authorisation, so it has not been done. Until then, this spec passes on `backend` as written.
  - **It is a separate controller from `/health`**, and that is not tidiness: `HealthController` carries `@Public()` because a probe that needs a token cannot report that authentication is down, and a route inheriting it by sharing the class would publish the platform's whole status vocabulary to anyone who asked. §20.3 is `✓`; this is `@Authenticated()`.

**🔒 Exit gate M27:** doctor Overview renders entirely from the API; the polling budget (10–15 s × N clients) is met by M32's load test.
  - **Gate status 2026-09-19: PASSED on the server; the polling budget is M32's, as the gate itself says.** Every widget of UX §2.1 is served — KPIs, today's schedule, the action queue, the 14-day trends, satisfaction, recent activity, quick actions and the Row-0 critical banner — as one composite and as six independent routes, plus `/meta/enums` and the fifth summary endpoint. ⚠️ "Renders entirely from the API" is a *frontend* statement: `dashboard/model/mock-data.ts` is still what the page reads, and swapping that seam is **M40**. ⚠️ The two p95 budgets are deliberately unasserted (RA-M19.1's reasoning; **M31.1** owns the cache that would make them meaningful). **2328 green across 111 files**, with typecheck, lint, build and `db:check` clean. **No migration** — `satisfaction_surveys` (DB §S7), `patient_clinical_alerts` (DB §B12) and `record_notes.signed_at` were all already there.
